# The Butcher’s Syllabus

Imogen Sallow


# Chapter 1

*Lesson One. Subject: The Gatekeeper. Function: to convert the complaint into the file, the file into the folder, the folder into the cabinet, the cabinet into the room no one enters. You have performed this function with distinction. Your failing mark is therefore not a function of incompetence. You confused process for protection. You will be evaluated accordingly.*

"Liver," I said, and lifted it out, and for a moment I didn't put it on the scale. I held it. You are allowed to hold it. Nobody writes that in the manual, but nobody writes in the manual that you can't, and I have always read the manual the way a lawyer reads a contract, for what it forgot to forbid. It lay across both my palms and over the heels of my hands, smooth and cool and slippery as a wet stone from a river, with the capsule drawn taut over it like the skin on a drum, and it had that particular heft that nothing else in the body has, a dense, settled, obliging weight, the weight of a thing that has been doing a great deal of work quietly for a long time and would like, now, to be thanked. Then I put it on the scale, and the scale thought about it, and said eighteen hundred and forty grams.

"Heavy," Mara said.

"Fatty. Look at the edge."

She looked. Mara Lin watches me work the way people watch a chef through the pass, though in fairness to chefs, they rarely have to weigh the customer first. When I turned the liver on the board and cut it, one long stroke, the surface that came up was the colour of putty and old butter, and greasy under the blade, and the smell of it rose to meet the rest of the room.

Smell is first. It always is with me. The suite under the extraction fans smelled of cold iron and chlorhexidine and a faint sweetness I could pick out of a crowd, and under that, the low faecal note that a decedent's colon contributes to every conversation and which I stopped minding in my first year and have never once stopped naming. I like the smell of the suite. It is the smell of things that are exactly what they are.

She had come in early that morning, the woman on my table, from a bus shelter, where she had been sitting upright with her bag on her knees and her eyes open, and had been sitting that way, the driver reckoned, for most of a morning before anyone thought to ask. No identification. A woman in her sixties, small, with good teeth for her age and bad feet. Around her left wrist, a band of skin paler than the rest, a centimetre wide, where a hospital bracelet had been and had been cut off long enough ago for the skin to start forgetting it, and not long enough for it to finish. I read her from the outside before I read her from the inside. That is the order.

"And the heart was what?" Mara said.

"Five-twenty."

"That's big."

"Hypertensive. Left ventricle's two centimetres. She was pushing against a closed door for years."

"Would she have known?"

"Headaches. Maybe."

That is about how I talk. On the page I am a different animal; make allowances, the way you would for a woman who is quiet at dinner and then writes you a twelve-page letter. Inside I was thinking about the heart, which had been sitting on the board to my left for twenty minutes, thick-walled and stubborn, a fist that had been clenched so long it had forgotten how to open, and about how muscle answers resistance by growing, the way a hand gets a callus, until the very thing it built to cope becomes the thing that kills it. Five hundred and twenty grams. I had held that too. I hadn't mentioned it to Mara.

Through the open door of the office, past the coat stand, the break-room television was on with the sound down. A map in two colours. A bar chart, his bar the longer one. A man in a navy suit, smiling at the camera with his whole face. MARCHETTI, it said, and underneath, in the ticker, the anchor's line, which I could read without hearing: *his to lose.*

"Debate's tonight," Mara said, because she had followed my eyes. "Are you going to watch?"

"Might."

I said it the way you'd say you might take an umbrella.

The brain came last. It's the dessert. It had come out heavy and soft and pale as a peeled egg, and I took it to the slicing board and steadied it with the flat of my left hand and made a single stroke through it with the long knife, front to back, one pull, no sawing. It opened like a book on a stand. I want to tell you how small that satisfaction is, so that you don't make it bigger than it is. It is the satisfaction of a door that closes without being slammed. It is the click a good pen makes. I did not call it joy. I called it a good day's work, early.

There was a small bleed in the left basal ganglia, the size of a raisin, the colour of oxblood against the cream. "There," I said. And there she was: blood pressure, untreated, a long time, and one afternoon in a bus shelter the door finally gave. I showed Mara. Mara wrote it down.

We closed her up together. I labelled the specimen pots and taped the labels with surgical tape, three strips each, pressing them down with my thumbnail because I like the sound the tape makes when it has properly decided to stay. My hands were steady. They are always steady. They have not shaken since my second year of residency, and I mention it now so you'll know what it means later if they do.

Mara stripped her gloves off inside out, the way I'd taught her, and dropped them in the bin, and said, without looking up, quietly, as if she were telling me the time: "You work like you're taking apart something you built."

I didn't say anything. I let it go past me, the way you let a car go past before you cross.

I liked hearing it.

* * *

The park at noon was hot enough to lean on, and the air had the consistency of soup. I had spent the morning with my hands in things that come out of the fridge at four degrees, by choice, and the heat went for the back of my neck before I had reached the gate.

Hannah had the blanket out under a plane tree, the big one by the sprinklers, with the cool bag and the girls. The girls were wet. They had been in the sprinklers in their clothes, and June was still in them, running at the jets and away from them shrieking, as if each one were a personal betrayal, and Astrid, who is eight and has a sense of her own dignity, was sitting at the edge of the blanket in a towel watching her sister with the patience of a lifeguard.

"Ris." Hannah got up and hugged me, and held on, and then held me off and looked at me with her nose wrinkled. "You smell like a hospital."

"I was at work."

"You're always at work."

I didn't correct her about the hospital. The suite was still on my skin under the soap; it always is, and I have never particularly tried to get it off. It is easier to let her call it a hospital. People like the word *hospital*. It has hope in it.

She had made sandwiches. Roast chicken with tarragon mayonnaise on soft white bread, cut into triangles for everyone, because Hannah believes, rightly, that nobody has ever been sad holding a triangle. She unpacked them in their greaseproof paper onto the blanket, and I took one and ate it, and then I took another and ate that.

They were very good. I want to be exact. The chicken was from Sunday, so it had been in the fridge two days and had firmed up and gone sweeter, the way roast chicken does, and the tarragon had got into the mayonnaise overnight and turned it faintly green and anise-sharp, and the bread had gone soft where it touched the ice pack, and the whole thing was salt and fat and liquorice and the specific comfort of white bread, which is the comfort of being a child, which nobody should be allowed to buy as an adult and everybody should. I ate the second one faster than the first.

Hannah ate half of one. She ate the way mothers eat, half a sandwich and the edges of other people's, and when June came back dripping and wanted a triangle and then didn't want the crusts, Hannah ate the crusts without seeming to notice she was doing it, as if she were tidying.

I noticed the ratio. Two sandwiches to her half. I put it down to having skipped breakfast, which I had, and which was true, and which is the kind of true that is very convenient.

June had a sticker on her T-shirt, round and navy and gold, MARCHETTI in block capitals across it, gone wavy at the edges from the sprinkler. "They were giving them out at the library," Hannah said. "She wanted one. I quite like him, actually. The veterans thing seems real. His brother."

"Mm," I said.

June sat down in my lap without asking, the way small children do, as if you were furniture that had been placed there for them, and the sticker had begun to lift at one edge, so I peeled it back, carefully, a centimetre, and smoothed it down again with my thumb, and pressed until it had properly decided to stay. Nobody looked at my thumb. Why would they.

Hannah started on me then, gently, from the side, the way you approach a horse. Was I sleeping. Had I taken any holiday this year, because she happened to know I hadn't taken any last year. Was it good for me, really, being down there all the time with nobody but the, she lowered her voice for the children, *you know*. I answered everything. That is the trick. You never refuse a question; refusing is information. You answer every question warmly, a little too fully, with a funny story at the end, and the funny story is the door you show them out through. I told her the extraction fans are so loud that the pathologist before me used to sing under them, and nobody knew for years. I told her I'd take a week in October. I won't. She laughed. I was very good. I want you to see how good, because you will need to know later what I am like when I'm being good.

Astrid had been listening, pretending to look at the sprinklers. "What do you actually do," she said. "All day."

"I find out why people stopped," I said.

Astrid considered this. She nodded, as if it were a reasonable job. Hannah gave me a look over her head, which I received and filed.

There was a third sandwich in the cool bag. I could see it through the gap in the lid, a triangle and its other half, still in the paper. I wanted it. Not very much, and not in any way I'd have mentioned, but specifically, in the stomach, the way you want the next thing when the first two were good. I didn't take it. I'm telling you because it was a fact about lunch, and I tell you the facts about lunch.

"So," Hannah said, and leaned back on her elbows, and squinted at me through the leaves, and I knew from the *so* exactly what was coming, the way you know from the first bar. "Are you seeing anyone?"

"Sort of," I said. "Early days. He works weekends."

That's all a lie needs. Three facts, one of which forecloses the next question, and none of which gives her anything to look up. No name, because a name is a thing people repeat to their husbands. A smile on *early days*, slightly shy, slightly pleased, so she'd know to leave it alone out of tact, and she did, and she squeezed my ankle, and said *good, Ris, good*, and *call me on Sunday*, and meant both, which was the only part I minded.

I'd give it a B-plus. It lost marks for compassion. Compassion is the only thing that ever gives a good lie away; you can always hear it, a little give in the voice, the liar sparing you something. A better lie would have been less kind. I am telling you this so that you'll know I can tell the difference.

And I wasn't, strictly, lying about the seeing. Strictly, I was seeing three people. I had photographs.

* * *

I watched the debate in the dark, on the tablet, propped against the fruit bowl on the kitchen counter, with the blue of the screen the only light in the flat. I had not eaten. I was grading them on structure, which is the only fair way to grade a debate, since content is a matter of opinion and structure is a matter of anatomy. Televised debates are autopsies performed by people who have never been told where anything is.

He was better. I'll say that plainly, because I am fair. He was better on syntax. The other man used sentences the way you use a ladder you don't trust, testing each rung before he put his weight on it, and you could hear him doing it. Holt Marchetti didn't test anything. His sentences went up and came down and landed where he'd meant them to land, every time, with a little room to spare, the way a good surgeon's hands are never quite touching the thing they are about to touch. I gave him a distinction for syntax.

They asked him about veterans. He talked about Mark. He didn't do it the way they usually do it, with a pause and a hand on the lectern; he did it quickly and almost apologetically, as if he knew you'd heard it before and was sorry to make you hear it again, but it was the only true thing he knew about the subject and he wasn't going to pretend to know another one. His brother. 2003. It was sincere. It worked. I watched it work on the audience, a long soft exhalation through the hall, and I gave him full marks for it, which is the worst grade I know how to give.

And then, at the end of the answer, while the moderator was turning to the other man, he did it.

He reached across with his right hand and took his left cufflink between his thumb and forefinger and gave it a quarter-turn. A small, neat adjustment, as if it had come loose, which it hadn't. His eyes went down to it. Two seconds. Then they came up again, and he was listening to the other man with his whole face, and it was over.

It looked smaller on a tablet.

The cold began in my forearms. I want to report it accurately, because it was not a figure of speech; it was a finding. It began in the skin of the forearms, on the volar surface, as if I had put them into a sink of water left standing overnight, and it moved down into the backs of my hands, which were on my knees, and settled there, and then it came up the sides of my neck and into the hinge of my jaw, which went tight and stayed tight. It was not the suite's cold. The suite's cold is a cold I choose. I put my coat on the hook and walk into it every morning the way you walk into the sea. This was the other kind. This was the kind that comes to you.

I did not move for an hour.

I know it was an hour because the tablet told me. The debate ended. A panel came on and talked about the debate, and a woman said *his to lose*, and the panel ended, and something else began. I have sat still for longer, but on those occasions the other party was dead, and it was their stillness we were documenting. This was mine. I documented it. Body upright on a kitchen stool. Hands on knees, palms down, fingers slightly flexed. No voluntary movement observed over a period of approximately sixty minutes. Skin of forearms and dorsum of hands cool to the touch. Jaw held. Eyes open.

Then I got up.

The writing desk is the only thing in the flat I paid too much for. It's walnut, and old, and the drawer under it locks with a small brass key I keep on the ring with my car key, so that every time I drive anywhere I am carrying it, which I have always thought was the point. I unlocked it. It slid out on its runners with the sound a good drawer makes, a long wooden sigh.

The three dossiers were on top. Pell. Brandt. Marchetti. Manila, with the string and the button, because I don't trust anything I can't hold in my hands and set fire to. Under them, the photographs. Under the photographs, my own signed copy of the agreement, three years old, my signature on the last page in the blue ink they gave me, rounder than my signature is now. And under that, in a clear sleeve, three printed pages, two years old, with a title page I had typed and then, a week later, felt embarrassed by, and then, a week after that, stopped feeling embarrassed by.

THE BUTCHER'S SYLLABUS.

The paper had gone soft at the corners. That happens to paper that is handled and not used. I took out the first page and stood in the blue light and read Lesson One aloud, all of it, to the flat, which had nobody else in it, and I heard what my voice did to it.

It didn't sound like a woman reading a document. It sounded like a judgement. It sounded like me. *You will be evaluated accordingly.* I had written that two years ago at this desk, at about this time of night, and I had never once heard it said out loud, and it turned out that I sounded, saying it, exactly like what I was, which was someone who had been marking things for years and had finally been handed the right paper.

The kit was in the back compartment, behind a panel that comes away if you press it in the right place. I took it out and laid it on the desk in a row, the way I lay out instruments, left to right in the order of use. The marker first. I uncapped it to check it, and the solvent smell came up, sharp and sweet and chemical, the smell of a classroom whiteboard on the first day of term, and I capped it again. Then the tape, black vinyl, a full roll, with that dull sheen it has, like the back of a beetle. Then the zip-ties, a bundle of them, and I drew one through its own head to test the ratchet, and it went with that small rapid sound they make, a sound like a watch being wound very fast, and held. The red marker, the black tape, the zip-ties. I looked at them for a while.

I was hungry.

Not in any complicated way. Plainly, in the stomach, all at once, the way you are hungry when you come in out of the cold and smell something cooking in someone else's house. I noticed it the way I'd note it on a chart: no food since the sandwiches, nine hours, blood glucose presumably falling, an entirely ordinary finding. It was physiology. I want you to have that the way I had it.

I didn't eat. I put the three things in the pocket of my coat, and put the coat on, and turned off nothing.

I left the tablet on. When I closed the door behind me he was still talking, in the dark, to a room with nobody in it, and I had decided to stop being one.


# Chapter 2

I came in through the hotel's front doors a little before seven on the Thursday, with a badge lying flat on my sternum that said CLARE WHITLOW / PRESS, and nobody looked up, because I walked in the way you walk into a building you are already late for.

Clare Whitlow is a real freelance writer who files rarely and was, as far as I could establish, out of the country. I had borrowed her name the way you borrow sugar from a neighbour who's away: without asking, and meaning to give it back unopened. On Tuesday night, in the end, I had only walked until the cold that had come into my forearms in front of the tablet had settled into something I could carry. The credential took what was left of that night and all of Wednesday's.

The fundraiser was a donor reception for the host committee in the mezzanine ballroom. The Senator was on the road, being his to lose in other cities; the dossier in my drawer put Diane Pell on the road with him. The War Room was on the thirty-first floor of the same hotel, because campaigns, like the rest of us, prefer to live above the shop. I was there to look at the building. Recon is work, and work was the one thing I had never had to explain to anybody.

At the press table a volunteer with a shoebox of lanyards looked at the one I was already wearing, at its colour and not its name, and pointed me with her pen towards the pen. There was no list. I want that down the way I would dictate a finding, because it will matter later: no list, no check, nobody on the door but a shoebox. Nobody checks a lanyard. A lanyard is a promise that somebody else has already checked.

The ballroom's air-conditioning had been set for men in wool, and it lay along my forearms like a hand. It was the morgue's cold, the sort you choose. I gave the press pen four minutes, which is about the attention span of a press pen, and left it by the service door at the back with my phone held face-up in front of me, as if it had my instructions on it.

The corridor behind a ballroom is the corridor behind a ward. It smelled of chafing-dish fuel and of beef jus reducing somewhere, and of floor cleaner with a pine note, suggesting a forest that has recently been mopped. Steel doors, a fluorescent tube flickering at the rate of a slow pulse, linen carts breathing their warm-laundry breath like cattle in a shed, and everyone with a lanyard presumed to belong. I have walked fifteen years of them at the pace of a woman slightly late, the most invisible pace there is.

I rode the service lift once to the thirty-first. There was a camera dome over its doors, as there is over every service lift, and I gave it the crown of my head and the badge. Hotels keep about a month. Nobody asks for a month of a donor reception. Upstairs, the executive corridor's door had a flap of water-case cardboard folded into its strike, so that it swung for anyone and the card reader blinked at no one, and beyond it a young man in shirtsleeves was propping a suite door open on its swing bar with a case of water. He didn't look back. I noted which suite, and rode down again.

A server came off the floor with a tray of lamb cutlets, frenched, the bones as white as piano keys, barely touched. Donors don't eat at fundraisers. Eating is what you do when you haven't already paid for the room. I took four in a napkin without breaking stride, and he let me, because I had a lanyard. I ate them in the service stair with my back to the wall.

I want to be exact. The fat at the edge had crisped under the grill and gone soft again under the heat lamps, so that it gave between the teeth like warm wax and then ran, and the eye was still pink and tasted of the animal, of grass and something close to sweat, which is what you eat lamb for. Char at the bone, bitter and good. The salsa verde had skinned over and gone warm, but there was caper in it still, and anchovy, and parsley chopped by somebody tired. I gnawed each bone clean, getting the last of it out of the angle where the shaft meets the eye with my incisors. Four. I had skipped dinner. It was physiology, and I filed it there. My hands, when I wiped them, were steady. By the top of the stair one of Clare Whitlow's shoes had opened a conversation with my heel.

From the mouth of the corridor I read the staff from the outside, the way I read anything that comes in at work. The deputy finance director had the yellowed sclera and spider naevi of a man whose liver was filing a complaint I could hear from the doorway, and like every complaint in that building, it was being managed. The advance man had bitten his nails past the quick and had a smoker's cough he didn't smoke for. A woman from comms stood with one shoulder higher than the other, a scoliotic tilt nobody had ever told her about, which also seemed a fair description of her job.

I ranked them, out of professional curiosity, the only kind you're allowed in public. The comms woman third; you can see most of a spine from outside. The advance man second, for the lungs. The finance man first by a distance, for the liver, which would have come out the colour of a church candle. Somebody ought to tell him. I would have been glad to.

It was not the ranking I had come to do.

A staffer came down the corridor fast, twenty-something, earpiece, a clipboard held flat to his chest like the shield on a knight's tomb. He looked at the badge and not at me, the right order for him and the wrong one for his employer, and waved me through to the back of the ballroom.

"Go on, Diane's coming down, she'll want the pen tidy."

Her name sat in his mouth worn smooth. I heard it first as a fact about the schedule, and the fact was wrong: Diane Pell was meant to be on the road. Then I heard it as what it was. She was here tonight, unscheduled, and so was I.

I had come to examine the premises. The patient, it turned out, was in.

* * *

I heard the change before I saw her. A ballroom at donor pitch has a roar to it, broad and steady, like surf heard from a car park. Under it a string trio sawed through Vivaldi for nobody; over it, ice in glasses. At twenty past eight the roar dipped on the main stair, the way a ward goes quiet when the consultant comes on, and Diane Pell came down into it.

I had not seen her in three years. Fifty-two. A slate suit, cut well and worn plainly. Low heels, about three centimetres, which I subtracted without being asked. Reading glasses pushed up into an ash-blonde bob. She stood straight from the hips, the way women stand who were once made to, with a swimmer's shoulders gone a little soft under the jacket. I took her height off the donor beside her and her weight off the way the suit sat across her back: about a hundred and sixty-five centimetres out of the heels, about seventy kilos. It was how I would have taken her on the table. I didn't notice, at the time, that I had done it.

She worked the room in loops of ninety seconds. She remembered which man had given the maximum and which had only said he would, and let the second see that she remembered. She touched no one for longer than a handshake. She handled money the way I handle organs: by weight, with both hands, and with no illusions about where it had been.

Then the screen at the end of the ballroom woke and Holt Marchetti was on it for four minutes, his voice a faint hiss half a second behind his mouth, as though he had been dubbed into his own life. I didn't watch him. I had seen the show. I watched Diane watch him from beside a pillar, and partway through her lips shaped one of his words a beat before he said it. *Together.* She had laid the road.

Earlier I had seen a young staffer at the rope line talking low to a man with a recorder and nodding at that pillar. When her loop brought her to it, the man was waiting with a question about staff turnover in the Senator's office. Diane paused once before she answered: a small stop, a held note at the glottis and then a release, so that her first word arrived a fraction late and very clean. He took it for thoughtfulness. Everybody does. I had read the file. It was a stutter that a girl had beaten, a long time ago and with nobody's help, into a pause, and the pause had been trained so well it passed for judgement. Her answer gave him nothing and made him feel thanked. I watched him write the nothing down, gratefully.

The young staffer's name was Evan. I know because I watched her say it, through the porthole in the service door beside the press pen, the kind they put in kitchen doors so that the people carrying soup don't kill each other. I couldn't hear a word. I read lips well enough, and the rest of him the way I read a body, by what had been done to it. She said his first name once. She said *badge*, and *desk*. His hands went to his lanyard as if it had got tight. Then she put her hand on his arm, a handshake's length, and said, *Go home. Eat something.* She meant the second thing. That was the decent part, and I want you to keep it, because nobody would have marked her down if she hadn't said it. Thirty seconds, door to door. I have seen slower deaths certified as sudden.

In ninety minutes she was never more than an arm's length from a donor, a staffer or her phone. Not one of them was security. Nobody in that room thought she needed any. When the phone rang, she answered on the first ring, in the middle of a donor's sentence, and the donor beamed as if he had been promoted. She read one text, and as she turned her left wrist the dress watch slid towards the heel of her hand, and where it had been was a band of paler skin, wider than the watch and the wrong shape for it, the shape of a sports strap. Her face did something private for about a second. Then the phone went away and the face came back.

I graded her. I didn't decide to. It came out in the voice I use for the dictaphone. Subject: the Chief of Staff. Donors: distinction. Press: distinction. Personnel: distinction, with a note for the boy and the sandwich she had more or less told him to buy. Decent in nearly every register audible from the back of a ballroom. The failing mark is not a comment on any of this. The failing mark is for what all of this is for. You will be evaluated accordingly.

Fail.

At about ten to ten she left by the side aisle, past the press pen, and as she went she looked along the row of us. She looked at me for exactly as long as she looked at the man beside me. Her eyes passed over me the way you pass over a fire door. Three years ago I had sat across the desk in her own office for forty minutes while I signed. She had uncapped the pen for me, the blue one, and laid it down with the clip turned away from my hand, so that I would have nothing to do but the one thing, and then she had watched my hand the whole time, to make sure it did it.

She registered nothing. I was invisible to these people. It was my advantage and it was the original injury, and I felt both. The first felt like cold air on my forearms. The second came up at the back of my tongue like a coin held there too long, and I didn't swallow it.

* * *

At ten the service lift took me back up, crown first under the dome. The cardboard in the strike had gone soft with the hours and the door had begun to bite through it; one more staffer letting it swing shut and the floor would have closed with me on it. I folded the flap once more on itself and seated it back with my thumb until the latch had nothing to find. The carpet changed underfoot from a pattern designed to hide gravy to a plain charcoal designed to hide nothing. The glass-walled corner suite was dark; everyone who used it was downstairs being thanked. The suite beside it, the one I had watched being propped on my first pass, still stood ajar on its case of water. A campaign that believed nothing was coming had left it that way, and I had banked on it the way you bank on gravity.

It was a bedroom being used as a cupboard: cases of water, rope-line stanchions, lawn signs in shrink-wrap, a bed nobody had slept in, snowed on by merchandise. The air-conditioning ran colder than the ballroom's and went straight for the back of my neck.

I put the gloves on before anything came out of the bag, left and then right, and the snap of the cuffs went into the carpet and stayed there. Then I laid my things on the desk the way I lay out instruments, in the order of use. Two units, bought for cash, wiped and bagged at home under nitrile: each a lens the size of a coat button on a short lead to a body the size of a matchbox. The roll of black vinyl. A cheap tablet, for pairing.

The connecting door had a deadbolt on my side and would have one on theirs. I drew mine back and pressed my fingertips to the edge of the door. A door with its far bolt thrown sits in its frame like a tooth in its socket. This one rocked on its latch the width of a fingernail. Nobody bolts a door against themselves. It is the founding principle of every institution I have ever worked for. The handle went down and the door came towards me. Grade for the campaign, on security: the door everyone walks through and no one checks. They had already written it themselves, on the corridor door, in cardboard.

The War Room smelled of coffee that had stewed through the afternoon, of carpet shampoo, and of the citrus solvent of a whiteboard marker left uncapped on the ledge. It wasn't my red one. I noticed that I checked. Glass on two sides, and below it the city in sodium. The wall screen, asleep at the end of the long table. By the door, a credenza with a coffee urn on it, switched off at its own timer, and beside it a waste bin and a stack of paper cups.

I put the first unit behind the bezel of the wall screen, low on the left, the lens facing the length of the table, its lead into the service port on the back, which stays live because nobody in a hotel turns a screen off at the wall. The tape came off the roll with a sound I hadn't heard from that kit before, a dry, rising rasp, like a zip opened in a library. The second unit went under the lip of the credenza beside the urn, angled up and out, its lead into the urn's socket strip. One short strip each. I pressed them down with the pad of my thumb until they had decided to stay. They would talk out through the hotel's own network like any guest's laptop; the tablet was only there to introduce us. My knuckle grazed the window, and the cold of the glass came through the nitrile. Two minutes. I closed the connecting door without touching anything of theirs, and drew my own bolt home.

I peeled the gloves off inside out into my pocket and sat at the desk with the tablet. The first unit came up and gave me the dark table end-on, like a runway at night. I was pairing the second when the suite door swung in.

My pulse came up into the side of my neck, where I could have counted it without touching anything. I didn't count it.

A night houseman with a cart. About sixty, by the hands and the hairline. A name tag: DESMOND. He had come in because the door was open, which is what doors are for.

I had my left shoe off, and that was true; the heel had blistered on the stair. I turned the tablet face-down on the desk, as if that were simply where I'd put it. His eyes went to it and came off it.

"Sorry," he said. "Door was open."

"Press. They said I could file in here."

"Who said?"

"Evan."

There is an Evan on every campaign floor, and this one had been sent home to eat something. Nobody was going to ask him anything tonight. Desmond nodded as if he knew him.

"Course." He looked at my foot. "That's a blister."

"Stairs."

It was a good lie, and it had the great advantage of a blister in it. The best lies are mostly true and partly bleeding.

He looked at me then, properly, at my face, the way nobody had all night. He opened the first-aid box on his cart and held a plaster out across the corner of the desk.

I reached for it with my left hand, and the hand trembled, once. A single fine oscillation at the fingertips, there and gone. Then it was still, and I took the plaster and said thank you.

He didn't see it. He was looking round for somewhere to put the backing paper. I saw all of it.

I graded him, out of habit and something nearer gratitude. Full marks for looking. Full marks for the plaster. It was the only pass I gave all night.

"I'll leave that wider," he said on his way out, and pushed the door back against its stop with his foot. "For the air."

I had noticed the tremor. I didn't know what to do with it, so I am going to describe it to you instead. Left hand, four fingers, the thumb uninvolved. Amplitude: a millimetre, perhaps two, at the tips. One cycle, quicker than I could have made it on purpose. One cycle is not, strictly, a tremor. I am calling it one. Duration: under a second. Onset as the hand reached for something offered. Resolution spontaneous and complete. Nothing in the right hand. Nothing since. The first since my second year of residency. I told you once that you would know what it meant if they ever shook. I don't. I am only putting it down where you can see it, the way you leave something on a table between two people. You're holding it too, now.

I put the plaster on my heel and pressed its edges down with my thumbnail, and packed the desk right to left, and left the door the way Desmond had, wide, for the air.

In the lift down I held my left hand with my right, two fingers across the wrist the way you hold a patient's to count, until the doors opened. I watched it the whole way, as if it belonged to somebody on my table and I was only there to write down what it did. Under my fingers the forearm was cold, and not the ballroom's cold: Tuesday's, the kind that comes to you, there all night under the other. The walls were brushed steel and gave back nothing but a smear of light. I didn't need them to. I could feel it in the zygomatic muscles, the corners of the mouth drawn up and back and held. I was smiling. I didn't know when that had started.


# Chapter 3

I unlocked the drawer before I took my shoes off. It was a quarter past six on the Friday, and I had come in from work with a paper parcel from the butcher's and put it in the fridge, and then the key, and then the shoes. The Pell dossier went on the desk, and beside it, out of its sleeve, my working master of Lesson One, two years old and corrected in red ever since, its margins grown a second, smaller, crosser text. I had the marker uncapped before I sat down, and the smell came off the felt the way it always does, sweet and sharp, a pear-drop solvent that goes straight past the nose and taps on the backs of the eyes. It was the one from the pen tray. Not the one in the back compartment; that one had a job. I wanted an hour. One hour of Diane Pell's week that belonged to no one: not a driver, not a donor, not the Senator, not her phone. I was going to find it before I went to bed.

The hotel went first. All night, in the lift and the car and the shower, the Calloway had looked like a gift: a building she worked in, a staff corridor nobody checked, doors held open with cases of water. I had come out of it with a blister, and a houseman's plaster over the blister, still on my heel that evening and gone grubby at the edges. At the desk, under the lamp, the hotel was a gift the way a cat's gift on the doormat is a gift. I wrote *the Calloway* in the margin of the grid and ruled one line through it, against the edge of a card so it would be straight, and wrote underneath: *Too many doors. Too much carpet. Everyone she has is in the room with her.*

The grid was a week: a hundred and sixty-eight hours in seven columns, every hour sourced, two years of my own work. Weekdays: the driver at ten to seven, HQ, the War Room, donor dinners, home after eleven. Home was an alarmed two-bedroom townhouse with a neighbour whose dog needed walking at all hours, which is an alarm system on a lead. Her phone was never off. She answered every call. I had seen that one for myself the night before: the phone going in the middle of a donor's sentence and Diane answering on the first ring. I wrote it across the weekday block and underlined it twice. *She answers. She always answers.* Then I underlined it a third time.

I think better with my hands busy, and my hands think better holding something that was recently alive. Six lamb's kidneys, out of their paper onto the board. The butcher had asked whether I wanted them trimmed, and when I said I'd manage he looked at my hands and said he didn't doubt it, and I liked him more for that than I have liked most people this year.

Raw kidney smells of what it does for a living. Under the lamb, which is a clean, milky, grassy sweetness, there is a thin note of ammonia, a public-lavatory note if I'm honest, and I am. I named it before I liked it. Then I liked it. The capsule peels like the skin on a grape; I pinched it at the convex border and drew it back towards the hilum and it came away whole, translucent, a small glove turned inside out. Then the knife, from the convex border through to the hilum, so that each kidney fell open into two halves like a pair of ears, and there it all was in section: the cortex dark and granular at the rim, the medulla paler and striated beneath it, and at the hilum the renal crest and the pelvis, the white funnel at the core. A sheep's kidney is unipyramidal. One pyramid, one crest where ours have papillae, no calyces at all, a single drain where ours have a dozen. I snipped the white core out of each half with the kitchen scissors. The kidney is the body's compliance department. It sees everything that passes, keeps what is useful, and sends the rest down a tube to be forgotten about. I had six of them on a board, and I had never enjoyed a department more.

I washed my hands with the unscented soap and went back to the desk with them still damp. The ammonia stayed on my fingertips. It came up off the page every time I touched the grid. Saturday was HQ from ten. Sunday afternoon, HQ. Sunday at four, her mother: the dossier had it from a trade-magazine profile, the quotation copied out in my own small capitals: *I call my mother every Sunday at four, no matter what.* Sunday evening, the weekly call sheet. And Sunday morning, in the narrow column between seven and noon, a single word in my hand: *home?* I had written it months ago, with the question mark, and never gone back. I circled the question mark in red.

The butter went into the pan. I let it foam and fall and turn the colour of strong tea and smell of hazelnuts, the second before it turns, and laid the kidneys in cut side down. They seized and arched and spat. Ninety seconds. Then over, and in with a spoon of English mustard, a pinch of cayenne, Worcestershire, a splash of water to bring the pan back into a sauce, and two slices of toast standing by. The cayenne got into the air and went for the back of my throat. The heat came off the pan onto my face and got no further. My forearms stayed cold, the cold I had brought home from the lift.

I ate from the pan at the counter, standing, with the papers on the far side of the flat out of the splash line. When I am planning I eat on my feet. I'll tell you the facts about dinner. All six, and both slices, and I wiped the pan with the last corner of the second one. The kidneys were pink at the centre, with that dense, iron-rich give they have, half liver and half muscle, and the mustard was hot enough to make my eyes run once, and the toast had gone soft under the sauce in the middle and stayed crisp at the edges. It was twenty to eight. I was still hungry when the pan was clean.

I graded the dossier, because it was mine. It was thorough. It was sourced. It was handsomely kept, and it failed. B-minus, for diligence, which is the grade you give the student who has done everything except the thing.

Then I did the sum again, a fingertip on each hour. Every hour of Diane Pell's week was either guarded or attended. A driver, staff, donors, an alarm, a phone she always answered, her mother at four. The campaign had made her the one person on it who was never alone, and not one of those guards was there for her sake; the campaign had never once imagined that anybody might come for the woman who kept its files. Her week was so well defended it had no room left in it for a life, which is the kind of thing you only ever say about other people's diaries. I looked at my left hand on the page, the one from the lift. It was still. I'm telling you so, and moving on.

There was a gap in the dossier, and when I saw it I saw what it was. A hundred and sixty-eight hours and not one of them unsecured is not a description of a woman. It is a description of a file with a hole in it. The hour existed, and I didn't have it. I wrote in the margin, in red: *Where does she go to not be reached?*

* * *

By ten I had moved to the writing desk proper, with the clean laptop open and nothing else on in the flat, so that the screen was the only light, blue-white over the edge of the dossier, turning the red ink black. The pan was soaking in the sink. Through the wall the fridge started and stopped. The question sat in the margin, and I did what I always do, which is go back to the body. If you want to know where a woman goes, look at what the going has done to her.

At the fundraiser she had read one text, and as she turned her wrist to read it the dress watch had slid down, and under it, on the left wrist, there had been a band of paler skin, wider than the dress watch and squarer, the shape of a sports strap. On Tuesday I had read the same kind of band on a woman from a bus shelter, a centimetre wide, where a hospital bracelet had been. Diane's had been put there by something she chose. She wore something that counted her steps, on days when nobody was looking at her, and things that count steps keep the count somewhere.

Behind my signed agreement in the drawer there was a printout, three years old: Diane's cover email, and in its header one stray CC to an address that wasn't the campaign's. Her own. I had clipped it there the day the email came, on purpose, because a private address on a public woman is a key, and I keep the keys I don't yet need.

I made an account on Trailmark from the clean laptop with no real name on it. I had read the privacy policy first, all of it, which makes me one of perhaps four people living; the app does not tell anyone who has looked at them. I put her address into the field that offers to find people you know.

She came up at twenty past ten. *D. Pell.* The profile had never been set to private, because the default is public, and Diane had never read the settings. The Compliance Director of a campaign under ten weeks from victory had accepted the terms and conditions without reading them, and I found this so moving that I had to look away from the screen. The avatar was the reservoir at dawn with no face in it, which was the most honest self-portrait I have seen anyone on a campaign produce. One hundred and forty-one recorded walks. All of them on Sundays.

I read them the way I read a run of bloods, the whole column before any single value. Every one was the same route: a loop of 6.4 kilometres, clockwise, from the south car park. Start times ran from 7:31 to 7:48. The early ones were the rule. The handful after twenty to were short-cut Sundays, or walks begun from the far side of the car park. The first walk was logged on a Sunday, eleven days after the date on my agreement. I am giving you that because it is the date. My mouth tasted of the kidneys, and of nothing else.

Then the pause. At the same coordinates every week, 3.1 kilometres in, the track stopped moving and stayed stopped for eleven minutes, give or take one: there by five past eight or ten past, gone again by twenty past. I knew it was a bench before the satellite view showed me one. Eleven minutes of nothing, every week, logged to the second. The only person I had ever met who kept better records of her own absence was me. Campaign security, which paid for her driver and her alarm and had her private address on her own personnel file: fail.

There were four Sundays when she walked about a third slower: the Sunday after Thanksgiving, two at Christmas, one in late May. Weather, I thought, or a knee. A woman of fifty-two is allowed a knee. I logged them.

Then I laid her track over the satellite map. After the bench the loop came round below the dam, and there, about 1.1 kilometres on, a trail marker post stood where a spur of path went off between sumac. Forty metres up the spur, behind the trees, was a square of dark roof: an old pump station, brick, abandoned, with the sumac grown up to its walls. Up from the bench at twenty past, and on round the water to the post. I did the arithmetic twice. She would pass the foot of the spur at 8:33, give or take nine minutes.

I printed the satellite view by cable, from the clean laptop, and the printer pushed it out warm, and I laid it on the desk and drew an X on the spur in red. The marker dragged a little on the cheap paper. Beside the X I wrote *8:33*.

Then I waited for the feeling, the way you wait for a result from the lab. I put two fingers of my right hand on my left wrist, the grip from the lift, and counted. Sixty-four. My hands were still. I licked my lips before I wrote, and they still tasted of cayenne.

In the margin of the dossier I wrote: *Affect: nothing identifiable.* Directly underneath it, in the same hand and the same red, I wrote: *Lamb's kidneys ×6, devilled. Toast ×2. Standing.* I didn't ask myself why the second line belonged on that page.

* * *

After midnight the pan was washed, the counter wiped, the dossier squared on the desk, and I took the rug out from under the writing desk where it lives. It is a flatweave, oxblood and bone, and I give its length in centimetres, which I never do for furniture, because I was measuring it as something else: about a hundred and seventy, and about eight kilograms. It smelled of wool and the old dust of under-the-desk. I rolled it tight on the kitchen floor and belted it at two points with my own belts, one round the waist and one round the knees, if a rug has knees, and by then it did. I know how this looks. A woman in a T-shirt and socks, at twenty past twelve, squaring up to a carpet. I would grade the exercise C for dignity and A for commitment, and the commitment carries it.

Diane, from the night before, out of her heels: about a hundred and sixty-five centimetres, about seventy kilos. The rug was a lighter woman than Diane by some sixty kilograms, and a great deal more cooperative, and it had never once answered its phone. The rug could teach me the order of my hands. It could not teach me her weight, and I noted that too. Then I gave it a face. A strip of surgical tape across the top end, pressed down with my thumbnail until it had properly decided to stay, and on the tape, in the desk red marker, two dots for the nostrils and a line for the mouth. It was the first time the marker had touched anything standing in for her. It looked faintly surprised, which was fair.

I stood it up against the counter edge, and the face came out about where hers would be. The industrial tape was pewter, the same make as the roll I would take with me on the day, not the roll itself; that one was never going to carry my kitchen on its edges. It came off the roll louder than I'd have liked in a sleeping building, like a sheet being ripped for bandages. I tore it into strips and laid them along the counter edge in a row, and folded one end of each back on itself to make a tab, the way you make a dressing, so that a gloved finger can find the end without looking. Two pairs of gloves, one over the other, as it would be on the day. My phone was propped against the radio in airplane mode, recording, and from the radio, low, came a late call-in show: a man from the suburbs saying it was done, the whole thing, he'd put his house on it. I wondered which room he would hand over first.

She would have a phone at her ear. A woman on a phone has one hand and half her hearing, and you come from the phone side. Hand over the nose and mouth, weight back and down, a strip across the mouth, then the wrists, which a rug can't help you with. I have spent fifteen years at the wrong end of this done badly; the table tells you what a struggle leaves, in the fingernails and the forearms and the backs of the heels, and I was working out how to leave none of it. The strip rode across the back of my left glove, tab towards the fingertips. The right hand did the covering, and came off the mouth only once the left hand had the nose, to find the tab blind and lay the strip across in one pull.

Attempt one. The rug folded at the wrong place, at the waist belt instead of the knees, and the strip stuck to itself, and I ended up on one knee holding a carpet in an embrace. At half speed it played like footage of a stranger. Seven point two seconds, first contact to the tab pressed down. I have sat through a great many teaching videos. None of them showed a consultant pathologist losing a wrestling match to soft furnishings, and the profession is poorer for it. Fail.

Attempt two. I corrected for the fold, took the weight down across my own thigh instead of fighting it, and found the tab first time. Five point one. The weave had caught the inside of my right wrist on the way down, just above the glove cuff, on bare skin, and left a friction abrasion about two centimetres long, superficial, weeping a little, with a few fibres of oxblood wool in it, and I documented it as I would on anyone else. The tiles were cold under my knees through the socks. The retired couple downstairs have complained once in four years, so I lowered the rug the last few inches by hand. If they ever asked, moving furniture at one in the morning was the better of the available explanations.

Closed is not the same as kept closed. On the day the airway would be shut just long enough to get her through the door and down, and then given back, because she would need her voice for what came after. A body without air does not go quiet first. It fights harder first: the diaphragm hauling against nothing, the neck cording, the hands going for whatever is over the face, and only near the edge does it stop arguing, and where the edge is depends on the lungs and the fitness and the fright of whoever is under your hand. Nobody knows it to the second. I would be counting, and I would let go early.

Attempt three. Phone side. Hand. Weight. Tab. Mouth. The rug went down without a sound I hadn't chosen.

On the video, at half speed, it was three point eight seconds. Under four. The airway was closed: my left hand over the dots, the strip across the line. I was sitting back on my heels on the kitchen floor with a carpet in my arms, and I was breathing hard, and I was happy. The kitchen smelled of adhesive and wool and, under them, me. I'll give you the findings. Respiratory rate up. A flush across the chest and the front of the throat, warm to my own hand. Forearms still cold. And the number, which pleased me the way a clean section pleases me, three point eight, a personal best.

I deleted the video, and then deleted it again from the folder where deleted things wait, because I know where people look. I peeled the face off the rug and folded it in on itself, sticky side in, with the used strips, and put the bundle in a zip bag and the bag in the back compartment of the drawer, and turned the key. I unbelted the rug and unrolled it. I carried the tape and the gloves and the marker back to the desk. It was half past one.

I capped the marker and laid it on the printout, parallel to the edge of the page, with its tip a finger's width from the X, so that it pointed. The rug went back under the writing desk, its corners square to the boards, as if nothing had been asked of it.


# Interlude — Diane

Diane was home by five past ten, which for a Wednesday was early. The donor dinner had run short.

She did not turn on the overhead light. She used the one over the range and left the rest of the kitchen dark. In the hall she had keyed off the alarm at the panel by the stairs without looking, and stepped out of her shoes, one and then the other, each heel pushed off with the toe of the opposite foot. The tile was cool through her tights.

Her phone said sixty-one unread. She turned it face-down on the counter. She had been on since six that morning. Eight weeks and six days. She knew it without counting.

She opened the fridge and stood in the cold from it. Everything in there had a strip of masking tape on it and a date in black marker, in her own hand. The heel of cheddar said last Thursday. She cut around the dry edge and ate what was under it, standing, with the last of the water crackers, and poured half a glass of white from the open bottle in the door. It tasted of the fridge. The cheese still tasted faintly of wax.

Then she called Olivia. Not a text. A text could be managed, and tonight she did not want to manage anything.

Four rings. Olivia answered in a whisper, then a fire door closed behind her and the whisper became her ordinary voice, out in the corridor. Two rooms along, someone was playing music.

"Kenzie's asleep," Olivia said. "With the window open. In September. Like a Viking."

It all came out at once. The window open all night, and a fan on. Olivia awake under one blanket, cold. And two nights ago Kenzie had borrowed the good sweater. To sleep in.

Diane sat down for the first time since six, on the step stool by the pantry. She pressed her thumb into the ball of one foot. She listened the way she listened at work, one ear on what was being said and one on what was wanted.

"Vikings didn't borrow sweaters," she said. "They took them."

"Mom."

Buy a second blanket; she would pay. Don't make it a fight. Put on paper what you want and what she wants, and a schedule for the window. If that didn't hold, take it to the RA and get it agreed.

"I don't want a process," Olivia said. "I want her to close the window."

"The process is how she closes the window."

She believed it. It was good advice, and kind.

Then Olivia, in a gentler voice: how was she. Was she sleeping.

Diane opened her mouth to say fine. It did not come. There was the old catch, a beat in which the word stood in the doorway and would not move. Then a different one came out from behind it.

"Tired."

Olivia waited through the pause without filling it, as she had since she was small. People at work took the pause for thoughtfulness. Olivia never had.

"Are you still doing the walk?" Olivia said, teasing now. "Do you still wear the watch?"

Diane looked at the dress watch on her wrist, then at the sports watch lying on its back at the end of the counter. Olivia had given it to her three years ago, at the end of that summer, and set it up on her phone for her.

"Every Sunday," Diane said. Seven o'clock, the reservoir, round the loop. A bench about halfway. Ten minutes there, maybe, with whatever she was reading. Then home. Mother at four.

"Why don't you just sleep in?"

Her reading glasses were still pushed up in her hair, forgotten. For once she did not arrange the answer before she gave it.

"Because it's the one hour of my life I'm not reachable."

Then she said it again. "To not be reachable for one hour of my life. That's all I want. One."


# Chapter 4

Diane Pell sat down on the bench at nine minutes past eight, which was within ninety seconds of what three years of her own fitness app had said she would do, and I gave the app a distinction before I gave her anything. I was a hundred and eighty metres away across the water, on the west shore, in a waxed hat and a fleece the colour of lichen, with binoculars, a field guide, a thermos and a logbook of herons. The logbook is real. I keep it. Nobody who watches a woman write *heron, 1, on the riprap, 7:52* in a small careful hand thinks about anything but herons, and the herons, in fairness to them, think about nothing at all. Birdwatchers are the only people allowed to stare at a stranger through ten-times magnification for forty minutes and be thought gentle.

My own phone was at home in its foil sleeve, in a drawer, telling nobody where I was. I timed her on my watch.

The reservoir smelled the way still water smells at the end of summer: wet stone, the green slick of algae where the riprap went under, pine resin coming down off the slope behind me, goose droppings on the path, all of it sharpened by the damp that comes off a body of water in the early morning. A cormorant stood on a post in the shallows with its wings held open to dry, like a man showing you he isn't armed. The water lapped at the stones below my boots. Across it, Diane took a paperback out of her jacket and opened it, and I heard the small flat sound a paperback makes when the spine is cracked back, which I could not possibly have heard at that distance and heard anyway. I am telling you so you'll know the difference between what I observed that morning and what I supplied.

She read for eleven minutes. She marked the page with a folded receipt, put the book back inside her jacket, and stood up at twenty past eight.

I had her body in the glass the whole time. About a hundred and sixty-five centimetres, about seventy kilograms, compact, with a swimmer's shoulders gone soft, the deltoids still broad under the olive waxed cotton but the definition banked like a fire; a slate shirt under the jacket, buttoned to the second button; the fat beginning its slow migration to the waist, the way it does after menopause, when the oestrogen stops telling it where to sit and it goes, like a guest nobody has seated, to the middle of the room. Her stride was short and brisk and economical, with almost no swing in the arms, the gait of a woman carrying a folder that isn't there. I put her liver at fifteen hundred grams and a good clean colour, a glass of white most nights and never two. I have been wrong about livers before, but never by much, and never in their favour. I liked her. I liked the way she sat down as if the bench had been booked. I had already weighed her. I want you to have both of those in the same hand, because I did.

The route I knew from the app, and that morning I watched it come true. Out of the south car park, clockwise, six point four kilometres of loop; the bench at three point one, the halfway mark; eleven minutes; then on round the second half, below the dam, where the path runs under a slope of sumac just starting to go the colour of a burn, past a trail marker post where a spur climbs forty metres through the scrub to a pump station whose roof I could just see from my side. She passed the post at about eight thirty-three and did not look up the spur. In the hundred and forty-one Sundays the app had given me, her track had never once gone up it. Nobody walked with her. Nobody waited for her in the car park. There was no detail, and her phone stayed inside her jacket the whole time I had her, which for a woman who answers on the first ring was like watching a smoker not smoke. The Chief of Staff of a campaign nine weeks out from an election was as easy to find as a mailbox, and about as well guarded, and every person who might have told her so was downtown in a glass room being sure of things.

My hands were cold on the binoculars, the knuckles first, the damp getting into the joints the way it gets into an old house. It was not a cold morning. It was my hands. The coffee in the thermos had been made at five and had burnt in the flask, black and bitter and tasting of the inside of the lid, and I drank it anyway, all of it. I was hungry. I had brought no food, because a birder who eats looks like a picnic, and a picnic is remembered. I noted the hunger and drank the coffee.

The next Sunday was the sixth of September, and I had planned a close pass. I would watch her sit from the same place on the west shore and, once she was well gone, go round to the bench myself and walk the stretch to the marker post at her pace, against the watch, thirteen minutes, her twenty past to her thirty-three past, on my own legs, at the place where it would happen. You do not learn a road from a map.

At nine minutes past eight there was nobody on the bench. At twelve minutes past there was nobody. At sixteen minutes past she came out of the trees, late, and she was not alone. There was a young woman beside her, taller, with an iced coffee in one hand and the other hand talking, and I knew the face from the dossier photographs before the binoculars had finished deciding where it was. Olivia.

I graded the plan before I graded anything else. It was Labor Day weekend. I had not checked the Wellesley academic calendar. The app had shown me four Sundays that ran a third slower than the rest, and I had looked at their dates and not at what the dates had in common. A two-year plan, failed on a public holiday. I docked the mark and logged it.

They shared the bench. Diane did not take out the book. Through the glass I watched the daughter say something with her free hand, a long shape in the air, and watched the mother laugh, head back, a sound I could not hear at all and did not supply. Olivia walked with Diane's short stride and none of her stillness; even sitting, her feet were going. Heritable gait.

I capped the lenses, front and back, and closed the logbook on the morning's one heron, and walked back to the car the long way round. Diane had a daughter in the glass now, not in a file.

* * *

On the Tuesday I watched the War Room from my kitchen, standing at the sink with the laptop balanced along my left forearm and a peach in my right hand. I plan standing up. I eat standing up when I plan. It was about nine o'clock and the peach was one of the last of the year, the skin furred and loosening, and the flesh gave under my teeth all at once, sweet and a little fermented where it had bruised against its neighbours in the bag, and the juice ran down to my wrist and I let it.

The camera in the bezel of the wall screen looked straight down the table, so I had faces, a dozen of them, and the glass walls, and the city lit up behind them like a circuit board, but not the screen itself; the numbers I got by ear. The sound was compressed and slightly late, laughter arriving in flat little packets, and under everything the hotel's air handling, a hum like a held note, and somewhere a chair on carpet. Brandt's chair had no one in it. It was Tuesday, and on Tuesdays he played the piano at the Tabard Club from nine until eleven. I registered his absence the way you register a missing instrument on a tray.

Holt was being briefed, in his shirtsleeves, one ankle on his knee like a man at a barbecue. He was up six in the tracking average. An aide read it out and said, pleased with himself, "Less mistakes from here and it's his to lose."

"Fewer," Holt said, and smiled, so that the correction arrived with a gift in its other hand, and the aide laughed, and so did everyone else. He was right. He was always right about grammar. I gave him full marks for syntax again, because I am fair, and because there was nobody in the kitchen to see me do it.

Then the donor. A man who had given a great deal had written to ask whether the Senator might attend his daughter's wedding, and, separately, whether the Senator might be photographed with his dog. Holt did the donor's voice. It was a slightly wet, slightly wounded baritone, the voice of a man who has been told no only by waiters, and the room came apart, and I laughed too, out loud, alone, in my kitchen, with peach on my chin. It was a very good impression. He was always very good at doing other people.

Diane did not laugh. She was at his left, two seats down, with a tablet flat on the table and her reading glasses pushed up into her hair, and she let the laugh run exactly as long as it was useful and then took the wedding off the table in one sentence: "A card, a staffer, and no dog." She moved him on to the next item, and on the way, before one word, she paused. The staff took it for thoughtfulness. I had watched them do it at the fundraiser. I have the dossier. It wasn't thought. It was a door she had taught herself to hold shut, a long time ago, and she still checked it every time she walked past.

Holt, who learned the names of his staff's children the way other men learn the names of wines, said, "She get back all right?"

"Monday night. The train."

"Sophomore year's the good one. Tell her I said so."

Two days before, I had been on the far shore of the same daughter. I noted it and went on.

You should have seen them. Twelve people in a glass box on the thirty-first floor, entirely confident that glass only works in one direction. Not one of them looked at the door, or at the walls, or at the bezel of the screen they were all facing, or at the lip of the credenza by the coffee urn, where a second little eye the size of a shirt button sat in its curl of black vinyl and watched them pour. They were the safest people in the city because they had agreed among themselves that they were, and they had been safe for so long that their safety had become a kind of furniture, and I thought that was very funny, and found my jaw had set while I thought it.

Holt stood. He gathered his jacket off the back of the chair and shook it out and put an arm into it, already half turned towards the door, and said over his shoulder, lightly, the way you'd ask whether someone had remembered to lock the car: "And the old files. Those are closed?"

"Yes," Diane said.

No pause. Not before that word.

It took five seconds. He went out. She said something to the woman beside her about Thursday. No name had been spoken, not mine and not anyone's, and I could not tell, then or now, whether he meant me, or a cabinet he had never once pictured with anything inside it. I have watched people discuss a case over a body. I had never before watched two people discuss me, in five seconds, and move on to Thursday.

I had to put the laptop down. I set it on the counter and squared it to the edge, parallel, the way I put things down, and my hands did it well; they were steady. The peach was still in my right hand, bitten down to the last third, the stone showing through the flesh like a knuckle through skin, and my fingers were sticky to the wrist. There was a taste at the back of my tongue like a coin held there too long. The cold had come into my forearms, on the volar surface, where it had come on the night of the debate. I stood and held the peach and did not finish it.

* * *

On the Saturday I drove out in the late afternoon, in a hatchback the colour of slate that nobody has ever looked at twice, and turned off the county road onto the old water-authority service track. There was a lawn sign at the turn, navy and gold, MARCHETTI in block capitals, and the grass around it had been mown more recently than anything at the pump station. I parked in the gravel lay-by where the track bends out of sight of the trail and walked the last nine hundred metres with the bag on my shoulder.

The pump station sits below the dam like something the dam forgot it had: one storey of brick gone the colour of liver, iron pipework coming out of the walls and going back into them, a dry wet-well under a grating, and a steel door with a hasp. On the door a water-authority notice had been bleached to the colour of weak tea. On an earlier night I had cut the authority's padlock off the hasp, its shackle rusted into the body and its keyway packed with grit and spider silk, and hung one of my own in its place, the same make, the same brass gone dull. Nobody had noticed. Nobody checks.

Inside it smelled of rust and cold wet concrete and machine oil gone to varnish on the pipes, and mouse, and from the grating a long mineral breath, like the inside of a well, which it was. It was colder than the evening outside, about eleven degrees, and it kept them.

I worked it the way I set out a tray. I swept the floor and bagged the sweepings, because dust keeps a shoe print better than plaster does, and mine were already in it from the night of the padlock. I laid the heavy sheeting and taped down its edges, the plastic crackling with static against my wrists. I staged the kit along the far wall, parallel to the brick, left to right in the order of use: zip-ties, industrial tape, black vinyl tape, the red marker, the foil sleeve. At the base of the old pump housing there was an iron bracket bolted into the plinth, and I took hold of it with both hands and put my weight back on it, all of it, and it did not give a millimetre. A woman of seventy kilograms, sitting, with her hands behind her, would never put that much on it. Then I stood in the middle of the room and named the things an investigator would look for in it, and took each of them away before he could find it. It is most of what I do.

The recorder was a cheap digital thing, bought for cash, not my phone. I taped it to the back of the trail marker post, forty metres downslope, where the spur meets the path, set it running, and walked back up through the sumac.

Clap one. Inside, door shut, as hard as I could make it, harder than anyone claps for anything. A brick room with iron in it gives a clap back to you sharp, with a short slap off the pipes, like a ruler brought down on a desk. Then down to the post, the recorder against my ear. Under the birds, faint and flat, a knock, the sound of somebody's door three houses along. It had come through the seam. Fail. I marked it as one.

Clap two, as hard. Outside the door, for reference. On the playback it was loud and clean and dry, the scrub swallowing the tail of it, and the birds stopped for it, all of them at once, the way a room stops for a dropped plate. Now I knew what a sound from up there weighed by the time it reached the path.

There is no dignified way for a woman in her thirties to applaud a building with nobody in it and then go and listen to a tape of it. I have accepted this.

I fixed the seam. Adhesive foam weatherstrip round the frame, the backing paper curling off onto my sleeves; the hasp side packed; a moving blanket hung on the inside of the door; the hinges greased until the door swung without a word. It was tape, done properly, which is the only way I do tape.

Clap three. Inside, door shut, as hard. My palms stung by then, the thenar eminences pink and tingling where the small capillaries had been asked to take it, and my hands were steady through all of it. I checked. I walked down and pressed play. Birds. The water at the stones. A cyclist's freewheel ticking past and away along the path, a long insect sound. Nothing from the building. Nothing at all. My patients are very quiet. It had never occurred to me to want that from a room. I played it twice, to be thorough, and then once more for no reason I put a name to.

I peeled the recorder off the post and put it in my pocket, and went back up the spur, and inside, and pulled the door shut behind me against the blanket, and stood in the dark.

It is not instant. At first there is only black, and the afterimage of the doorway sliding away when you try to look at it. High in the back wall, away from the trail, an airbrick let in what was left of the dusk, less light than a watch face gives. It is enough. The rods take over from the cones; the rhodopsin in them, bleached by a day of light, regenerates a little at a time, and the room comes back to you not in colour but in no colour at all, the pipes first, then the edge of the pump housing, then the pale line of the kit along the far wall. Twenty minutes, by my count, and I counted. My pulse slowed while I stood there until I could hear it, and then I could hear nothing else.

I did not mind it, and I stood in it longer than I needed to. I found I liked it.

The oxtail had ridden out in a plastic container in the footwell, braised the night before with butter beans and onions and most of a bottle of wine until the meat had let go of the bone, and the night in the fridge had set the fat to a pale lid on top and firmed the gelatin underneath, so that the whole thing was a single trembling piece I could have turned out onto a plate. I didn't have a plate. The gelatin went to liquid on my tongue before I had closed my mouth on it, salt and marrow and the wine gone dark and sweet, and the beans had drunk all of it and gave it back soft as butter. There were bones in it, and I knew what they were: the coccygeal vertebrae, which is all oxtail is, each one a little smaller than the last, the transverse processes running down to nothing, and I sucked each one clean, the smallest last.

I ate dinner in the car at the lay-by with the dome light off, all of it, cold, out of the container with my fingers, and then I licked the container, corners first.


# Chapter 5

I had been standing at the gap in the door since ten past five, and by eight the station had got into everything I had brought with me. Wet concrete first, the mineral cold of it, a smell like the underside of a paving slab. Iron gone to rust along the pipework, the smell of a cast-iron pan nobody has bothered to oil. From the grating over the dry wet-well came the old sump breath of oil and water that had stood a long time and then left. And through the gap, a hand's width of it, the reservoir: weed, wet stone, the green, faintly metallic breath that open water gives off at first light before anything has warmed it. I stood in all of it and breathed it, and I waited for her.

The dark had stopped being dark hours before, the rods long since in charge, and I knew where everything was. The sheeting, taped at its edges. The pump housing on its concrete plinth, and at its base the iron bracket I had hung my weight from on Saturday, with two zip-ties already threaded through it and laid out long across the floor, cut to a length I had measured on the bench at home. The kit in its row along the far wall, squared to the brick. At my feet the bag with the rest: two more ties for her ankles; the head-torch with its filter, switched off; the page in its clear sleeve; the zip bag I am not going to describe yet. I had liked the dark on Saturday. I liked it better with something to wait for in it.

I had suited up by feel at a quarter past five: white coverall, hair cover, overshoes, two pairs of nitrile, the hood down on my shoulders for now. Under the hair cover an earpiece ran on its wire to the phone in my hand, one of a pair of prepaids. The other I had put out at ten to five, face-down in the grass beside the door at the top of the sumac spur, forty metres up from the trail marker post. My padlock hung open on the hasp outside, where anyone could have seen it and nobody would look. A rubber wedge held the door off the frame, the blanket was tied back clear of the gap, and a loop of cord on the inside was how I would shut it.

The station holds at about eleven degrees whatever the morning is doing, and it was in me. It came up through the soles of my overshoes from the concrete, and it had settled into the backs of my hands inside both layers of glove, and into the hinge of my jaw, which I had to remind to unclench. It was not a cold I had chosen. I stood in it anyway. I had moved perhaps a handful of times in three hours, each one a weight shift from heel to heel, and I recorded the stillness the way I would record anyone's: female, upright, hands at rest, breathing slow, mouth dry.

The mouth was dry because I had drunk nothing since midnight. I have drained the bladders of enough people who died of cold to know what three hours on cold concrete does to one, the blood pulled in off the skin and the kidneys reading the extra volume as a surplus and getting rid of it, and I had no intention of leaving my DNA in a pump station for the sake of comfort. My tongue sat against my palate like a stamp that wouldn't take. I had eaten nothing since the oxtail in the car. I said nothing to myself about that.

I checked my hands. Since Desmond's plaster, I check. They were steady. I checked them again, which is not the same thing as doubting them. From eight o'clock I kept them working, fist and spread, twenty at a time, the way you tell a patient with a new cannula, and between sets I tucked them under my arms, gloves and all, where the blood still was. Cold fingers are slow fingers, and nothing that was going to happen at half past eight could be done slowly.

At about ten to six the gap had gone from black to slate. Then a thin bone colour came up off the water, and a mist lay on the reservoir the way fat lies on the top of cold stock, and a heron was standing in the shallows on one leg as if it had been put there to scale the view. I would enter it in the logbook later. I keep the logbook honestly. By seven the sky had gone over to ash and stayed there. Nobody was on the trail. Three years of her Sundays sat in the app, public by default, and nobody at the campaign had ever thought to change the setting.

I had her numbers. The app had given me a hundred and forty-one Sundays and I had given it two of my own. Out of the south car park between 7:31 and 7:48. Clockwise. The bench at 3.1 kilometres by five or ten past eight; eleven minutes with the paperback; up at twenty past; on round the second half under the dam to the post at the foot of my spur at 8:33, give or take nine. I walked the loop in my head, at her pace, and graded it. A sensible route. Flat, open, well used, nothing on it that could reach her. In a hundred and forty-one Sundays she had never once left the loop, and that was the only consistency I needed her to have. Distinction.

The last time I had stood on this morning she had come out of the trees with her daughter, and I had capped the lens and gone home. I had checked since.
