Monday night, the train: I'd heard her say it to him myself, through a bezel, eating a peach. Olivia was back at college. I listened for two sets of footsteps anyway.

There was one. Gravel, coming on from the bench side, brisk, short in the stride. Through the gap I watched her come along the trail towards the post below me: the olive waxed-cotton jacket, the slate collar, hands in her pockets. She walked like a woman who had been told walking was good for her by someone she paid, and believed it because she'd paid. Six minutes early, inside the window. I had built the window. I liked her. I had her liver at fifteen hundred grams. I could not have told you, watching her come on along the gravel, which of those two facts was the warmer. It was the last time I saw her from the outside.

I put my hood up. At 8:27 I rang the phone in the grass.

It went off up the spur beside my feet, loud and cheap and insistent, and down at the post Diane stopped. *She answers. She always answers.* I had written it in her margin. I watched it be true. She looked up the spur, which her track had never once climbed in a hundred and forty-one Sundays, and then she left the loop. The whole of her followed the one sound, the way a body on the table follows when you lift a single limb: the shoulder comes, then the hip, then everything. She climbed the forty metres with her short stride gone long. On the fourth ring she was beside the door. On the fifth she bent, and picked it up, and straightened with her back to me, an arm's length from the gap, looking down the spur the way she had come.

"Olivia?"

I heard it twice. Once through the gap, in the open air, small and level. Then, a fraction of a second behind it, in my own ear, through the earpiece, closer than anyone had spoken to me in years. It is the name a mother says first to a ringing phone, before anyone has spoken. It was the only name she had.

In the dark, I almost stopped. I didn't. I noted that I almost had, the way you note a reading off a scale.

* * *

I ended the call and had the door open before the tone died. "Hello?" she said to the dead line, and looked down the spur for whoever had left a phone in the grass, and did not turn round. I was already behind her. A strip of pewter industrial tape, torn in advance with one end folded under, hung across the back of my left glove, exactly as it had on the kitchen floor. My right hand went over her mouth from behind, my left forearm went across her collarbones, and my weight went down through my knee into the back of her right knee, into the popliteal fossa, where the leg has no opinion, and the leg folded. Coffee came through the glove against my palm, her breakfast on a hot breath, and I took it as the first sample.

Backwards over the threshold, into the dark. It is an ugly manoeuvre, and I did not try to make it anything else. Her left hand came up and found my right wrist and dragged at the glove, and the glove held; her right hand still had the phone in it and knocked once against the frame going past, and kept it. A body holds on to whatever it was holding. Two steps inside I turned her and put her down, and she went down face-first, and her left cheekbone met the concrete, grit and all, which is a courtesy to the forensic examiner and none at all to the zygoma. I heard it. I logged it the way I would at the table: abrasion over the left zygoma, about two centimetres. Rushed. The tape went across her mouth from the back of my glove in under four seconds, as rehearsed, and I left the nose clear. I needed her breathing.

A knee between the scapulae. I took the prepaid out of her right fist one finger at a time and dropped it in the bag. Her wrists behind her, one into each of the loops waiting at the bracket, and the ratchets went with that small rapid sound, twice. Her ankles, the two ties from the bag and then a band of industrial tape round both. Then up, the wedge kicked clear, the blanket let down, the cord, and the door drawn shut, my heel against the weatherstrip and my shoulder against the blanket to seat it, and I heard the room go deaf. I had tested that with my hands on Saturday. It was better with her in it. Then back down, my knee between her shoulders again. She breathed through her nose, fast and wet. My own breath was the louder of the two.

It felt good. It felt good the way the clean stroke through the brain feels good, the door that closes without being slammed, except that it was not in my hand this time, it was everywhere: in the big muscles of my thighs and my back where her weight had been, in my lungs, in the heat that had come into my face, warm, actually warm, and the cold gone out of my forearms for the first time since ten past five. I was breathing hard and happy, as I had been with the rug. The rug had been more cooperative. It had also been less motivated, which in fairness to the rug was the point of the rug. My pulse was up and I let it be up. I filed it where it belonged. Rehearsal, execution, under four seconds. Pass.

Her phone buzzed. I felt it through her, in my knee: one buzz from the left side of her chest, where the jacket's inside pocket lay between her and the floor, and then nothing.

I rolled her left shoulder up on my knee and took it out. The screen lit the room, the blue of a phone at the wrong hour, and showed me a stack of notifications. The staff channel. A polling alert. The comms director, twice. Twenty-three of them. The campaign had sent her twenty-three things that morning, every one of them sure she would answer, and her daughter had sent one, and her daughter's was on top.

*are you on your walk? call me after, it's the roommate thing again.*

I didn't know about the roommate. A nineteen-year-old awake before nine on a Sunday is in trouble, or in love, or in a row, and it is usually only one of them. I didn't grade the daughter.

I held the buttons down on the side with the message open on the glass, and the slider came up over it and dimmed it, and I drew it across with a gloved knuckle. It asked me for nothing. The text was still on the screen, dimmed and legible, when it went dark. It did not go first and leave the light behind it; the words and the light went out together. Then the phone went into the foil sleeve, and I folded the sleeve shut.

* * *

A minute later I had her up. I took her under the arms and sat her with her back against the pump housing, and the wrist ties rode the bracket as I had measured them to, with enough slack for her to sit and none for anything else. I did it without a word. While I was that close I peeled the tape from her mouth. It came away with the fine vellus hair of her upper lip in it and left a dull line of adhesive behind, and I folded the strip sticky side to sticky side and put it in the bag. Nothing of mine was staying in that room unless I had decided it would.

Then I sat on the plinth of the old pump, a metre and a half from her, out of reach of a kick, and switched on the head-torch and angled it down at the page, and the station came back in oxblood: her, the housing, the sheeting, the slick of the pipework. With the hood up and the beam above and ahead of my face, and her eyes still full of the morning she had been taken out of, my face stayed black. What she had of me was a light and two gloved hands. The foil was on the concrete between us, where I had put it down.

She did not scream. She had already read the room: the shut door, the way her own breathing came straight back at her off the brick. She had understood what it had been built for. That is an administrator's skill, knowing which rooms are worth raising your voice in, and I gave her credit for it. She swallowed. For the moment she asked for nothing.

I took the page out of its sleeve and read it to her. All of it, from the subject line down, in the order I had typed it.

I had read it aloud once before, to a flat with nobody in it, and heard what it sounded like; what I had not known, because a flat cannot tell you, was what it would do when it reached the person it had been written for, and it reached her: it went out across a metre and a half of cold air in a room I had made deaf, and nothing in that room took any of it away from her, not the brick and not the blanket on the door and not the iron, so that every word arrived at the weight I had typed it, and I watched them arrive in the spill of the torch, in her face and in the set of her shoulders and in the small adjustments of a woman sitting very straight on a floor, the way you watch a weight settle on a scale until the needle stops, and the reading is the reading, and with each word that landed the warmth came back up the insides of my forearms, as it had at the door, and stayed there while I read. It was not the voice I use for the dictaphone. It was not the one I use for Hannah. It was the voice the page was written in. It was mine, and I had never heard it arrive.

*You will be evaluated accordingly.*

I lowered the page.

She was fluent. I want that on the record, because I had the dossier and I knew what she had beaten, and I was listening for it. Not one of the pauses her staff took for thought. On a floor, with her hands tied to iron, that is its own kind of discipline.

"Whatever this is," she said, "there is a process. I can walk you through it."

She led with process.

"I didn't run the press," she said. "Brandt did. I can give you Brandt."

She said it easily, like a woman who had opened with Brandt before, in other rooms, and found that it usually bought her the rest of the meeting. I already had Brandt. He was in the drawer at home, under her.

"There's a list. I can tell you who else signed."

She offered me a list the way the family offers the pathologist a history: helpfully, and some time after it could have made a difference. I heard what I expected to hear. Counsel. The witness line. Whoever had countersigned me into the cabinet and initialled the drawer shut. I didn't ask which list.

There was one name she had said that morning, once, to a ringing phone. She did not say it again. I noticed that as well.

I graded the negotiation in my head while she waited for me to choose from it. A strong opening. Poorly sourced. Wrong currency. Everything she had laid out on the concrete between us was information, because information was what a gatekeeper had; she had spent her life deciding who could have which piece of it and when, and it had never once failed to buy her something. She was sure there was a price. She was sitting in a pump station with her wrists on a bracket and a graze on her face, and she was still sure, and the certainty sat on her as neatly as her collar. I could have pinned it to the whole campaign.

At four o'clock her mother's phone was not going to ring. I mention it as a fact about the schedule.

The cold had begun to come back into my hands. Peripheral vasoconstriction: the body withdrawing its funding from the extremities to protect the core, which is the oldest policy there is, and the one the campaign ran on. I could hear her waiting.

"I don't want information," I said. "I want the grade."

She went quiet. I watched her understand it in the oxblood: not the words, which were simple, but the shape of the room around them. There was nothing she had that I would take. A woman who had spent her life inside negotiations was sitting on a concrete floor finding out what it is like to be outside one.

And I had been waiting two years to say that sentence out loud. Since the night the page went into the drawer. I had said it to nobody, and I had said it now, to her, and it had come out at exactly the weight I had always meant it to have. I report that the way I reported the almost, at the phone. My mouth was no longer dry.

The phone lay on the concrete between us in its foil, a small silver brick with her daughter's last words sealed inside it, and my pulse was in my teeth.


# Chapter 6

*Lesson One. Subject: The Gatekeeper. Function: to convert the complaint into the file, the file into the folder, the folder into the cabinet, the cabinet into the room no one enters. You have performed this function with distinction. Your failing mark is therefore not a function of incompetence. You confused process for protection. You will be evaluated accordingly.*

The pump station smelled of what it had been built to do and had stopped doing. Damp concrete, the cellar breath of it coming up through the floor. Machine oil gone to varnish in the seals of the old pump, sweetish, like the inside of a grandfather's toolbox. Rust off the iron pipework, which is iron oxide, which is a cousin at a couple of removes of what I smell at work every day, and which I found I liked having in the room. And Diane. A floral drugstore shampoo, freesia, or something that had been allowed to use freesia's name. The clean sweat of a Sunday walk, dried on her by now and being replaced, from the axillae outward, by the other sweat. The waxed cotton of her jacket, which smells like a wet dog that went to a good school.

I had turned the lantern on so that she could see me. It stood on the concrete between us, a battery LED, blue-white, the light of a fridge opened at night, and it threw both our shadows up the curved brick so that there were four of us in the room and two of them were very tall. Diane was on the floor with her back against the pump housing, knees drawn up, ankles bound, wrists behind her: one zip-tie per wrist, each joined to the iron bracket at the base of the housing. Zip-ties are designed by people who never have to take one off, which is how you know they work. Her wrists were already flushed raw at the edges of the plastic. Beside the lantern lay her phone in its foil sleeve, wrapped like a leftover.

I was in a white disposable coverall with the hood up and two pairs of nitrile gloves, and I looked like a crime scene that had arrived early. I sat on the pump's plinth, out of her reach, my face turned a little off hers; I know what breath carries. My hands were cold inside the gloves. They were steady. I checked, and did nothing with the information. My pulse was still up from the door, the knee, the tape; I could feel it in the hinge of my jaw. Somewhere in the dark a drip fell into something, slowly, like a clock that had been asked to keep it down.

"I'm going to explain the course," I said.

Earlier, in the oxblood of the head-torch, she had seen a light and two hands. Now she had a face, and I watched her open a file on it.

I explained it the way the convenor does on the first morning, standing at the front with the handbook, to a room that has not yet worked out which of them will fail. Three pages. Three subjects. Three evaluations. She was the first. The grade was not negotiable, and it was not pending. It had been given two years ago. Today was only when it was handed back.

She listened the way compliance directors listen: as if she would be asked to initial it later. When I stopped she waited a full second to be certain I had stopped, which is a courtesy you learn in rooms where interrupting costs money.

"If this is about a complaint," she said, "there are channels."

"I know. I used them."

I was pleased with that. I hadn't planned it. Five words, and the whole case for the prosecution in them, and she would have to sit with them on the floor, because there was nowhere else for her to sit.

She was quiet. Then, carefully, choosing each word as though it came out of an account she intended to keep in credit: "Is anyone near Olivia?"

"No one is near Olivia."

It was true. I said it quickly, and heard myself say it quickly, and graded the speed as a kindness, and took a mark off for it. Nobody had asked me to be kind. It wasn't on the paper.

Then I gave her a number. I didn't need notes. "LC-0311."

It is in the footer of every page of every one of the campaign's nondisclosure agreements, under a thin rule: counsel's matter number. A gatekeeper knows the numbers on her own gates; she had walked that one through the building herself, and she would have known it the way you know your own postcode. Her eyes went to the number first. I watched them do it, a small movement up and to the left, a woman reading a footer off the inside of her own skull. Then they came down, in the lantern, to my face.

She made a sound. It was small, a dry click at the back of the throat that she had not sent for, the larynx shutting on something it had not been told to swallow. I know that sound. I have heard it down the telephone from mothers and from husbands at the moment my sentence reaches its noun. I had never been the noun before.

She looked at me for a long time. I let her. At the fundraiser her eyes had passed over me the way you pass over a fire door. In three years nobody from the campaign had looked at me and had anything happen behind the face, and now something was happening behind hers, slowly, and I had waited long enough for it that I could wait through it.

"I'm sorry," she said. "I'm so sorry. I should have protected you from that."

She said it quietly, and fluently, and she meant it. She was not apologising for the agreement, or the file, or the folder, or the cabinet, or the room no one enters. She was apologising for the thing the file was about. She was sorry she had not kept me safe from it.

It was the worst thing she could have said. She still believed the file had been the protection and only the protecting had failed, as though the cabinet were a good cabinet someone had forgotten to lock. I watched her not know why. For one line I was not amused by anything, and a taste came up at the back of my tongue like a coin held there too long, and then the line was over.

* * *

I am going to describe this properly. I am the only person qualified to.

First I let her sit with the grade: two hours, she against the housing and I on the plinth, not speaking. It is the part of the course where the student reflects. Then I stood up.

The black vinyl first. I tore two strips and the roll gave them up with a dull, almost polite unpeeling, the sound of someone getting up from a leather chair. Temple to temple, the first over the brows and the upper lids, the second a little lower, overlapping, pressed down along the zygomatic arches with my thumbs. Her eyelashes moved against the adhesive once, a small trapped flutter under my thumb, like a pulse you are not sure you have found, and then they stopped. She was in the dark. When I opened the zip bag her shoulders came up at the sound, a noise she had heard over a thousand packed lunches and never once as a threat.

The paper in the bag was photocopies of pages one to eight of my own campaign agreement, no signature page, cross-cut at my kitchen table into confetti the size of fingernail parings. I held the bag up in front of her blindfold, for form's sake. It was the first time anyone at the campaign had been asked to swallow one.

Her mouth was open. The pump housing held her head. I put the fingertips of my left hand either side of the larynx, at the borders of the thyroid cartilage, to steady the head and pin the larynx so that a swallow could not carry the paper down from where I meant it to sit. With two gloved fingers of my right, my face turned up and away over her head, I packed the paper in past the incisors and over the dorsum of the tongue and seated the bolus towards the oropharynx in one push, with the pressure I use to seat packing in a cavity at the end of a case, and was most of the way out when the bite came. Her teeth met paper and the tip of the outer glove, which is what the outer glove is for. A pinch at a time gags in a series. One push gags once, hard, and is spent. I had tipped her chin down, because breakfast at seven can come back at half past ten, and I waited. It stayed down. Then the paper did what it had done in a glass of water at my counter the week before, when I pressed it with the back of a spoon like a woman checking whether a cake was done. Dry, it was card. It drank her saliva weight for weight, card to felt to something like the skin on a pan of porridge. It smelled of toner as it wet, warm and faintly electrical, the smell of an office at the end of a long day.

Then the industrial tape. Industrial tape cannot be unrolled discreetly. It is the only tool in the kit that announces itself, a shriek coming off the roll that in that room went up the brick and stayed there. I shut her jaw over the bolus with the heel of my left hand and laid the free end on her cheek first, to anchor it, and drew the first pass across her mouth under my hand, and only then let the jaw go. Then I tipped her head forward with my palm on the occiput and went round: three turns, over the mouth and both nostrils and round the back of the skull, pewter, pulled snug. On the second turn it caught the fine hair at her nape. I felt it catch, and noted it, and did not solve it. I had a lot on.

My watch said 10:40.

First, nothing. She held still. Twenty seconds. Twenty-five. Thirty. It was discipline, the same muscle that had once taken a stammering child and made of her a woman whose pauses passed for thought, and I admired it out loud.

"That's very good," I said. Even now she was doing it by the book, as if holding still were a channel.

Then the body overruled her. The smell came first, the other sweat sharpening through the freesia, before the rest of her had been told. Then the first involuntary inspiration came against the closed airway, and the diaphragm pulled down on nothing, and the tape dimpled inward over the nares in two small soft pits, and the suprasternal notch drew in above the collar of her shirt, and the intercostal spaces sank between the ribs as though each rib were being tugged on a string from the inside. It isn't the lack of oxygen that does it. Oxygen falls quietly; you could faint of it at altitude and never notice. It is the carbon dioxide, building in the blood, that the brainstem cannot tolerate. The body is not afraid of having no air. It is afraid of its own exhaust. I have some sympathy with the brainstem. I have been somebody's exhaust.

Then the struggle. The zip-ties bit and knocked against the iron bracket, plastic on metal, a sound like someone trying a locked gate. Her heels went against the concrete together, scraping first, then drumming, the bound ankles lifting and coming down. A hum came up through the tape, a voice with nowhere to go, and went round the room and came back. Behind her back her fingers opened and closed on nothing. My own pulse had come up into the hinge of my jaw and was keeping time with her heels, and I let it. I checked each phase against my watch the way I would check a registrar's timings against the book, and each phase came when the book said it would, and the book was right.

Then the loss of consciousness, which you can see happen in the hands: they stop meaning anything. Then the convulsion, the whole of her going rigid against the housing, and the shaking came through the concrete into my knees, and I kept them where they were. Then the bladder, warm urine rising into the cold air, expected, logged. Then a stillness long enough to be mistaken for the end, and then the agonal gasps, spaced further and further apart, each one arriving after the last like someone remembering something halfway through a sentence and then losing it again. Above the pewter band her face was going dusky, plum going to slate. I'd have put money, unseen, on petechiae in the conjunctivae under the black, pinpricks of ink through blotting paper; she had fought hard enough to earn them. I have opened this from the inside dozens of times. I had always come in at the end of this. I was watching the outside of it now, from the beginning, in order.

I put two fingers on her carotid and counted it down. It slowed and thinned and went irregular, a few beats and a gap and a few beats, and I counted the gaps as well, and then there was nothing to count, and I kept my fingers there anyway until I was sure, and it was 10:47.

Then the quiet: the drip somewhere in the dark keeping its own slow time, the faint electrical whine of the lantern that I had not been able to hear over her, my own pulse loud inside the hood, a hundred and something, banging in my ears and in my jaw and in the soft tissue of my throat, my face hot, actually hot, in a room at eleven degrees, warm again for the first time since the door, and all of it arriving at once and staying, the way the sound had stayed, as if the room had been built to keep it for me. It goes on the sheet next to 10:47.

I have left something out. After the black went over her eyes, and before the paper went in, Diane began to say a word. The old hesitation caught it on its first syllable: once, a stress and a fall. Not the pause her staff had always taken for thoughtfulness. This one stuck. That was why her mouth was open. I put the paper in before the word arrived. I never heard what it was. I have heard the hesitation since.

* * *

I sat back on my heels on the concrete for nine minutes by my watch.

She was cooling at the rate I would have expected, which in the first hour is almost no rate at all. The core holds on a plateau before the curve begins to fall. Only the periphery goes early: the fingertips, the tip of the nose, the backs of the hands, giving their heat to a room at eleven degrees that had more need of it. I read cooling curves every working day, from the far end, from a thermometer and a liver and a clock, working backwards to a time nobody saw. I had never been present at the start of one. I sat and watched the first of it the way you watch the first minutes of a bath running, knowing exactly how long it will take and staying anyway.

My knees ached on the concrete. My fingers were stiff inside the nitrile. The heat had gone out of my face.

Then the tape. I peeled the industrial tape off slowly, the reverse shriek, a long tearing complaint going backwards round her skull. It took the fine hair from her nape with it and left a line of adhesive across both cheeks and round the back of the head, the colour of dishwater. I bagged the tape. Her jaw stayed open around the bolus. The paper showed at her lips, softened, bone-pale going to ash at the edges where it had wet through, and on her lower lip one fragment still carried part of a footer: LC-03 and then the cut. I left it.

I cut the zip-ties at the bracket, one per wrist, and bagged them. Her wrists came round into her lap with the ligature furrows already printed in them, pale troughs with a margin the pink of a skinned knee. I let the furrows stay. They were evidence of nothing I owned. The ties at her ankles, and the band of tape there, went into the bag with the others, and left their furrows where they had been.

Her jacket came open to the sternum. Under it the slate shirt, buttoned to the second button, warm still at the centre. The page was a fresh print of Lesson One; the master stays in the drawer. I set the page flat over the manubrium, in the midline, and put a single steel pin through the page and the shirt and the skin. Over bone the skin is thin, and it went in like a pin through a hem. The fabric didn't argue. Nothing flinched, and nothing came. Nothing ever does, at my end of it.

The red marker was the only new smell in the room. The solvent came up sharp and sweet in the cold, and after the urine it was almost festive. I held the cap in my teeth, lips shut on it, face up off the page. A cap in a glove touches everything the glove touches after; a cap in the mouth touches nothing. It tasted of solvent and somebody else's stationery cupboard. I drew the circle round THE GATEKEEPER the way I circle a finding for a registrar who has missed it: once, closed, no going round twice. The nib squeaked on the paper. Then I dropped the cap from my teeth into a glassine envelope and pocketed it. The campaign never used red. Red was for corrections.

The Evaluation was where I had typed it two years ago. CONFUSED PROCESS FOR PROTECTION. I read it against the woman who had asked about channels, then about her daughter, then had apologised for not keeping me safe. It held.

The paper I left where it was: in her mouth, and the few pieces that had fallen onto her jacket and the floor. That was policy. I was satisfied with where the number pointed, and I stopped thinking there.

As for the rest: it had gone well. The first lesson had gone very well, and the pleasure of it was the pleasure of the grade being handed back to the right person, in the right room, in full. I said that to myself in the dictation voice, and filed it, and I was confident about it, because I had decided to be.

My mouth had gone dry under the hood. Then it was watering. I wanted the first investigator to read the fragments. Whoever it would be, crouching where I was crouching, with a torch, reading counsel's number off a wet scrap on a dead woman's lip without touching it, because you don't touch it, and understanding, slowly. I wanted that. And I noticed, as a finding, that I wanted a lot of things just then. I wanted to take the black off and look at her eyes, and collect on the petechiae. I wanted to open her. Professional curiosity. I had a bet with myself about her liver, fifteen hundred grams, and I wanted to put it in the pan and read the scale. I wanted to sit there longer. None of the things I wanted was to leave.

I left the way I had come in, out by the steel door, and closed it on her. I was ravenous.


# Chapter 7

Nobody grades leaving. When it is done well there is nothing left to grade, so I graded it myself. I have told you I left; that was the short version. The long one took twenty minutes, item by item, and I had written it in my head two years before, while forty metres down the sumac spur the trail went on being a public footpath on a Sunday morning.

The station smelled different with her finished. Damp concrete still, and the iron of the pipework, but over them now the toner coming off the wet paper, and under everything the urine, which a body lets go at the end, and which I named and stepped round. Every contact leaves a trace. That is Locard's exchange principle, and it is also true of family Christmas, which nobody has ever successfully cleaned up after either. You do not get to leave no trace. You get to choose which ones.

What went with me, first. The bag was already half full: the industrial tape; the four zip-ties, cut at the bracket and at the ankles; the band of tape from her ankles. Zip-ties carry lot numbers, and a lot number is the loose end of a sleeve that anybody patient can pull. In after them went the prepaid that had rung for her, which had come over the sill in her hand, battery out, the battery zipped into a pocket of its own, because a phone in two pieces is not a phone, it is a paperweight with ambitions. My own prepaid and its earpiece. The head-torch. The clear sleeve the page had travelled in. The zip bag that had held the paper. The red marker, and the glassine envelope with its cap in it. The row along the far wall came up into the pack the way it had gone down, left to right: the ties I hadn't used, the vinyl with what was left on the roll.

Diane's phone was still in its foil by the lantern. I slid it out. The foil was kit, so the foil came with me; the phone went back where she had kept it, powered off, into the left inside pocket of her jacket, under the waxed cotton. A missing phone writes a robbery, and I was not going to have anybody else's story laid over mine. The last thing that screen had shown was her daughter, asking her to call after her walk. I knew that. I didn't turn it on to check.

Then the room I had built round her. I pocketed the wedge, untied the cord loop, took down the moving blanket and peeled the weatherstrip off the frame in one long curl, like the rind off an orange I had no intention of eating. The station went back to having a door. The sheeting came up last, from the far wall back towards the door, folded in on itself as I went, with whatever my overshoes had left on it inside. It stopped a stride short of the plinth. I had cut it to stop there on Saturday, so that she would be found on the station's own concrete and not on anything of mine. The blanket I rolled tight and strapped under the pack. A woman with a rolled blanket on her pack looks like a picnic, which is the least suspicious thing a person can be.

What stayed I can give you in one breath, because I had built it to be read in one. Diane on the concrete with her back to the pump housing. Two strips of black vinyl over her eyes. The page over the manubrium on its single pin, THE GATEKEEPER circled in red. The paper in her mouth, and the few pieces on her jacket and the floor, gone soft as felt where they were wet and ash-grey at the edges. Nothing else: no tie, no tape, no fibre of mine. Every page I had cut up for her had carried the number under its thin rule, the campaign's own filing aimed back at the people who did the filing, and I was very pleased with it. Numbers point both ways, though. A matter number tells you which matter, not whose, and that one ran, as it turned out, to a list with me on it. I should have asked. I have taken the mark off since.

Then the sweep. I laid the lantern on its side at the threshold, at my feet, so that its light went flat across the floor instead of down onto it. Overhead light forgives. Oblique light finds: a fibre stands up and throws a shadow ten times its length, a hair becomes a cable. I have watched scene examiners for a decade, and most of them hold the torch up like a candle at a vigil, pious and useless. I crouched and read the floor in strips from where I was. You don't walk back across a floor you have just read. Nothing stood up out of it but dust, and the dust was the station's.

I stood with the lantern and looked at her once more from the door, the way I look at a body laid out for a viewing before I let the family in. The page lay flat. The circle was closed. The vinyl was level, temple to temple. Presentation: distinction. I listened a moment longer than I needed to. The drip. The faint whine of the lantern. Nobody beginning anything. I logged the lingering as what it was, a cost to the schedule, forty seconds over, and turned the lantern off and put it in the pack.

Then my own trace, at the sill. Outer gloves first, the right one with the print of her incisors in its fingertip. Then the hood and the hair cover. Then the coverall, peeled down and rolled in on itself so that its outside would touch nothing again, and last the overshoes, one at a time, each foot coming over the sill as its overshoe came off, so that the first thing my trainers touched was grass. All of it into a bag, and that bag into another, with the small dry crackle of somebody unwrapping a sweet in church. Under it I was a Sunday walker in a fleece and trainers. The inner gloves stayed on. They would stay on until the car. It was twenty-five past eleven.

I drew the steel door to. My padlock had hung open on the hasp since before dawn; I lifted it off with a small chink and put it in the pack, and swung the hasp over its staple with nothing through it, a flat clack, iron on iron. A shut door with no lock on it. Nobody had looked at that door in years. It was the door I had clapped against on Saturday, three times, to learn what the trail could hear through it. Nothing.

The day outside was overcast and flat after the lantern's blue, the colour of the inside of a sink, and my eyes took a moment with it. Sumac, leaf litter going over, the green breath of the reservoir. The sweat from inside the coverall went cold on my neck. Down the spur to the marker post, the trail clear both ways, and nine hundred metres of service track to the lay-by where my car sat out of sight. Hers was in the south car park.

I walked at the pace of a woman with nowhere to be. That is a skill, and I am good at it. For a woman alone on a trail it is a costume with very few pieces and none of them optional: hands in the pockets, eyes on the middle distance, the gait of someone thinking about lunch. My hands were in my pockets because of what was on them. I was thinking about lunch. I had eaten nothing since Saturday, and my stomach was taking it up with management. I noted it as a fact about the schedule.

I was at the lay-by, the car in sight and the county road loud beyond the hedge, when a bell rang behind me. Two notes, the ding-ding of good manners. Air on my cheek before I had turned my head. Hi-vis, a back, a wheel. Gone down the track towards the road, past the MARCHETTI sign at the turn, not looking back. I saw no face. Nobody saw mine. Tyres on packed earth make almost no sound. The bike had come up behind me from the direction of the pump station, and I did not know how long it had been there. What it could give anybody who asked: a woman in a fleece, on foot, a slate-grey hatchback in a lay-by, and the time. I put it in the debit column. I took my pulse through the glove with the fleece cuff pulled down over both. Ninety-eight. I didn't care for it. I had not been alone out there for as long as I had believed.

* * *

I was home at a quarter past twelve. Procedure first. The bag inside the bag went where it goes, and the blanket with it, and I am not going to tell you where. The padlock went back to where I keep such things; the vinyl and the spare ties went back behind the panel in the desk. The trainers went into a bag of their own, the fleece and everything under it into the machine on the hottest wash it does, which is hot enough to set an egg. I had peeled the inner gloves off at the car, inside out, and they had ridden home in a pocket; they went into the bag last, before the bag went.

Then forty minutes in the shower, as hot as the boiler allows. Unscented soap. I scrubbed the way you scrub for theatre: the nails with the brush, then each finger as if it had four sides, which it does, the webs between, the backs of the hands, up the forearms to the elbow, the water running off the elbow and never back down onto the hands. Forty minutes is how long a surgical scrub would take if the surgeon had nothing to operate on afterwards and simply wanted to be clean. The pump station would not come out of my hair. Damp concrete gets into hair the way a cellar gets into a coat, and I washed it twice and the cellar stayed. The morgue has never come off my skin either, so this was only a new tenant, and I am used to tenants.

I checked for tears. I check for them the way I check a decedent for a pulse, which is to say as procedure, not as hope. Two fingers and a count. There were none.

I lay down on the bed in a towel, with my hair wet on the pillow, to let my heart rate come down. I gave it an hour. It was a quarter past one. When I opened my eyes the flat was dark, and the pillow was damp under my cheek, and the clock said twenty to two, and it was the next morning. Nobody had knocked. The building was as quiet as I had planned it, which is what a schedule sounds like when it holds.

I had slept through four o'clock. Diane called her mother every Sunday at four; she had told a trade magazine so. That call was now somebody else's problem to explain.

It was hunger that woke me. That had never happened before, and I noted it as new data. I had been ravenous at the pump-station door, and I had slept straight through ravenous for twelve hours, and that was the first thing all day I could not account for.

I cooked by the lamp in the cooker hood and nothing else. The marrow bones and the onglet were from the butcher's. I had bought them on Saturday. The oven went on as hot as it goes. There were three bones, beef femur, cut lengthwise into canoes by a man with a band saw and opinions, and I salted the cut faces and put them in to roast. The steak came out of the fridge to lose its chill on the board. I sliced shallots thin. I cut the heel off a sourdough loaf and toasted it. I did all of it standing, the way I plan.

At twenty to three I ate the marrow on the stool at the counter, and I am going to take my time over it, because I did. It came out of the oven sizzling at the edges and trembling in the middle, the yellow marrow gone from opaque to nearly clear, which is the adipocytes giving up their fat, cell by cell, in the heat, and I ran the handle of a teaspoon down the medullary cavity of a femur, where I had last seen marrow on a slide, and it came out of the bone in one soft glistening scoop that I laid on the toast and salted and covered with parsley and lemon and put in my mouth, and it was warm salted butter with a mineral note under it, like licking the spoon you have left in the stockpot, the parsley green and bitter against it, the lemon cutting through the fat like a door opened in a hot room. I did all three bones. I scraped the third with the spoon end until it squeaked.

At three I seared the onglet. The pan as hot as it would go, the fat spitting, the fridge humming to itself, and outside the building one car, a long way off, changing down for a light that nobody else was waiting at. A hard crust on both sides and rare in the middle. The shallots went into the butter left in the pan, and the butter went over the meat, and I sat on the stool and ate it with the rest of the bread to mop. The crust was salt and char. The middle was oxblood, faintly ferrous, with a liverish depth to it that nothing else on a cow has. Sear: distinction.

Onglet is the crura of the diaphragm, the two muscular pillars that anchor it to the spine, the muscle that does the breathing. I had watched one stop the morning before. I salted it. The French call it the butcher's steak, because the butcher kept it for himself.

Then, because I am a doctor, I gave the lecture. Fight or flight: adrenaline, a flood of it, topped up for hours. The liver turns out its glycogen and the muscles burn theirs, and when the emergency ends the body finds its stores gone and asks, quite reasonably, to have them back. Cortisol stays up behind the adrenaline like a creditor, breaking down protein for the liver to make new sugar from, which is gluconeogenesis, which is the body pawning the furniture. The literature is clear that the post-adrenergic body wants its glycogen back. The literature is silent on whether it wants bone marrow. I have read the literature. Adrenaline also produces a fine tremor of the hands, eight to twelve hertz, which is why people who have just had a fright can't get a key into a lock. All of that is true, and I said all of it to myself in the voice I use for families, and I gave the lecture a pass, which is a lower mark than I gave the steak.

The plate in front of me was clean. I had wiped it with the last of the bread. My fork was in my right hand. My left hand lay on the counter beside the plate, palm down, fingers slightly apart, and it was perfectly still. No eight hertz. No twelve. Nothing at all. I gave it a line in the dictation voice, the way I would a hand on my table. Left hand, at rest. No tremor observed.

* * *

I washed up. The marrow bones went into the pedal bin double-bagged, because that is what we do with bone at work, and I did it at half past three in my own kitchen out of pure habit, and if you can't find that funny I can't help you. Then the lamp off, and the window black until five.

At five I made tea, builder's, with milk, and drank it standing at the window, open a hand's width, with September lying on my wrists in the full dark. The tea was too strong and too hot, tannic enough to fur the teeth, and the first mouthful went down over a stomach that was still full and still warm from three o'clock, like pouring hot water onto a hot-water bottle. I was rested. I want that on the record as a finding: rested, for the first time in weeks, with nothing behind the eyes but the eyes.

I knew where she was. She would be in the cold room by now at four degrees, on a tray, with a tag on her toe in somebody's capitals, the lividity fixed low in her the way it settles in somebody who died sitting up. Eighteen hours, and a little more: rigor at its height, the jaw first of all, set round the paper as hard as if she had decided to keep it. Rigor mortis is the body's last attempt to hold a position. In the cold it holds longer, and then it lets go, a muscle at a time, in the order it came. It was my work, going on without me in a fridge across the city at exactly the rate I would have predicted, and I stood at the window and marked the accuracy of it, the way you check a clock that keeps good time and find that it has.

The light came up slate towards six. Down the street the sodium lights went out block by block, as if somebody were walking along switching them off by hand, and the first bus went by with nobody on it.

My phone buzzed on the counter at two minutes to six, loud on the hard surface as a dropped instrument. A news alert. The campaign had a statement out. *A senseless act. Our hearts are with Olivia and the whole Pell family. We ask for privacy at this time.* I read it twice. As condolence, it was a C-minus: it spelled grief correctly and had nothing in it a person had ever said aloud. As something else, it was very good. They had used her daughter's name in the second sentence. Somebody in that room knew exactly what they were doing, and I hoped it was someone I'd met.

I had not opened the War Room feed. It was there, Sunday night in that room, recorded and waiting, and I had not let myself look. I was saving it the way you save the last bite.

At four minutes past six the phone went again. Mara. *You up? Unexpected one in overnight. Woman, 50s, off the reservoir trail. Chief rang me at home.*

I knew whose body it was. I read the text twice, the second time for what it left out: no name, no tape, no page, no paper. Whoever had told her what was on the tray had not told her what had come in with it.

At eleven minutes past: *He's given it to me. Says he wants 'a face that's never been near politics'. You're senior reviewer. Hope that's ok??*

Nobody had opened her yet, and the building had already decided what kind of body she was, and chosen accordingly. A face that has never been near politics, which in this city narrows it down to the dead and Mara, and the dead were busy. I read the two question marks at the end of her message and put no mark of my own beside them.

I typed a reply and deleted it. I typed another, shorter, and deleted that. My thumb on the glass was steady both times, and in the blue of the screen I watched it be steady.

I hadn't been asked to take her. I would be in the room anyway, on the far side of the table, standing where Mara had stood last week, watching somebody else take apart a thing I had made.

I replied: *On my way.* I was already dressed. I had been dressed for an hour.


# Chapter 8

I had been nearer to her than this. On Monday morning, first case, half past eight, I stood on the far side of the table, which is the observer's side, where Mara had stood the week before, gowned and ungloved with my hands clasped behind my back like a woman in a gallery who has been told not to touch the frames. I had never stood at a table with my hands doing nothing. They were perfectly still. Mara had the near side and the knife. Diane lay on the steel under the overhead lights with her arms at her sides, and the suite smelled the way it always does, of chlorhexidine and cold iron under the roar of the extraction fan, and under that, faintly, absurdly, of freesia. A drugstore shampoo. Hair keeps a smell longer than a face keeps an expression. I have never found that in a textbook, and I have never once been wrong about it.

Most people have to die before they get a retrospective. I got mine on a Monday, from the other side of the table, and the subject was the one who'd had to die.

The campaign had got into the building before either of us. Through the office door, on my way in, the break-room television had been running it in captions with the sound down. *A senseless act. Our hearts are with Olivia and the whole Pell family. We ask for privacy at this time.* At six, on my phone, I had marked it C-minus as condolence. Read again in the captions, as work, it was something else. Three sentences, and not a word in them a lawyer could object to. The shortest one first, to stop the eye. The daughter's name set in the second, exactly where the eye lands once it has stopped. Privacy asked for in the third as if it were being offered. I knew whose syntax it was the way you know a colleague's sutures from across the room. Brandt. Pass with distinction.

Mara began at the outside, as I taught her, and I read along as a critic reads. What was elegant: there was no wasted bruise anywhere on her. None of the defence injuries a struggle writes over a body in a hurry. Nearly every mark on her was a mark I had meant. What was rushed: an abrasion about two centimetres across over the left zygoma, where her face had met the concrete at the capture, because I had wanted the tape on more than I had wanted her face off the floor. And at the wrists. Mara found the ligature furrows at both wrists and both ankles, and measured and photographed each one with the scale beside it, and the left was deeper. I had pulled the left tie one notch tighter than the right. I noted it the way I would note it in a junior. Not wrong. Not even. You can always tell the side someone started on.

"Margins sharp, no rebound. The restraints were removed after death," Mara said, into the dictaphone. "Nothing recovered at the scene. Nothing came in with the body. They were taken."

I listened to that the way you listen to a correct answer to a question you set yourself.

Then she lifted the black vinyl from the eyes. It came away from the left temple with the lashes in it, a dark fringe stuck along the adhesive like bristles left by a cheap brush, and behind it a residue on the lids and in the brows, tacky, gritted the colour of cement with dust from the pump-station floor. I had anticipated the lashes. I had not anticipated the residue. Mara worked it off with solvent in small circles, and each swab went into its own bag. I ran my list behind my back, on my own fingers: gloves, sleeves, hair. Nothing of mine in it. I ran it again anyway. Every pathologist in this office is on the elimination register, so that a trace of one of us on a body comes back with a name. Mine would come back with mine, and the only thing that would make it contamination and not evidence was that I was standing at this table, gowned. It is the only filing system in the building designed to find you, and for once I was exactly where it would look.

She found the adhesive line across the lower face, the colour of dishwater, and the torn hairs at the nape, and a band of residue at each ankle, where a tape had been and was no longer. About the face she said nothing. She photographed it. Then she took the paper out of the mouth with forceps, a little at a time, pulp-coloured and swollen with saliva, like bread left in the sink, and the forceps ticked on the steel tray between mouthfuls. Every fragment went into its own bag, sealed and initialled across the seal. On some of them, under a thin rule, the footer: LC-0311. A sheriff's investigator by the door signed for each bag as it left the tray, bound for the people who read paper. I approved of the chain of custody. I want that on the record as approval of the chain of custody.

Then the rest of the outside. Petechiae in the conjunctivae under the lids where the vinyl had been, pinpricks of ink through blotting paper, exactly where I had bet on them. The congestion of a face that has run out of air. Over the manubrium, a small puncture with no vital reaction round it at all: the pin had gone in after. The page itself had been taken at the scene and was in an evidence store somewhere, and I found that I missed it. Inside, briefly. Her Y was clean and her hands were quick. When she reflected the diaphragm I looked at the crura for slightly longer than the case required.

Then the stomach. She opened it along the greater curve and the smell came up, sour and sweet, and there in a thin residue, mostly gone on, were the blueberry skins, inky and split, with porridge the colour of wallpaper paste and the dark of coffee. I had assumed breakfast at about seven. Nothing in there argued with me. My mouth filled. It came all at once, under the tongue, the way it does when someone opens an oven in the next room, and I noted it as a fact about the hours: no food since three in the morning, and that was a long time ago.

Then the neck. She took it layer by layer, in a bloodless field, as you must, and found them at the upper border of the thyroid cartilage, one on each side. Two small bruises, symmetrical, each the size of a fingertip. The hyoid was intact. No more force had been used than was needed. They were the print of the thumb and first finger of my left hand. Behind my back, my left hand had let go of my right. The thumb and first finger stood apart at the width of a larynx and stayed there. I had not sent them. I noted it as I would have dictated it: left hand, posture of grip, no tremor observed.

Mara was quiet for a while. The fan went on. Then she said, carefully, as if setting something down that might spill, "Both sides, and nothing wasted. Whoever did this knew the anatomy. They knew exactly where to put their hands."

"Yes," I said. "It looks that way."

I said it the way you agree with a taxi driver about the weather. Underneath I was having a different morning. Someone had looked at my work, at the whole of it, and seen what it was. In fifteen years it was the only review I had ever had from someone qualified to give it.

She picked up the pen to write the finding on the worksheet, and stopped before the word. One beat, a stress and a fall, and I heard Diane in it, the hesitation in the pump station before the word I put the paper in front of and never got to hear. This time the word came. *Practised.* Mara wrote it down. It was in the file now. I was proud of her. I had a mark ready, and I didn't give it. Under her word, at the foot of the worksheet, was the reviewer's line, where my signature would go. I was in the file.

* * *

I ate standing up, because I was planning. Strong tea with milk, which I made and then forgot, so that it stewed to the colour of a saddle and came up bitter at the back of the tongue. Rye toast, dark and sour, with salted butter put on while it was still hot enough to take it, so that the butter went in rather than on. Two anchovies from a tin, laid across it, the oil running into the butter, salt on salt, the fish soft and faintly rotten in the way that only good things are allowed to be. Outside the window the light was going sodium. I was hungry again. I put it down to twelve hours of sleep and a day with nothing in it but one meal at three in the morning, which is arithmetic, and which I am very good at.

The key was on the ring with my car key. The drawer slid out with its long wooden sigh. The sleeve lives at the bottom, so I lifted everything above it out as a block, the three dossiers, the photographs, my signed agreement, and set it on the desk without letting the top one slide. I took Lesson Two out of the sleeve and read it under the lamp for an hour.

I am not going to give it to you yet. I will tell you only that it reads well under a lamp at night, that it is the most careful thing I have ever written, and that the Evaluation at the foot of it still held when I got there: *Mistook clarity for cruelty.* I read it the way you read a good case history, for the order in which it tells you things.

Then I took Diane's dossier off the top of the block and put it underneath his, finished, because the file I am working lives on top, squared to the knot in the drawer floor. I opened his beside the page.

It is two files, really, one laid on the other. The original is two years old: typed sheets, long-lens photographs, my pencil in the margins. Over it, later, in ballpoint, the additions: the divorce, the house sale that didn't happen, the records. At the back, held with a bulldog clip, the blind items he had planted about me three years ago. I didn't reread them. I know them by heart.

He was a good case. Mingus, Dolphy, Coltrane, on vinyl and nothing else. Tuesday nights at the Tabard Club, a private club, where he played the piano from nine to eleven for nobody in particular. Charcoal suits, every day, so that you would have to look at him to know which one. And the divorce, which he did not talk about. He didn't talk about it, which is how I knew it was the most important thing in the file. People talk about their kidneys right up until the kidneys are the problem.

There was a long-lens photograph of him leaving the Tabard on a Tuesday just after eleven, collar up, under the light over the door. I looked at his hands in it for longer than I needed to. One was in his coat pocket. The other was at his side with the fingers a little curled, the way hands are when they have been doing something for two hours and haven't finished being shaped by it.

*The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady* was on his list. I found it and played it through the clean laptop's speakers, and it came out small and tinny and somewhere else, a big band heard through a wall. I turned it off after a few minutes. It was like having a menu read to you when you're hungry. Accurate, and beside the point. The silence after it was better, and I sat in it with my feet cold on the floorboards and his file open in front of me.

I will tell you this straight, because it is a fact and I tell you the facts. I was interested in him in a way I had never been interested in Diane. Diane was a file. Brandt was a reader. He was the one who would read the page and see what it was doing. That was all. I moved on.

I weighed the two dossiers, one in each hand, the way I'd weigh a pair of kidneys. Diane's was thicker. Two years of Sundays will do that. His was lighter and more handled: the corners gone soft as felt, the pencil gone glossy where my thumb had been over it and over it, a shine on the graphite like the shine on a stair rail. I graded my own research. Pell: thorough. Brandt: thorough, and more than that. I gave his file the higher mark for care. I did not ask myself why I had taken more care. It was a grade, and I gave it.

Beside the photograph of him at the Tabard, in the margin, there was a note in pencil in my handwriting.

*the only one who'll understand it.*

I didn't remember writing it. I aged it the way I'd age a bruise. The hand was more upright than mine is now; I have leaned forward since. The graphite had smudged under the heel of a hand. Give me a section under the microscope and I can age a bruise to within a day or two. My own handwriting I could only get to within a year, which tells you which of the two I had spent more time studying. It was mine, and it was two years old, and I could not find the night I had written it.

* * *

The recording was from Sunday night. I hadn't watched it. At 11:52 on Sunday I had been asleep, nearly eleven hours into twelve, and when I woke I had cooked, and then I had dressed for work, and the laptop had stayed shut on the desk. I had saved it.

So it was late on Monday when I sat on the bed in the dark with the clean laptop warm on my thighs and a mug of tea going cold on the floor beside me, and opened it.

The bezel unit faces the table. Whenever anybody looks at the screen, they look into my lens, and the screen itself is behind me. I see the faces turn to it. I never see what is on it.

The timestamp in the corner opened at about 11:40. There were a dozen of them, not one in a chair, and every face was lit from below by a phone, blue, the way you light a face when you want to frighten a child with a story. The sound came through the laptop's speakers flattened and a fraction late, voices over the hum of the air system. In twelve minutes comms asked for a statement by six, counsel said four times that nobody should say anything, and somebody offered *activist* and somebody *lunatic*, without the anatomy to back either.

I graded the room as a body, by what it did and in what order. First denial: people saying it couldn't be her, while holding the phones that told them it was. Then noise: everybody talking to the person beside them and nobody listening, which is the room's equivalent of a racing pulse. Then the search for someone senior, heads lifting and turning towards the door, the way a body in shock pulls its blood in towards whatever it thinks will keep it alive. Twelve people, and between them the anatomical knowledge of a butcher's calendar. They did not know what they were looking at. They knew only that it was bad.

Then the photograph came up on the wall screen. I knew it from the faces. Every one of them lifted towards me at once, and stayed there, and I watched a dozen people read my page without being able to see it myself, which is the way, I suppose, that most writers get their reviews. And then the phones came up. They photographed the screen, at an angle, from where they stood, the way tourists photograph the Mona Lisa: not to see it, but to prove they were in the room with it. Nobody yet knew what it was. My page was being copied all over the room by people who thought they were keeping evidence. As a professional observation, I noted how fast the campaign leaked. It leaked the way a nicked femoral leaks: everywhere at once, and nobody in the room trained to press on it. I was pleased, and I put that under professional observation too.

Somebody said his name into a phone. After that the room held itself the way a room does when the person who decides is on his way.

He came in at 11:52 by the timestamp. Charcoal suit, no tie, which told me he had come from home. I switched to the unit under the credenza lip by the urn, low and close, to watch him take a coffee from it without looking at it, a hand that knew where the cup was, and then I switched back to the bezel. I checked the file against the man. Posture: upright, unhurried, the shoulders down. Hands: one round the cup, the other loose. The room: it rearranged itself around him without anybody moving very much, the way iron filings rearrange themselves when you bring the magnet in under the paper. He was exactly as I had written him. Everywhere the laptop wasn't, I had gone cold, and I did not stop the feed to do anything about it.

"Phone," he said. "Not the wall."

The wall went dark behind my lens. The glare went off every face at once, and after that they were lit by their phones alone. He took his out and looked at it. The bezel had him from slightly above. I could see his face. I could not see what was on his screen.

He stopped. No voluntary movement observed.

I timed it, because I time things. The coffee in his other hand. His thumb on the glass, not moving. No blink that I could find, and I went looking. I know exactly how long that page takes to read, because I wrote it: under a minute, read slowly, read twice if you are slow. Nobody reads it for two minutes. He stood in the middle of that room with a dozen people talking round him and did not move for two minutes and eleven seconds. The only separate sound in all of it was a phone set face down on the table somewhere behind him, a small *tock*, like a knuckle on wood.

Then someone off camera said, "Cam?"

I paused it before he answered.

I didn't know why he had gone still. I want to be exact about that, since I have been exact about everything else. I had no theory, and I didn't make one up. It was the first thing on the feed I couldn't read.

I rewound the feed and watched him go still again. Then once more. The third time, my own thumb had stopped on the trackpad, and I left it there.


# Interlude — Cam

He was the last one in. Eight minutes to midnight on a Sunday, the charcoal suit and no tie, which was the only sign that anyone had called him at home. The War Room was loud. A dozen people standing, nobody sitting, every face lit from below by a phone.

There was an urn by the door. He took a coffee from it without asking how long it had been there. Tepid. It tasted of the urn and the wax lining of the cup. He drank it anyway. The cup gave one hand something to hold while the room told him things.

Comms wanted a statement by six. Counsel wanted nobody to say anything. Both of them would get what they wanted, Cam thought. In that order.

Someone said *activist*. Someone said *lunatic*.

The photograph was up on the wall screen. Everyone was reading the page in it.

"Phone," he said. "Not the wall."

Someone killed the screen. He opened the channel on his own phone and looked.

Diane on a concrete floor. The pump station; he knew the walk. Black tape across her eyes, the dull black of a record sleeve. A printed page pinned to her chest, one word circled in marker. GATEKEEPER. Under it, in a typed line: *Confused process for protection.*

He read it once.

Fair mark. The thought came and he turned it face-down.

Then he looked at what nobody else in the room was looking at. Her mouth. Packed full of paper, the paper gone soft where it was wet. Something she was no longer using, stuffed with something nobody had ever been meant to read twice.

He put two fingers to the glass and spread them.

A serif face. A thin rule along the foot of a page. Half a number below the rule.

He knew the typeface. He had chosen it. Four years ago, for the template he had Diane use. Century Schoolbook, because it looked like a judge had written it.

His thumb stopped on the glass and stayed there.

The coffee went on cooling in his other hand. The air system hummed. Somewhere behind him a phone was set face-down on the conference table, a small separate sound, and somebody's room-service fries were going cold on a tray by the window, and the hotel carpet smelled the way hotel carpet smells at midnight, of everybody. He did not move.

"Cam?"

The deputy comms director. Wanting to know if it was political.

"Crank," he said. "Probably."

Then: "Who's in the channel."

Fourteen people. He asked who had posted it. A boy near the window put his hand half up. Theo Akers, junior digital. A contact at the county sheriff's office had sent it to campaign security. Security had sent it to Theo. Theo had thought everyone should see it.

Cam's thumb moved. It saved the image to his own phone. Then, as channel admin, it deleted the post. The order of those two things was not quite a decision. He noticed it afterwards, the way you notice you have already locked a door.

"Off your phones," he said. "All of it. Nobody has seen this."

Theo began to say something about the record. About keeping a record.

Cam looked at him until he stopped.

Counsel was standing at the glass with the city behind him, one hand flat on the window. He asked whether the paper in her mouth was anything. He asked it without looking at the photograph. Cam watched him not look.

"Paper," Cam said.

Counsel had drafted those agreements. He had never seen what they looked like torn up and wet. Cam thought about how many things had Diane's name on them. Some of them were his. He did not say that either.

He finished the coffee. Cold now. He dropped the cup in the bin by the urn. He put the phone in the inside breast pocket of his jacket, on the left side, where the page had been pinned on her, and he buttoned the charcoal jacket over it.

He did not tell anyone what he had recognised. Not counsel, who had written the agreements and would not know them in pieces. Not comms, who wanted a sentence by six. Not the Senator, who would be told in the morning that this was a tragedy, and would be handed a statement that said so. Standing in a room of fourteen people who all thought they were working on the same problem, he understood that he was already working alone, that he had begun the moment his thumb stopped on the glass, and that it did not feel like a decision at all. It felt like twenty years of habit, turned for the first time on the room he was standing in.


# Chapter 9

The deadbolt turned with the weight it always turns with, a quarter of resistance and then the soft, heavy give at the end, like a joint going back into its socket. I came in sideways with the grocery bag on my hip and the morgue still on my skin, and the flat smelled of itself. It was Wednesday, the third day after Diane, a little after half past six. Chlorine from the sink, where I had rinsed a cloth that morning. The lemons in the fruit bowl, beginning to think about turning. The dry, faintly scorched nothing of a radiator that has not yet been switched on for the year. And under that, so faint I could have talked myself out of it, something that was not mine. I own unscented soap and nothing else that smells of anything. I have spent fifteen years training my nose, and I am not going to waste it on lavender. I stood in my own hall with the door still open at my back and the street's air going past me into the flat, and by the time I had shut it I knew someone had been in.

I didn't go to the desk.

I went to the kitchen, and I kept the bag on my hip until I got there, and put it down on the counter. Eggs, a tin of anchovies, butter, a lemon, a loaf. I didn't switch on the overhead. The last of the day was coming through the window over the sink, the colour of weak tea, and it was enough. The knife block first: full, every handle at its own angle, the way I leave them. Then the fridge. The seal came away from the frame with that small wet kiss it gives. The butter's paper folded back where my thumb had folded it; the cling film tight over the half lemon. The milk a finger below the shoulder of the bottle, where I had left it, with this morning's dry white drip line on the neck. The kettle with the inch of water I had left in it, and not a millimetre more. Nothing opened. Nothing added. Nothing swapped.

Most women who come home to a burglary check the jewellery. I checked the butter. I'm a pathologist. I know where the jewellery is.

Then procedure, which is not instinct and doesn't pretend to be. The tablet had been in my work bag all day; since Monday it goes where I go. The clean laptop was shut on the desk, behind a password that exists only in my head, and its log had nobody asking for one. I went round the flat for the kind of thing I buy myself, the smoke alarm, the sockets, the lamp base, the underside of the desk, because I know what a button the size of a fingernail feels like under tape. I had fitted two. Nothing. He had come to read, not to listen.

Then the desk. I put the desk lamp on, and the smell was here, stronger, at about the height of a standing man's chest, where a man would stand to read an open drawer: on the brass pull, on the back of the chair. Dry, green, rooty, expensive, like a cellar somebody keeps well. The drawer was locked, and the key had been on my ring, in my coat pocket, all day, in a building where I am surrounded by people who cannot be burgled. He had not needed it, which I took personally. I bent and looked at the brass of the lock face under the lamp, the way I would look at the skin around an entry wound, and there was nothing. No scratches, no bright new crescents where a pick had slipped. Entry, competent. I went back and looked at the front door the same way: a clean face, a bolt that threw true. A key, or somebody better with a pick than I would have guessed. I left that open. I unlocked the drawer, and it came out on its runners with its long wooden sigh, and everything was there. The three dossiers. Under them, the photographs. Under the photographs, my signed agreement, with Diane's cover email clipped behind it. Under that, the clear sleeve, and in the sleeve the title page and all three pages, the working masters, soft at the corners. I pressed the panel at the back and it came away, and behind it the rest of the black vinyl and the bundle of zip-ties lay as I had left them, and I could not tell whether he had found them or not. It is the one thing in the flat I could not grade. A man who had found that panel would have put it back exactly as it was. So would a man who hadn't.

Here is the evidence. The dossier on top I square to a knot in the grain of the drawer's floor, a dark oval like a thumbprint in the walnut, the left edge of the folder on the knot, every time. I have done it every time for two years. Tonight the edge sat a quarter of an inch to the left of it. I measure bodies in centimetres and furniture in inches. Nobody has ever asked me why, and the furniture has never complained.

My hand went out over the folder to square it, and stopped, and stayed there, a little above the manila, the fingers slightly flexed. I noted that. Then I took the hand back.

That was the evidence. The kitchen was the tell, and the tell was mine. I had come into a flat a man had been in and checked the food before the files, before I had a reason to: whether he had touched anything I was going to put in my mouth. My body had been afraid of that before I had been afraid of anything. The fridge had told it back that he had not come to kill me. Not today.

The dossier on top was his. It had been on top since Monday night, when I read the Lesson Two page for an hour with his file open beside it, and put the file back last, on top, because it was the one I was working. The sleeve lies at the bottom, so a man who wants the pages has to lift the dossiers off as a block, and slide the sleeve out from under the photographs and my agreement, and put it all back afterwards, and the block had come back a quarter-inch left. A man who opens a drawer with three files in it reads his own first. In his, in the margin beside the photograph of him leaving the Tabard, there is a pencil note in my hand from two years ago. *the only one who'll understand it.* He had read that, in my handwriting, about himself. It is an odd thing, to have a note reach the man it was about, two years late, by burglary. I worked his visit backwards from the room, which is what I do for a living. He came in daylight, while I was at the table with someone else's lungs in my hands. He read everything. He took nothing, because taking is evidence and he removes evidence for a living. And he put it all back almost exactly, the way the bereaved tidy a room before the police come: lovingly, and a quarter-inch wrong. The quarter-inch was the size of a man whose hands were not quite as steady as he had thought they would be. Entry competent, restoration very nearly excellent, one error. I found the error because I have spent fourteen hundred cases finding the one thing somebody else put back wrong.

I had not eaten since a coffee at ten. Under all of it I was hungry, and I noted it, and left it on the counter with the eggs.

He had read the syllabus. He knew that Lesson One was done, because the whole of the thirty-first floor had seen it by Sunday midnight, and that two lessons were still to be set, and the three dossiers lying over the pages had told him whose names went on them: his, and Holt's. And under the dossiers, in the same drawer, on the campaign's paper, was my signature, in the blue ink they gave me, rounder than it is now.

Mine was a name to him now.

* * *

I cooked standing up, because I was planning. Four eggs, broken into a cold pan with far too much butter and stirred over the lowest flame the hob will hold, the curds coming slowly, the butter browning a little at the edge where the pan was hottest and smelling of hazelnuts. Two anchovies mashed into a spoonful of soft butter with the back of a fork until it was the colour of pale salmon gone to clay and the smell of it was salt and rot and the sea, which is the smell of every good thing that has ever been preserved. Toast, the anchovy butter going into it while it was too hot to hold. The eggs over that, barely set, trembling. The lemon zested over the lot, so the oil in the zest went up into my face. I ate it in instalments, off the plate in my hand, the pan pushed off the flame to the back of the hob, with the tablet propped against the fruit bowl, where it had stood for the debate, and I was very happy.

With the first plate I scrubbed back through the day's recording. The bezel unit had the table, wide; the unit under the credenza lip had the urn, from below, close. It took me a quarter of an hour to find the gap. At 12:58 he left the room. At 3:06 he came back, in the same charcoal suit, and went to the urn and drew a coffee and stood holding it, and did not drink it. Two hours and eight minutes. I can do my flat, at a careful walk, in two hours and eight minutes, if I allow for traffic and for reading.

After nine, I watched it live. The room was quieter than it had been on Sunday. Holt had given his statement on Diane in the afternoon, and the staff were drafting a vigil, which is a press release with candles. Brandt sat at the end of the conference table, not working, with his phone face-down by his hand, and I ate, and I watched him not make three phone calls, which is the most intimate thing I had seen a man do in years.

The first came to him. The security lead put a slip of paper by his elbow and said something I lost under the air system, and I heard *sheriff's office* and *liaison*. Brandt held the slip a long time. Then he folded it in half and put it in his inside pocket and did not dial. You can't call them. A name means telling them what the paper is, and the campaign cannot be seen to have made that paper, seven weeks out. Correct.

Holt came in at half past ten with his tie loosened, and the cold began in my forearms, on the volar surface, the way it did the night of the debate, and moved into the backs of my hands. I kept eating. I want you to know that was a choice. A junior staffer near the window, a young woman, had been crying on and off all evening, and he went to her first and put his hand on her shoulder and talked to her quietly, and he knew her parents' names. He has a good memory for the first names of people he is about to make into paperwork. He was kinder in that small room than anybody who works for him. Then he stopped by Brandt's chair and asked if there was anything he needed to know.

"No, sir. Statement landed."

Brandt watched him go. You can't tell him either. There is a section in Holt's dossier, in my drawer, for the men he has stopped knowing. I have watched him do it on footage, a man standing at his shoulder one month and gone from the next month's photographs, and never a raised voice. The moment he hears that somebody has been into the old files, the problem is not the somebody. The problem is the man who kept the files. Correct again.

The third was nearly midnight. He picked the phone up and scrolled, a long way, past everyone he liked, and his thumb stopped. I couldn't read the screen at that resolution and I didn't need to. I read the thumb. Then he put the phone down on its face on the table, a small separate tock, and took his hand off it, and put the hand inside his jacket, on the pocket with the folded slip in it, and left it there, flat. You don't know what I have. You don't know who I've told. A dead pathologist with a dossier somewhere you can't reach is worse than a live one. Full marks.

I have told no one. I don't own a safe-deposit box. I own a fruit bowl. He couldn't know that, and I found that I wanted to keep it from him the way you keep the end of a book from someone who is enjoying it.
