I filled the kettle while I thought, and made toast for the last of the eggs, which had gone cool and firm in the pan the way I like them second time round, and ate that too. It was more than I had meant to eat.

He would go home, after this, and put on a record. It was in his file. I had nothing to put on.

I had been read correctly, from across a city, by a man who had stood in my flat and handled my things, and I had read him back, and got it right, and watched him get me right in real time. Nobody had done that to me since I signed. Nobody had looked at me long enough.

On the tablet his hand was still flat on that pocket. He was going to come for me himself. Not with the police's permission, not with Holt's, not with a stranger's on a clean brief. He had walled himself in, on all three sides, with me. We were the same kind of alone.

The kettle clicked off. I made the tea strong with milk and stood holding the mug in both hands in the blue of the tablet, not drinking it, and something warm went through me, low and brief, and I put it down to the tea.

* * *

After one I put the desk lamp on and nothing else. The flat had gone properly cold. The radiators were still off, and the floorboards came up through my socks, and the lamp made a pool on the walnut the size of a dinner plate with the rest of the room standing back from it.

I unlocked the drawer and lifted the dossiers out the way he had, as a block, my fingers spread under the bottom file and my thumb flat on the top file, and set them on the desk without letting the top one slide. I slid the sleeve out from under the photographs and my agreement. The paper in it has gone soft at the corners the way paper goes when it has been handled for two years and never used. I took out Lesson Two and laid it under the lamp.

I read it as he would have read it, at this desk, in daylight, while I stood across the city under a fan with my hands in somebody else's chest. The redactions, square and black. The second person, coming at him off the page. *You see every room exactly as it is.* I heard it in his voice. I had only ever heard his voice through a button the size of a fingernail, flattened and a beat late, and I heard it anyway, because now I knew he had heard it in his own.

I asked myself whether today changed the Evaluation. I took the question seriously; when new evidence arrives you go back to the finding, and you don't go back to it hoping. I read the line at the foot of the page twice. It held. If anything it read better for his having read it.

"Addendum," I said to the flat, in the syllabus voice, which is the voice I use for the dictaphone with the warmth taken out. "The Architect has sat the paper early."

That was the polite version. The Architect was hunting the student. The curriculum had assumed I would set every lesson. It had made no allowance for a subject turning up in my flat, uninvited. He had broken into the staff room to read the marking scheme, and then put it back nearly where he found it, which in my experience is the difference between a cheat and a scholar.

I noticed I was not afraid. Whatever had sent me to the fridge at half past six had gone.

I went looking for it the way you go looking for an organ that should be there: the spleen, say, which can hide up under the ribs and which you have to put your hand in and feel for. I put my hand in. It wasn't there. What was there instead, in the place where fear should have been, was interest, heavy and firm, the way a congested spleen is when it has been taking in more than it was built for. Noted.

Under Lesson Two in the sleeve lay the third page. I didn't take it out. I didn't look at it. I only knew, with the lamp on the back of my hands, that he had read it this afternoon, and that he was now the second person alive who had.

The pen tray holds my red pen, the ordinary one, the one I use on the masters. Markers are for circling people. I uncapped the pen and the smell of it came up, faint, a little bitter, nothing like the solvent of the marker; it smelled of a school desk rather than a whiteboard. The last thing I had circled in red was pinned to a woman's chest. It felt almost wholesome to use red on paper nobody was wearing, the way red was designed to be used, by a teacher, at a desk, late, with a cold cup of tea at her elbow.

My hand was steady. I bent to the margin beside the subject line, and the nib went across the paper with a dry, small scratch, the only sound in the flat, and I wrote one word and a full stop.

*Excellent.*

I capped the pen and laid the page back in the sleeve, squared to its own edge, not to his. I thought about the lock, and about moving the sleeve somewhere he had not been, and I left both where they were. I slid the sleeve home under the photographs and my agreement and lowered the dossiers onto the stack as a block. Then I put the edge of his file to the knot, out of two years' habit, and moved it off again, and checked the distance against the width of my fingernail, the way you size a margin when there is no ruler to hand. I put my hand flat on the manila, on his name, and kept it there, and didn't move the folder at all. The tiredness came up through me all at once, from the cold soles of my feet, the first thing my body had asked me for all day that wasn't food. I took the hand away.

I left his dossier a quarter-inch to the left, where he had put it, and slept nine hours.


# Chapter 10

The locker room at twenty to seven smelled of floor disinfectant and the clean-laundry steam that comes off fresh scrubs, and I was half into mine, the top still folded on my knee, when I opened *The Cloakroom* on my phone.

I have read it every morning for three years. It is the anonymous political gossip site where the blind items about me ran, and I read it the way you press on an old fracture when the weather turns. Not because it helps. Because you want to know it's still there.

That Monday there was a new item, posted at six, and by its second sentence I knew whose hand it was.

A former campaign volunteer, it said, let go by Diane Pell last spring for "erratic conduct", had been posting about Pell for weeks under an anonymous account. Then "a source close to the investigation" said that whoever had killed her "knew anatomy". I know his sentences. The ones he wrote about me are clipped at the back of his file in my drawer: the passive verb that lets someone be let go without anyone doing the letting, the single phrase in quotation marks, put there to be repeated. He never tells you what a thing is. He sets it beside something else and lets you do the arithmetic, and the answer is his.

*Knew anatomy* was Mara's word. It was *practised*, carried out of the building in somebody's pocket and come out shorter and worse. Somebody had the file, and somebody had handed it to him. It was the first time anything in that file had been used against anybody, and it had not been used against me.

Under the item, a thumbnail of a woman's face, and a link to the account, run through a shortener. A shortened link is a door with somebody standing behind it, counting.

My thumb went to the link and stopped a few millimetres above the glass, which was warm from my palm. I watched it the way I'd watch a thumb on a table, flexor pollicis longus holding the distal phalanx bent over nothing, and noted it, and took it away. I put the phone in my locker with the link unopened and carried it through the day like a sweet in a pocket. I knew what the deferring was. I am a woman who eats the crust first.

The air conditioning had been turned down for autumn, and autumn hadn't come, and the back of my neck was damp before I had my hair pinned. My case was a man in his seventies who had come off a ladder clearing his gutters: a subdural haematoma, a crescent of clot the colour of blackcurrant jam lying between the dura and the brain like a letter slipped under a door. It came away in one piece when I lifted it, and I liked that, and I weighed it. The gutters, according to the report, were clear. He had finished. The tablet was in my bag in the locker, but I don't open the feed inside a county building, and I spent the afternoon wishing I could.

The flat that evening had every window open, and the night came in smelling of tarmac giving back the day's heat and of somebody's barbecue two streets over, charcoal and fat and something sticky, and I stood tearing bread off a loaf and salting a tomato over the sink and eating it out of my hand like an apple. I eat standing up when I plan.

I scrubbed back to the eight o'clock meeting and watched the War Room find the item. They were delighted. A crank volunteer is a gift five weeks out; somebody actually clapped. Comms was drafting *we do not comment on online speculation* aloud, trying the words in different orders, as if one order might say something. Brandt stood at the glass with his back to the city and said nothing. Surprise is a small piece of work on a face: frontalis lifts the brows, the jaw drops a few millimetres. Every face in that room did it once. His didn't.

Then the account, on the clean laptop, through a cached archive copy, never the live page. Its first post was dated Thursday 17 September, the day after he had stood at my desk and read my drawer. It was eleven days old, and he had been growing it the way you grow a sourdough starter: fed at the same hours, kept somewhere warm, and to be thrown out the moment it had done its job. The misspellings were placed like seasoning, never in a name. The grievance began small, how Diane Pell had once spoken to her, and grew louder in a curve I have seen in real files, who Diane was really working for, what they were all hiding, until it was the voice of a woman nobody would believe about anything. Nothing in it about paper. Nothing about anyone else. One woman, one grudge, one dead boss. That was the craft, and it was his old craft. He had built a woman whose accusations sounded like symptoms.

I gave it an A. I don't give A's to fiction.

I will tell you plainly what I felt. I was enjoying it. I want to be exact about where. Heat in the face, and along the forearms where they lay bare on the window ledge, and a quickening under the sternum that was not anxiety. I know what mine feels like. Somebody good had come looking for me, and it was the most interesting thing that had happened to me in three years. He had read me, and now he had written back.

Then the photograph, at full size on the clean machine. At first I took it for a composite, the kind of face a careful man uses because it belongs to nobody. Then I looked at it the way I look at faces for a living. The pores across the nose, open and real. A slight asymmetry at the alae, the left nostril a fraction higher. And through the lateral third of the left eyebrow a healed laceration, a fine pale line, with the hair growing back white along it. Nobody puts a scar like that on a composite. Composites are drawn by people who want you to remember them, and scars are given you by people who don't.

I ran a reverse image search on the clean machine, and it came back in seconds, which told me he had wanted it to. A public profile. A veterinary technician. A greyhound the colour of wet slate, asleep on a sofa with its legs folded like a deckchair. A sister's wedding. A birthday cake with candles reading 31.

The profile had a real face. It was a woman who looked like me: my jaw, my widow's peak, the parting on the same side, a few years younger. He had chosen her for that. Her name was Tessa Quinn.

* * *

By nine on Tuesday morning the internet had her name. He had left the image findable, and it had been found, and it moved the way infection moves through a ward, along the hands. I followed it between cases on my phone, from the public side only, where I was one of thousands of people looking and therefore no one.

Inside forty-eight hours: her home address, posted. Northgate Animal Hospital, where she worked, its phones flooded until it closed for a day. A man livestreaming from the clinic car park. *KILLER*, sprayed across her garage door. The police, asking her where she had been that Sunday. And a thread of strangers analysing her hands, with arrows, in a photograph of her holding a spaniel puppy, her palm under its chest and its hindquarters along her forearm, the way you are taught to carry anything that might wriggle. They had found a killer's grip in it, which is a thing you can do if you have never held a scalpel or a spaniel. I corrected their anatomy in my head, all of it, and could not stop.

Mid-morning the office's public information officer came down with a sheet of paper and a pen. One line: the office *does not comment on active investigations*. The Chief had approved it. He wanted Mara kept out of it, and his own name further out, so it would go out over mine, as the senior reviewer on Pell. I signed it. By the afternoon a wire story was quoting Dr. Iris Vale, the senior pathologist who reviewed the autopsy. The office does not release photographs of its pathologists. My name was in the news and my face was not. I was in the news for a sentence that said nothing, which is the most accurate thing ever printed about me.

That evening I braised a lamb shoulder, browned hard until the fat on its back went the colour of toffee and spat at me, then a head of garlic in its skins, rosemary, half a bottle of wine, the lid, the oven low, until the flat smelled of wine and resin and sweet lamb fat down to my hair. While it went I chopped the salsa verde, parsley and capers and anchovies and too much lemon, and the tablet stood against the fruit bowl with the War Room running live.

Later Theo Akers turned his laptop round to show the table that the volunteer was trending. Somebody cheered. Brandt said, "Don't share it." He said it without looking up, the way you'd tell a dog to leave something, and then he took out his own phone and watched it for a long time with nothing on his face at all. The feed was too coarse to give me his screen. I filled it in myself.

I narrated it for him, because somebody should. The bait only works if nobody cuts the line. Whoever moves to help her is the one who knows she didn't do it, and that is the one he wants. He wasn't watching Tessa Quinn. Nobody fishing watches the fly. He was watching the water around her, for a ring. Correct. Full marks.

And mine, since I owe you it. The tactical reason first: any contact, a call, a message, a word to the investigator, would be the rise to his fly, and I am not a trout. That is true. It isn't the reason. The reason is that Tessa Quinn is not on the syllabus. There are three names in my drawer. There is no fourth. I am telling you that the way I told it to myself, with the lamb in the oven and the lemon on my fingers, and it sounded very clean.

On Wednesday morning Mara stopped at my office door with her hair still in its cap and her arms folded across her scrubs.

"They're saying a vet tech," she said.

"I saw."

"It's my word. They've got my word." She came half a step in. "Iris, the adhesive alone..."

"Not in the corridor."

She stopped. She looked at me, and I let her look.

"It's still in the file," I said. "Where you put it. Go and wash up."

She went. I listened to her down the corridor until the doors took her.

I was in the suite until noon. At twenty past twelve I sat down at my desk with my lunch in its glass container, the cold lamb and the salsa verde in two compartments, and the message light on the desk phone was going. One new message, left at 11:38 a.m. I played it on the handset with the receiver against my ear and the container's lid in my other hand.

Forty-seven seconds. A woman's voice, close and compressed by the cheap line, with a breath taken before each sentence, audible, the intercostals doing work the words didn't need.

"Hi. My name's Tessa Quinn. I'm sorry. I'm sorry to call the office like this." She was looking for the pathologist in the news, the woman, because she hadn't written the name down, and the front desk had put her through to the name on the wire story. She had never met Diane Pell. She had never been to the reservoir. The police had asked her where she was that Sunday. There were men outside her house. She had thought that someone who actually knew what had happened could just say so. "I'd just like to talk to someone who knows. If you can." She said a number, slowly. And behind her the whole time, on a hard floor, a dog shifting its weight, the claws ticking as it settled.

There is a form for a call like that: the time, the number, what was said, and it goes to the investigator the same afternoon. I pressed 7 with the hand that held the lid, one finger free of it, and watched the finger do it. It was steady. The phone gave its small electronic chirp, and the automated voice told me, in the pleasant tone the county pays for, that the message had been moved to the archive. Everybody in county service knows that nothing in a public office is ever deleted, only moved to a folder nobody opens. I have never opened mine. I did not write the number down. I listened to it once. I deleted it.

I ate lunch. The lamb was cold and dense, and the fat had set white in its seams overnight and melted on my tongue as it warmed, slow and sweet and faintly of rosemary, and the salsa verde cut through it, sharp and green and briny with caper, the anchovy coming up underneath, salt and rot, and too much lemon, so that my jaw ached at the hinge with it. I took the fork from my desk drawer and ate all of it, sitting down. It was as good as it had been the night before.

* * *

By ten that night every window in the flat was open, and the heat sat in the rooms at the temperature of a proving drawer. Somebody down the street had a stereo on, music with a lot of horns in it that I couldn't name and didn't try to. I stood at the counter with the heel of the loaf and the end of the salsa verde in its jar, and I didn't eat them. I was thinking about machinery.

An account that regular is not run from a man's phone at a conference table. It is run from somewhere that keeps hours. Nine days earlier I had followed his car out of the hotel garage at eleven at night to a building in the warehouse district, and I knew where it stood and almost nothing about what was in it, and I would have liked very much to see inside it. I didn't go that night. I put the bread back in its bag. It was ten at night, I had eaten nothing since lunch, and I wasn't hungry. I am reporting it.

Detachment is the first principle of my trade, and the syllabus took it whole. You meet the body before you meet the name, and you do the findings before you read the history, because a history is a story somebody else wants you to believe, and the body has never once tried to win me over. Three names, and everybody else is history that isn't available. I had been very clear with myself about the number. It was the only thing in the syllabus I had never had to redraft.

I kept to it, mostly. I did not search for Tessa Quinn that night. There was a tab still open on the laptop from Monday, and I closed it without looking, though I looked long enough to know it was the greyhound. But I knew things, the way findings are there whether you go looking or not. The scar through the lateral third of the left eyebrow, and the hairs growing back white along it. The name of the clinic. Her age. The greyhound's name, which was Pilot. What colour her garage door had been under the paint. I knew the dog's name. I hadn't asked for it. Nobody ever asks me what I know, and I know everything anyway.

There is something here. I can see the outline of it the way you see a shadow on a scan before anyone has put a calliper to it, a difference in density, a place where bone-white goes a shade towards ash, and I am not going to measure it. I am telling you that I am not. Watch me not.

I unlocked the drawer. The brass key turned with its small oiled click and the drawer came out on its long wooden sigh, and there was his dossier on top, still a quarter-inch to the left of the knot, where he had put it and I had left it. I lifted the three files out as a block, fingers spread under the bottom one and thumb flat on the top, the way he had, and set them on the desk without letting his slide. I slid the sleeve out from under the photographs and my agreement and took out Lesson Two.

The paper had gone soft at the corners from two years of handling. *Excellent.* sat in the margin beside the subject line, in red, in my hand, where I had written it a fortnight before. The red pen stayed capped in the tray. I took the black pen from the tray and uncapped it, and it smelled of what it was, ink, faint, and under that, on my own fingers, lamb fat. I had washed my hands twice since lunch. I could still smell it.

Below *Excellent.* I wrote *Tessa Quinn*, in the small upright hand I use for case numbers, so that it could be read by anyone and mistaken by no one. The reason was administrative. A name that isn't written down goes on following you about. Once it is written, it is filed.

A car alarm started up a couple of streets away and went on for a while, and stopped on its own, without anybody coming. Most of my patients went the same way.

I drew his dossier to the edge of the page and laid my left palm flat on it, on his name, and held it down. My hands were as steady as they are over a body. I had never struck through a name before. I set the nib at the start of her name and drew it along the manila edge to the end, one stroke, without lifting, the nib dragging very slightly on paper two years soft, and the line came out straight and level.

I capped the pen. I put Lesson Two back in the sleeve, squared to its own edge, and the sleeve back where it lived, and I lowered the files onto the stack as a block, and set his a quarter-inch left of the knot, and checked it against my fingernail. Then I closed the drawer and locked it.

I wasn't tired. I went to bed anyway and lay on top of the sheet with the windows open and the heat on my skin, and did not sleep nine hours, or anything like it. Late, the stereo down the street stopped. In the quiet it left I found I had something by heart that I had never tried to learn. Wednesday 30 September. 11:38 a.m. Forty-seven seconds. I had deleted the message and kept the time.


# Chapter 11

At twenty to eight on a Thursday morning I was eating a char siu bun in my car, across a service road from a building that did not want to be looked at, and I was looking at it.

It was single-storey, brick to the height of a man's hip and ribbed steel above that, with no sign over the door and a leasing agent's board wired to the chain-link fence, which is how a building tells you it belongs to nobody. I had the window down two inches. Smell first. The condensers on the roof were breathing out across the road, a dry, steady exhalation of hot dust and the faint ozone that electronics give off when they have been working all night, the smell of the inside of a hairdryer. Under that, diesel, from the generator cage. Under that, wet cardboard, from the bins of the unit next door, which had been rained on some time in September and had never got over it. The condensers roared, and they would be roaring when I was dead, because whatever was in that room was never allowed to sleep, and was kept cool the way you cool soup, by blowing on it, for ever.

The bun was from the bakery on the arterial corner. I want to be exact about it, because I always am about what I eat on a job. The dough had been steamed so soft that my thumb left a print in it that stayed, faintly sweet, with the yeasty breath of something that had been proving since four; and inside, the pork was cut small and lacquered in its own glaze, honey and soy and five-spice gone dark and sticky as varnish, so that every bite pulled a thread of it across my lip. I had told the office I had a morning appointment and would be in by ten. It was true. This was the appointment.

I had been doing to him what he did to me. Ten days earlier I had followed his car out of the hotel garage at eleven at night to this street, and watched him badge in at the side door and stay forty minutes. I came back on the twenty-fourth, by daylight, and watched from here. That morning it was paperwork, which is my medium.

I wiped my fingers on the napkin, one at a time, before I touched the keys. The clean laptop was tethered to a prepaid hotspot; my own phone was at home in its foil sleeve, where it would stay until the night's work was done, and where it had spent more nights that month than I had. The leasing board named only the managing agent. But a man can hide a great many things from a great many people, and nobody on earth can hide a diesel generator from a permit office. The city's register of permits named the occupier: Lantern Data Services LLC. The state's register of companies did the rest. Lantern Data Services was wholly owned by Ostler Holdings LLC. Ostler Holdings was owned by Brightwater Row Partners LLC. Brightwater Row was owned by Fenwick Lane Holdings LLC. Three holding companies deep. Three layers, like the meninges: the dura, which is tough and which everybody respects, the arachnoid, which is cobweb and which nobody notices, and the pia, which sticks to the brain so closely that you cannot lift it off without taking a little of the brain with it. None of them is difficult if you know where the seams are. I gave the structure a good mark. It was the work of a man who had read the anatomy and assumed nobody else had.

The badge was already in my tote. At twenty past seven, at the pickup end of the bakery counter, I had stood beside a man in a hi-vis vest with his badge on a reel clip at his hip, level with my tote, as I had on the twenty-fourth, and while he waited the reader in the tote took his card's number the way a swab takes a cheek. He bought two coffees. He bought the second one for someone. I noted it and let it go.

At five past eight the climate contractor's white van pulled in at the kerb, as it does every Thursday, and my man with the two coffees went to the side door, touched his badge to the reader, and in the glass vestibule beyond it keyed a code into a panel on the wall. The binoculars are the ones I bought for herons. They do not resolve digits through two panes of glass and a road. They resolve a hand. His went straight down the middle of the keypad, top to bottom, four presses and done: the midline. People choose incisions the way I would, if you let them. The line of least resistance. The one that heals.

At a quarter past eight the operator arrived, mid-thirties, backpack, no breakfast, and badged in at the side door, as he had on the twenty-fourth. No guard. No second car. A camera over the door and, presumably, something inside, and not one living person watching either. Brandt had built a room that watched itself, and I admired it the way you admire a well-made bone saw: there is not much to say about it except that it does exactly one thing.

I went back to the register for the top of the chain. Fenwick Lane Holdings had filed its annual report eleven months before. One officer. A signature at the foot of the form, and I saw the signature before I read the name, because I look at the hand before I look at anything a hand has written. It had no hesitation in it. It was a signature that had never once in its life wondered whether it was allowed. I knew it. I had sat across a desk from that hand for forty minutes three years ago while it watched mine, and it had countersigned the agreement in my drawer.

Officer of record: Diane Pell.

The Gatekeeper had kept the Architect's door. She was the pia. She was the one that sticks.

I laughed out loud, alone, in a parked car, with my mouth full of pork bun. It is very hard to laugh with a mouthful of pork bun, and I recommend it. It came up from the diaphragm in hard involuntary contractions, the way a hiccup does, and sprayed crumbs on the dashboard, and my eyes ran, and I went on laughing until I had to put the laptop on the passenger seat so as not to drop it.

I checked the other three while I was still wiping my eyes. Eighteen days dead, Diane Pell was the officer of record of four companies, which is more than most people manage alive. The register had not been told. I knew which of those two facts was on file where.

* * *

I left the office at five, and was back across the service road by ten to six, and at three minutes past six the operator came out with his backpack and the building went on roaring to nobody. Then I moved the car two streets away and waited for it to be dark enough.

At five past nine I tethered the tablet and opened the feed. The War Room, thirty-one floors up across the city: Brandt at the glass corner table with his jacket on and his phone out, the posture of a man who is not going anywhere yet. I watched him for longer than I needed to. Once I was through that door I would not be able to see him, and that is the only part of a job like this that I dislike, leaving the man I am working on in the car.

I had budgeted forty minutes. I laid out the kit on the passenger seat the way I lay out a tray. Workwear from a trade-supply shop, matching the contractor's: a hi-vis vest and a cap, with no logo I would have had to explain. Nitrile gloves. The cloned card. A foil survival blanket, folded flat. The head torch with its oxblood filter. A tension wrench and a pick. A blade and a roll of black vinyl. Glassine envelopes. The tap, in a padded pouch. The tablet stayed in the car.

At twenty to ten I walked in with my head down and the brim of the cap to the door camera, which was the only camera I knew of. To the camera I was a cap with a vest under it, which is all a contractor ever is to anybody. The cloned card turned the reader green on the first try. In the vestibule I keyed the midline into the panel, two, five, eight, zero, and the panel gave a small polite tone and accepted me. Breaking and entering is mostly entering. In my trade the breaking is done by the saw, and nobody has ever thanked the saw.

Heat came out of the hall to meet me, the way it comes out of an oven you have opened to baste something: close, dry air pushed out of the backs of the racks, warm metal, a burnt-dust note like a radiator switched on for the first time in autumn, and the sweetish plastic of new cable. Then the sound, which was not a sound but a condition, fans in their hundreds, a wall of white noise so dense you could have leaned a ladder on it. It was a room you could scream in all night and be filed as a cooling fault. I noted that and moved on. In the hot aisle it was near thirty degrees and inside a minute I was sweating under the vest, at the collar and down the groove of my spine. It was the warmest room I had ever been in where I was not allowed to be, and I liked it.

The ceiling lights were the kind that wake when something moves. In the oxblood of the torch I could see the sensor lenses up among the cable trays, small and pale and domed, like the eyes of something blind that had been very carefully placed, and I read them as a floor plan: every lens a cone pointed down, every cone a place I would not stand. I read the trays for cameras too, the smoked domes that live at the ends of rows, and found none. He had paid for a room that noticed movement and not one that remembered faces. I walked the gaps between the cones, left, straight, left, along the backs of the racks. The ceiling stayed dark.

I was enjoying myself. I want that on the record in so many words, without any help from adrenaline, which I have blamed for a great deal and which was not, that night, involved. The last time I had enjoyed a room this much there was a liver in my hands.

At the end of the hall a glass-fronted office looked back over the racks: the room the operator ran. Above its doorway a second sensor was mounted on the ceiling, a white box with its own small light, its lens cut to hang a curtain down over the threshold and the first three metres of floor. A second lock on a second door. The room inside the room. I gave Brandt very high marks for that. I knew the kind. It counts: one pulse is a maybe, and it wants a second inside a few seconds before it believes anything. There was no way in but through its curtain, so I stepped into the doorway and let it have its maybe, and the little light flickered amber, once.

I stopped mid-stride, with my weight on the ball of my right foot and the heel not yet down. I held it. Soleus, the slow postural one, will hold an ankle in plantar flexion for a long while; gastrocnemius, which is built for sprinting and showing off, will not, and it was gastrocnemius that began to burn, and I counted, the way I count a pulse. My own was up, and I filed it as a finding, and the finding was appetite, not fear. The flicker stopped. Nothing sounded.

A sensor like that does not see a body. It sees change: something warm crossing something cooler. So I gave it no change. I let the heel down a millimetre at a time and, very slowly, shook out the foil blanket, the phone sleeve's big sister, and its crackle was the loudest thing I did all night, and the fans ate it. I held it up in front of me at arm's length, the way we hold the sheet at a viewing, and I crossed the office at the speed of the slowest thing I know, which is a body cooling. I have timed a great many people losing a degree an hour. I could go slower than that. It took me four minutes to cross three metres. The little light stayed dark.

Sight last, and all of it past the edge of the curtain, in the sensor's blind spot. One desk, one chair. Two monitors, dark, giving back the racks' green and amber pinpricks and nothing else. A keyboard. A mug with a ring of cold coffee drying in the bottom, for which I docked the operator. And against the wall a gunmetal four-drawer steel filing cabinet, with a wafer lock of the kind they put on cabinets so that nobody feels foolish for not locking them. The wrench and the pick had it open in under a minute.

I opened every drawer, because I am thorough. The top three held the shell company's life: incorporation papers; annual reports, Diane's signature at the foot of each; equipment warranties and service contracts. The bottom drawer held the building's own paper, the lease, the insurer's schedule, the fire certificate, and, hung between the lease and the fire certificate, one manila folder with a name typed on its tab.

Mine. A printed file, three years old: the campaign's dossier on Iris Vale. Clipped inside the front cover, a colour photograph taken with a long lens. My face, outside my own building, younger.

* * *

I closed the folder before I got anywhere near the complaint. The job came first. I put the file on the desk, squared to the edge, and I was a little proud of that, in the way you are proud of not eating the second biscuit until the work is done.

Back out under the foil, and along the gaps between the cones to the rack where the building's outside line came in. The tap was the size of a pack of cards. The line in from the street was no use to me; everything on it travels sealed. I went past the box where the seal comes off, to the uplink on its inside, behind the cable management, where the traffic runs bare, the way the vessels do once you are through the capsule. Seating it inline meant cutting the link, and I counted while it was cut: seven seconds. Buildings drop seconds every night and nobody writes them down; I let mine go into the noise. Its lead went into a USB port on the rack's power strip, and its light came on and went off again. It keeps what it hears on a card, for collecting by hand, and sends out only a thin summary and a heartbeat inside the building's own traffic, like a pulse taken through somebody else's wrist. I had read its manual the way I read a toxicology report, and fitted it three times on my kitchen table.

He would know someone had been here, because I was about to tell him. He would sweep the place for a transmitter and find nothing with an aerial. And he could not call the police about a break-in at a building he kept whatever he kept in, owned three companies up by a woman eighteen days dead whose autopsy I had signed as reviewer. He could not tell Holt without explaining the building. The three walls were still standing, and I was standing behind them.

Back through the doorway under the foil. At the desk I cut a single rectangle off the black vinyl, the length of a pair of eyes, with one clean snick of the blade, and laid it on the desk in front of the keyboard, squared to the edge. No page. No marker. A redaction bar with nothing under it. I meant it to say only that I had been. It is what a student leaves, when she has read the teacher's file.

Then I graded the file, because I cannot not. My address, the same flat. My salary band. My height, two centimetres out, and everything else right, which is a better mark than most of my residents got. A pencilled column of my movements over two weeks, home, work, the butcher, home. A printout of the blind item that had ended my credibility, with edits in the margin in Brandt's handwriting, which I knew from his dossier. A typed page headed with a summary of my complaint, which I turned over without reading. I know how it ends. I was there for the ending. And a routing slip, in Diane's hand: *Reviewed. Contained. D.P.* I gave it a pass. It was accurate. I had been filed between the lease and the fire certificate. I had been assessed as a fire risk. I had been assessed correctly.

The photograph last, because I look after I know. A six-by-four print, long lens, a little soft. A woman coming out of my building's front door with a paper bag of groceries in the crook of one arm, celery leaves over the top of it, her hair longer than I have worn it since, in a coat I gave away. On the back, in pencil, a date in October three years ago, and a reference number. The paperwork had a number for my face.

She looked more like Tessa Quinn than I look now. He hadn't matched Tessa to me. He had matched her to this. I am telling you that I saw it, and I am telling you I am not going to think about it.

I unclipped the photograph and slid it into a glassine envelope from my pocket, and left the rest.

The paperclip stayed on the folder, and the folder went back between the lease and the fire certificate, and the plunger of the lock went home. Out under the foil and along the gaps, the midline keyed in to arm the panel, and the side door closed behind me at twenty-one minutes past ten. Forty minutes and change.

Two streets away, in the car, I didn't start the engine. On the tablet Brandt was still at the War Room table with his jacket on, knowing nothing yet. I watched him for a minute. Then I turned the screen face-down on the passenger seat. The sweat was drying on my back under the vest, a long slow evaporation, and the condensers were a faint roar two streets off and then I stopped hearing them. I stripped the gloves off inside out. I was hungry, in the old, frank way, in the stomach and the back of the jaw, and I held it the way I had held the step.

I took the photograph out of the envelope by the edges, the way I hold evidence, and bare-handed, the way I never do. The satin of it under my fingertips was the first thing I had touched without a glove since I pulled the gloves on at twenty to ten. The street lamp was sodium, and it made the colours of three years ago go to tea and ash. I looked at her for a long time. I knew the hour was passing, and I let it.

I didn't recognise her either.

In the bottom drawer of a dead woman's cabinet, under a paperclip that was holding nothing, the space where my face had been.


# Chapter 12

It was a little after ten, the side door to the loading bay was twelve metres ahead of me, and in the inside pocket of my jacket was a sliver of plastic smaller than a fingernail with a day of Brandt's traffic on it. I had chosen a Friday because he was at a donor dinner, being photographed with a fork in his hand by a room full of people who would swear to it. I was pleased with that. It is the kind of thing I am pleased with.

The hall roared. It is a room built to keep something cold that would otherwise spoil, and like every room of that kind it never stops talking about it. It was louder than the morgue, a noise I felt in my back teeth, and under the roar the cold aisle breathed up through the grilles set in the painted concrete and into my trouser legs, a long way from the soft early-October night I had walked in through. Ozone. Warm dust from the hot aisles: last night's smell, and I knew it now. The racks stood in their rows with their status lights going jade and amber, and the ceiling stayed dark, because I had kept to the route I had mapped the night before, where the motion sensors don't look. I had keyed the midline into the panel on the way in, as before.

I wrote up the exit while I walked it, because I cannot walk anywhere without writing it up. Card out, new card in, tap left seated on the uplink, no light disturbed. Route clean. Time well under budget.

The bay smelled wrong before it looked wrong. Cigarette smoke, recent, stubbed out half-smoked by someone who had been told to stop. A menthol lozenge over it, sucked to hide the cigarette. And over both, cheap cologne gone flat on warm skin, the smell of a man who had put it on hours ago for a short job and had been waiting ever since. Then the van: white, panelled, backed up to the roller door with its side door open and the yard's sodium lamp coming through the gap, the colour of weak tea on the concrete. Nobody in the cab. It had not been there on the way in.

Two men stood in the dark at either side of the door. Not police. Police announce themselves; it is the one courtesy the job requires, and these two were working hard at not making a sound, which in that room was a waste of effort. The older one was heavy and bearded and stood with his weight forward and his knees soft, as if he had done this before and been paid properly for it. The younger one carried his weight on the forefoot, heels unloaded, with a lock-knife clipped to his belt and his hand a careful distance from it.

I read them the way I read a tray laid out before a procedure. Zip-ties, a loose bundle looped at each man's belt. A black cloth hood in the young one's left hand, hanging like a dead bird. No firearm drawn, none printing through either jacket. The van open behind them. Nobody brings a hood to a killing; the dead are never in any danger of seeing where they're going. Take her. Don't damage her. Find out what she has and whom she's told. It was the third of his walls, the one about no clean kills, and he had put it on two strangers like a pair of coveralls and sent it to stand in his own loading bay.

I had thought he would come himself. I had got as far as wondering what he would wear. He had sent hands instead and stayed at his dinner, and I felt it the way you feel being stood up, briefly and low in the stomach, and then as information. The rest I worked out standing there, and could not check any of it. I had not risen to Tessa Quinn, so he had stopped fishing and sent men in to wade. The rectangle of black tape I had left squared on the operator's desk would have told him I was coming back, when I had meant it to say only that I had been: two men parked nearby since morning, then my cloned card turning his reader green, then their phone. The syllabus had taught; the student had learned. I gave him the mark for that.

They could not afford to hurt me badly, because a woman who cannot talk is no use to a man who needs to know what she knows. I could afford anything at all. I liked that asymmetry very much. I could have called it an advantage. I didn't bother. I felt my pulse come up and my face go warm, and I wrote those down as findings. My hands were in nitrile, and steady. The scalpel was sheathed in the inner sleeve pocket of my jacket, against my left forearm. I did not touch it. I noted that I was not touching it.

The bearded one put his hands up, palms out, the gesture men use on horses and on women in car parks. He took one step towards me.

"Easy," he said.

And then he said my name. Not Dr. Vale, which is how the wire stories say it, and the paperwork, and every stranger who has ever been handed a file with me in it. He said, "Iris." It is the name a man uses when someone has given it to him on purpose. Brandt had chosen it, and put it in a stranger's mouth, in the dark, across the city from his dinner.

* * *

My own sweat, coming sharp out of my collar under the cold air. Then nothing for a while that I had time to smell.

The young one came in from my left with the hood held open in both hands. The bearded one was behind me before I had finished turning, quick for his weight, and locked his forearms across my sternum with my humeri pinned to my sides, and lifted me off my heels. His breath was menthol. My feet were off the concrete and somebody had told him I would scream.

I didn't. I let my whole weight go dead, which is a thing I know a great deal about, and when my heels came back to the floor I drove the right one down onto the top of his foot, onto the first metatarsal, where the bone lies close under the skin, and his weight came off that foot. Then I threw the back of my skull into his face and felt the nasal bones go, a crunch like stepping on a snail in the dark, and his arms opened.

I am not a fighter. I had never been in a fight. I am someone who knows where everything is.

The scalpel was in my right hand before I had decided it should be. The young one was inside my reach with the hood still in his left fist, and he had done the thing his brief told him not to do. The lock-knife was open in his right.

He put it into my left flank as I turned, and he pulled the thrust, because somebody had told him not to kill me, and I had known he would. I narrated it as it went in. Skin. Subcutaneous fat, which offers nothing. External oblique, its fibres running down and forward like hands into pockets, cut across their grain. Internal oblique under it, fibres the other way. Then the bite of transversus abdominis, and the blade stopped. I felt where it stopped and knew the layer, because the depth was his choice and I had read him making it: below the tip of the twelfth rib, forward of the kidney, a layer short of the colon. A knife in the left flank has four things to worry about: the kidney, the colon, the spleen and your opinion of yourself. He got none of them. *I'll live,* I thought, in the dictation voice, and I was delighted.

I have read fourteen hundred wounds after the fact. I had never had one read to me as it happened, by the nerve endings, in the first person.

I cut across the volar surface of his right wrist, one stroke, from just inside the radial artery to just short of the ulnar artery, through the superficial and deep flexor tendons and the median nerve, and his hand forgot how to hold anything. The knife rang on the concrete like a dropped spoon. I took the tendons and left him both arteries, on purpose. It is the most considerate thing I have ever done for a man who had just stabbed me. Put it on my file.

We had come off the bay and onto the floor between the first racks, and the sensors found us. The lights came on overhead with a clack, one row and then the next and the next, LED the colour of a fridge interior on painted concrete and steel and the three of us. I have spent fifteen years in rooms like it. It had felt, the whole time, like the morgue with the lights on, and now the lights were on. It felt like work, and I have always loved my work, and it was the most fun I had had in three years. I am telling you that the way I would tell you the time.

The bearded one came back with his face streaming and the brief gone out of him. He was angry, and anger is heavy and slow and announces itself. Blood from his nose had soaked his beard into a single oxblood tuft. He came in low, the sweat of the effort standing on his forehead, and got his arms round my waist, and we went down together onto the painted concrete, and my flank tore as I landed, a hot bright rip that I filed under *later*. He was heavier. I was better informed. I let his weight carry us over, because that was where it wanted to go, and came up on top of him with my knee in the soft angle under his ribs and my left hand flat on his chest through his shirt and the scalpel in my right.

The young one was against a rack upright with his good hand clamped round his wrist and his eyes on the lights. The bearded one was pinned and breathing wet through the wreck of his nose, his heart going under my left palm through the shirt like someone knocking to be let out. My knee told me he could not get up. My palm told me he was not going to try.

I held it for one breath. He was the one who had said my name.

Then I opened the side of his neck. Anterior triangle, at the front edge of sternocleidomastoid, one deep stroke through the common carotid and the jugular beside it, the arterial wall resisting the blade for an instant like a wet tendon and then not. The smell came first, opened blood, iron in the cold air, straight through his menthol and the flat cologne. Under the fans, close enough to hear it, his breath changed: a click, a gargle. The spray came up warm across my face and down my front before I had my hand back, in pulses against my skin, one to each beat of his heart. It got into my mouth: salt, and the taste of the inside of my own cheek when I bite it, iron over the iron from his nose. My own pulse was going faster than his and my face was hot under his blood, and I liked it. That is the whole finding. I stepped back off him and took both hands away. I counted by ear, the wet breath under the fans and then the gaps in it. Unconscious inside fifteen seconds. Gone in about three minutes. I enjoyed the counting, all of it, and the fans went on as if they had not noticed, which they hadn't.

Only then did I look. He lay on his back under the LEDs, his beard soaked to the jaw, the jugular's liver-coloured welling slowed at his neck, the blood fanned across the painted concrete to the nearest grille and running into it, and I stood over him with my face wet and wanted to keep looking.

There was no page for him. I had not planned him. Nothing to pin, no role to circle in red. He was the first thing I had done that was not on the syllabus, and the first thing I had ever done for no reason except that I could.

* * *

The card was still in my inside pocket. I checked it before I checked myself.

I folded the young one's cut wrist into his armpit and closed his arm down on it, then zip-tied him to the rack upright with his own ties, one round his good wrist and one round the post, and he let me. He was alive, bleeding brisk and venous and survivable, and he would be there when Brandt sent someone to collect him. I worked the dead man's wallet out from under his hip with two gloved fingers, which was the only time I touched him after, took his driving licence out of it without reading it, and dropped the wallet on the concrete beside him. The licence went in my pocket with the card. I picked up the hood they had brought for me, folded it into a pad, and pressed it under my jacket against my flank, which was the first good use anyone had made of it all night.

My blood was on his floor, and the elimination register would put my name to it. But it was the floor of a man who could not let anything have happened on it, and I gave the job to the Architect. At the vestibule I keyed the midline into his panel. Whatever the young one set off would ring Brandt, I assumed, not a monitoring company; a man who owns a building three companies deep does not invite strangers in to check it. Then out into the sodium and the two streets to my car, the hood held to my side like a hot-water bottle. Before I touched the wheel I did my face, neck, hairline and wrists by feel with the isopropyl wipes from the door pocket, and bagged them with my gloves. It was not enough, and I knew it, and I drove home at the limit with my jacket zipped to the chin.

Home at about eleven. I brought the desk lamp into the bathroom on its long flex and sat on the edge of the bath, the tile cool under my thighs, and cut my shirt off with the kitchen scissors. Blood, his over mine. I looked down and twisted into the lamp until the wound came round into the light, at an angle of the spine no physiotherapist would sign off, and held it there for the whole repair.

Single-edged blade: the posterior end squared, where the spine had sat, the anterior end acute. Three point two centimetres at the skin, longer than the blade was wide, because I had been turning and the skin had been pulling the other way. A track of about four centimetres, upward and medially; later the closed jaws of the forceps found it ending where I had felt it end. The absences, one by one. No guarding. No rebound. No pain at the tip of the shoulder, which is what blood under the diaphragm tells you about. Urine clear. Peritoneum and kidney, on the evidence, intact. Blood loss perhaps three hundred millilitres, two-thirds of a donation, most of it now in a hood that had been meant for my head.

*The skin tells you about the blade,* Mara said in my head, from the projector, in the lecture we gave together last spring. *The muscle tells you about the hand.* I had taught it to her. The muscle told me about his hand. It had been holding back.

I made saline in the kitchen, nine grams of salt to a litre of boiled water, cooled: about a third of the strength I brine a chicken in, which tells you what I think of chickens. I irrigated the track until it ran clear into the bath. My residency suture kit lives in the drawer under the sink. Lidocaine lives in hospitals. The dead never ask for it, and it had never occurred to anyone to issue me any for the living.

*Close it along the lines it wants to keep.*

I closed it. Three buried interrupted sutures of the braided absorbable through the external oblique and its fascia, knots turned down out of the way, so that the deep layer would hold when I bent; then seven simple interrupted stitches of the nylon in the skin, the needle going in at right angles and out at right angles, the forceps' teeth biting the skin edge, which is my skin edge and has opinions, the drag of the nylon through my own dermis like a thread through felt, each edge everted a little so it would heal flat, each one an instrument tie, square knot, the tails left long enough to find again on the tenth day, and the pain arriving at every pass exact and bright and local and reported, and beside it in the same breath the deep plain pleasure of doing a thing properly with my own hands, which were steady. They were steady the whole time. I put the needle down. I dressed it, and taped the dressing with the same micropore I use on specimen labels at work.

I was going to be very hungry. I had known since the floor.

At a quarter past twelve I sat at the counter, my left arm held against my side, and made tartare from a fillet tail I had bought for something else, chopped by hand with a single-edged knife; I noticed that. Shallot, capers, cornichons, Dijon, Worcestershire, a yolk broken over the top, on toasted sourdough: the beef cold and mineral and soft, the yolk coating everything, the capers going off like small sharp lights, the toast cracking under it. Haemorrhage wants iron and protein replaced, and I replaced them, which is what I would have said in August. I ate all of it.

I was not tired. Across the city Brandt would be home from his dinner by now, being told about his floor. I dressed carefully over the dressing, dark and high-waisted, and went out at twenty to one to a late bar in a business hotel by the river. There was a man. I didn't ask his name. At his flat, which smelled of someone else's laundry powder, I kept my shirt on and fucked him sitting up, fast, and came hard enough to feel every one of the seven stitches. I checked his hands. The fingertips were soft. Nobody had ever made him practise anything. I noted it, and did not tell myself what I had been checking for. He had a drawer of spare toothbrushes still in their cellophane, which told me more about him than anything he said, and he said a great deal. I used one and took it home in a tissue, because it had me on it.

Home at four. Asleep by ten past, and nothing, no dream, until twenty to seven: two and a half hours, gone under the way a patient goes under, counting backwards from ten and never reaching seven. I dressed for the Saturday rota, badge, keys, the wound under my shirt, and went to the sink to brush my teeth.

I had a mouthful of foam when I reached for his grade. I have graded everyone I have met since August, including a donor's dog. I reached, and there was nothing there. He had no page, no subject and no function, and I stood at the sink and could not think what he had been examined on. My fingertips had gone to the dressing. I hadn't sent them. And I had woken rested. I had slept better than I had in years.

On the sink edge, parallel to the tap, the needle. In the cup beside mine, a second toothbrush, still damp. I threw it away and went to work.


# Chapter 13

His house was two degrees warmer than mine. I knew it before the door was shut behind me: the air came up against my face already at the temperature of another person's preferences. His heating ran on a timer. Mine is on a dial I keep low. I stood inside his back door at twenty past nine on a Sunday night, the pick still in my gloved hand, and let the house tell me about him before I looked at any of it.

Coffee grounds, a day old, gone to forest floor. Rye, faintly, from a bottle on the counter. Fennel and garlic from the day, the anise of the one riding the low sulphur of the other. And nearer the sink, something dry and green and rooty, a cellar somebody keeps well. I had last met it at the height of a standing man's chest, on the brass pull of my own drawer. Here it came off a bar of soap by the tap. I didn't put a name to it. I wanted something left for later.

He was on the thirty-first floor of the Calloway until about midnight, working the Sunday call sheet, which had been Diane's job until Diane stopped having Sundays. There was no alarm and no camera over any door. A man who knows what logs are for keeps none of his own. The deadbolt had taken me four minutes, and afterwards I put the penlight to the lock face: no scratches on the brass, no bright crescents where a pick had slipped. He had come through mine without a mark, and by now I had decided it was a pick. I had come through his the same way. We were even. I didn't give either of us a number for it.

He had come into my flat and touched nothing in the kitchen. I am a better guest. I brought something.

The print was in the inside pocket of my jacket. Eight by ten, glossy: the official scene close-up of Diane's mouth, the fragments crowding at the vermilion border like teeth that had come in wrong, and on the one at her lower lip, under its thin rule, half the matter number. I had printed it from the case file. The system logs every look I take and expects them, because the scene set comes to the reviewer of record as a matter of course. Reviewer: that is what the word is for. I laid the print face-up beside the kettle, squared to the counter's edge. He had looked at a copy of this on his phone for two minutes and eleven seconds. A man ought to see his work in the field.

Then I went through his house.

The sodium came through the blinds in bars, the colour of weak marmalade, and I kept a penlight with a darkroom-red lens low, at my knee. I didn't need to touch anything. I touched everything, as if I were choosing him a present and wanted very much to get it right. The charcoal suits in the wardrobe, each sleeve rubbed once between finger and thumb: worsted, a close twist that holds a crease without being asked. His reading glasses, one arm mended with clear tape. Not my tape. Two pillows. The near one dented and soft and faintly rancid with the oil of a scalp; the far one firm and cool. It had not been slept on in two years. You can tell, the way you can tell a lung that has never been smoked through: it is simply too pleased with itself.

The front room was the records. The piano was there too, a black shape at the far end with its lid down, and I didn't go to it, and I knew where it was the whole time I was in that room. The records ran the length of one wall, spines out, alphabetical by artist and then by the date of the recording. Coltrane. Dolphy. Mingus. Yards of names I had never heard. Cardboard, and the faint vanilla of ageing sleeves, which is lignin breaking down, which is a library dying contentedly.

The first unit, the War Room model, went on the top shelf, angled down over the turntable, the drinks tray and the piano, with one strip of black vinyl to hold it and its lead run down behind the spines to the turntable's socket strip. It would go out through his own router, on the network key from the first Lantern card. The tape coming off the roll was the loudest sound I made in that house, a small dry tearing, and in his warm front room it sounded like somebody clearing their throat at a funeral.

Then I chose, by the label, like wine in a language I can't read, and the Dolphy I chose had a clock on it with too many hands, and a sign under the clock that said WILL BE BACK. *Out to Lunch!* I liked the joke. I drew the disc out and got it wrong at once, flat between both palms like a plate out of a hot oven, the leather across the grooves; everything else in that room told me it was wrong. It doesn't matter how you hold one you are about to break.

The second unit went on top of the bedroom wardrobe, facing the bed, its lead down the back of the wardrobe to the socket behind it. I had to go up on my toes, and the wound on my left flank pulled once under my shirt, a short hot tug along the stitch line like a hem catching on a nail.

Then the break, which I did with my trade and not my strength. Anybody can snap a record. I wanted it to come apart where I said. I laid the disc on his chest of drawers and scored it with the scalpel across its whole diameter, through the label, one steady pass. Then I set the score along the edge and pressed down on the overhang. Vinyl breaks along a score the way a bone breaks along a fracture somebody has already started. One dry crack, like a knuckle, and two clean halves. My pulse was up in the pads of my fingers. I laid the halves on his pillow, the used one, a hairline apart, the split running through the middle of the label, and the sleeve beside them with its clock face up.

Then the licence. I had taken it off his warehouse floor two nights before and read it at my own table on the Saturday. Dale Garrity. Forty-three. A heavy, bearded man looking at the licensing office's camera as if it had asked him something personal. In the corner, a small heart, pillar-box red. He was an organ donor. Nobody had asked me.

I laid it beside the halves. It was an invoice. He had sent two men to take me without damaging me, which is a brief written by somebody who has never had to carry one out, and this was what it had cost, and he should see the bill, itemised, with a photograph. My fingertips had gone to my side while I thought it, through the shirt, to the place two days old under my own stitches. My face was warm. Then I reached out and slid the licence a quarter of an inch to the left.

I didn't say why. There was nobody to say it to.

* * *

At ten past ten I was back in the kitchen, and everything was placed. At work, when the last pot is labelled, you strip your gloves and leave the suite; anyone still in there after that is there for herself. He would be home at about midnight. I stood with my back to his counter, beside the print, and did not leave. I am reporting it.

I opened his fridge. The light came out over everything at once, white and total, and I read his shelves like an opened abdomen, everything in its place and telling me what it was for. Good butter. A lemon. A jar of cornichons. Eggs. Half a bottle of white with a stopper in it. And front and centre, where a man puts the thing he means to come back to, a lidded enamel dish. My mouth watered before I had the lid off. I had eaten a late lunch and nothing since. Then I shut the fridge on purpose, with the dish in my hands, to have the dark back.

Pork shoulder. Braised that morning with the fennel and the garlic, set cold in its own jelly, one end already carved, a few slices gone at lunch. It was far too much pork for one man. He cooked the way people cook who used to cook for two: too much, and well. The recipe had outlived the marriage.

I took off my right glove.

I decided it, and I did the sum first. The office already had my DNA on file, and he would wash me off his dish himself by morning. I peeled the leather off and pocketed it, and stood in a man's kitchen in the dark with a bare hand, and it was the first time in my life I had bared a hand anywhere I should not have been.

Burglary is a felony. I have never been able to find out what the charge is for eating a man's pork.

I ate standing up, by touch, from the end he had already broached, so that whatever I took would come off his own cut. I ate as I palpate, with my eyes shut, by knowing where things were. The jelly first. Cold on the fingertip and cold on the tongue and then not cold, because jelly from a braise is collagen given back as gelatin, and gelatin lets go a little under the heat of a mouth; it melted at about thirty-five degrees, which is my tongue, and went from solid to gravy without passing through anything in between. Then the meat. The fibres of a shoulder braised long enough part along their own grain, the way muscle parts on the table once the fascia is open and it has been told, at last, that it may. A fennel seed cracked between my molars, sweet and then camphor, the cold anise going up the back of my nose. His hand with the salt was heavier than mine. And at the edge, where the crust had caught in his oven, a bitterness like burnt sugar, which was the best of it, which is always the best of it, which is why you never let anyone else carve. Distinction. I docked nothing for the salt.

About a hundred and fifty grams, two slices' worth and the jelly round them, about a kidney's weight. I'm giving you the number because you'll want it. It was not the meal after. Nobody was dead tonight. I didn't have a word for the other kind.

Then I tidied. With one bare fingertip I smoothed the cut face of the pork and drew the skin of the jelly back over the place I had taken from, the way you bring a wound edge to edge so that it heals flat, and wiped the rim of the dish with a square of his paper towel, and put the dish back in its place, and shut the door on its light. A burglar takes something. I smoothed the jelly. He was not going to know, and I meant him not to. Everything else in that house I had left for him to find, and billed. This was the one thing I kept off the invoice.

I turned his tap with my gloved wrist and washed my hand with his soap. That was a decision too. The bar was worn to a lozenge, and his first wash would take me off it. The lather came up thin and dry and smelling of the cellar, and I worked it into the webs of the fingers as I scrub at work, and dried my hand on the same square of towel and pocketed it, and put the glove back on over the smell of him. My right hand would smell of him for hours. I knew it, and didn't wash again.

The piano was in the next room, and this time I went to it.

A black baby grand, lid down, fallboard closed. I lifted the fallboard. It came up without a sound, which is the sound a thing makes when somebody looks after it, and in the sodium the keys were the colour of old teeth. They were ivory. A tusk is a tooth, and I have spent fifteen years looking at what people smile with. I have never had a piano lesson. His hands know where the notes are in the dark. Mine know where the aorta is.

I know one note. Everybody in an orchestra tunes to it, so it seemed the polite one to ask: the A above middle C. I counted up to it the way you count down from the angle of Louis to find a rib, and pressed it with one gloved fingertip.

It went out into the warm house like a drop of ink into water. I can't name a pitch by ear. But I can tell a clean note from a sour one, as I can tell a clean cut from a ragged one without measuring either. A sour note has a slow wobble in it, a beating, where the three strings that strike together aren't quite agreeing. There was no wobble. Three strings, one note, nothing between them. He kept it true for nobody, in a house with nobody in it.

I lifted my finger and the damper came down, and the room held the note a moment longer than the strings did, and I closed the fallboard over it while it was still sounding, and left the room before I could play more.

* * *

At twenty-five to eleven I stood in the bedroom doorway with the page in my hand. Lesson Two, a fresh print, made at my desk the night before. The master stays in the drawer with *Excellent.* in red in its margin, in my hand. That one is for me. You don't return a student's script with the grade on it before he has sat the paper. It only makes him careless.

I folded it in quarters, ran my thumbnail down each crease and laid it on the pillow beside the halves and the licence. I had known what it said for two years.

I did not stay to watch him find it. I went out through the kitchen, past the print and the fridge and what the fridge knew, and crouched on his step and relocked the deadbolt with the pick, which goes faster than unlocking it, because the pins have already told you everything they know. At ten to eleven I was walking down his lane with nothing of his on me but the smell. In the car I took the gloves off. My right hand, on the wheel, smelled of his soap. I own no scented products. For once in my life I smelled of something, and it was his.

I drove three miles to the hospital, where there is a multi-storey that never shuts, took a ticket at the barrier that I would pay in cash at the machine, and parked on the top deck by the parapet, facing the city, under the sodium deck lights. A hospital car park is the only place in a city where a woman can sit alone in a dark car at one in the morning and everyone who sees her assumes she has a good reason. I did. The engine ticked as it cooled. Cold vinyl, old coffee. I propped the tablet against the wheel, put it on the hospital's open guest Wi-Fi, and brought up both feeds, front room and bedroom, dark, hissing a little, listening to his rooms. The cold came into the car and I let it. It wasn't where I was keeping the warmth.

At twenty-six minutes past twelve the front-room feed heard his front door. He crossed the frame in charcoal with his collar up, as he comes out of the Tabard in the photograph in my drawer, and went out of the far side of it towards the kitchen, where there was no camera. The audio gave me the fridge's hum through the wall, and then, for a long time, nothing. I knew what he was looking at. I listened for the seal of the fridge, that small wet unpeeling, and didn't hear it. That is not the same as it not happening. The dish was in its place either way.

Then the bedroom. The lamp came on beside the bed, low and yellow, and he came in and saw the pillow and stopped, and sat down on the edge of the bed on his own side. He picked up the two halves, one in each hand, and put them together along the break, the way you try a fracture for fit: the ends offered to each other, a small press, a small turn, until they seat. Then he sat there holding them. He sat there a long time.

I did not time it.

I noticed afterwards that I hadn't. I timed his stillness in the War Room to the second. I timed Diane on her bench to the minute, Sunday after Sunday. I time my eggs. I do not know how long he sat on the edge of his bed holding my break together in his two hands. It was the first thing on any feed I had failed to measure.

He put on the glasses with the taped arm and picked up the licence. He read the name and the age, and his face did nothing at all: a man reading a stranger's name off a card. I could watch him not know it. He looked longer at the folded page, then unfolded it and read it once, slowly. He folded it again along my creases and put it in his inside breast pocket, on the left, and slid the halves and the licence into the Dolphy sleeve.

Then he laughed.

A real laugh, a surprised one, the kind that gets out before the person has been consulted. His head went back with it, and it was loud enough that the cheap microphone on the wardrobe clipped it, sheared the top off it into a burst of white noise. I had never heard him laugh. Not once in five weeks of feed. My pulse went up, and I counted it at the left wrist with two fingers of the right, the hand that smelled of him. Ninety-two, against a resting sixty-four. That one I counted.

He went into the front room, into the other frame, and put the sleeve back in its place on the wall, and at twenty to one he poured a drink from the tray by the turntable, rye, neat, two fingers. Then he chose a record. Not the broken one. *The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady.* He lowered the needle on side one and sat down in the chair by the piano with the glass on his knee and did not touch the keys.

The first time I heard that record, in September, through the clean laptop, I turned it off after a few minutes. It was like having a menu read to you when you're hungry. It sounded no better now: three miles through a button, thin, a big band heard through a wall. I didn't turn it off. I sat in the dark with the city in the windscreen and listened to the whole side with him, about twenty minutes of it, the first whole side I had ever heard through, and once, without deciding to, I lifted my right hand to my face. I am putting it down as a finding. It still smelled of his soap.

At the end of side one the tone arm lifted and went home by itself, and there were two silent rooms three miles apart, and neither of us got up to turn it over.


# Interlude — Cam

The print was on the counter beside the kettle, squared to the edge. Twenty-six minutes past twelve, his keys still in his hand. Diane's mouth, half the matter number in the footer. Sharper than the copy on his phone. Vale's.

He stood and looked at it. Not off the channel. Off the file, and her name was on the file. The house was warm, the heating still on its timer, and nothing in the kitchen was out of place but the one thing, and that was squared better than he would have squared it.

He did not open the fridge. He had crossed the front room without turning his head to the records. He went to the bedroom and turned on the lamp.

The bed smelled of him, as it always did, and of nothing else. The record was on his own pillow, where his head went. Two halves, a hairline apart, the sleeve beside them. Dolphy. *Out to Lunch!* His own.

He sat on his side of the bed and fitted the halves together. They met with a small tick. Under his thumb, a groove along the break. Cut first, then snapped. Not temper. Care.

He sat like that a long time, doing for himself what he had done for other men for twenty years: who knew, what it cost, what came next. The sum came out the same every time.

He put on the reading glasses with the taped arm and picked up the licence. Dale Garrity, forty-three, a beard, a donor heart. The name meant nothing; he had paid not to be told it. The heavy one, then. It had lain a quarter-inch to the left of true; he had seen that from the door, before he touched anything. His own measure, given back. Even.

Then the page, folded in quarters. Lesson Two. The Architect. He read it once, to the foot. *Mistook clarity for cruelty.* Fair mark. He did not turn it over.

The page went back along her creases into his inside pocket, against the phone. He sleeved the licence with the halves. Then he laughed, his head going back, too loud for the room. He did not know what at. It stopped the way it had started, without asking him.

He took the sleeve through to the front room and put it back in its place on the wall, among the D's, spine out like the rest. Twenty to one, a rye from the tray. It did not count; a drink after midnight belonged to the day before. He put on side one of the Black Saint and sat in the chair by the piano with the glass on his knee, not touching the piano. He knew every bar of it. He listened as if he did not, for anything that was not where he had left it, and found nothing. The tone arm lifted and went home, and he did not get up to turn it over.

He slept on top of the covers in his clothes, the jacket buttoned over the page, his head where the halves had been. He left the lamp on.

It was still on at six, yellow against the black of the window. The page was where he had put it, warm from him. He changed his shirt and not his suit. In the kitchen the print was where Vale had squared it, and he made coffee beside it and drank it standing, and did not move it.

In the War Room he accused the Akers boy, in front of everyone, of something the boy had not done, and he was home before eleven.

At eleven-forty he poured a rye at the counter beside the print. First drink before noon in nine years. He noted the time the way he would note another man's. It tasted thinner in daylight. He took out the page and opened it on the counter and read it again, to the foot, more slowly, with the glass beside it. Then he folded it along her creases and put it back in his inside pocket.

The house was quiet the way it was only quiet on a weekday, and in the quiet he did the other sum, the one he had left on the pillow, and it came out whole. She had been in this house with a blade, and had stood in the dark at the place where his head went, and what she had cut was a record. She could have killed him in his sleep, and she had chosen to warn him instead. She wanted him to understand first, and he found, to his surprise, that he wanted to.


# Chapter 14

The card reader on the War Room door made two notes when it refused you, a high one and a lower one. By the time they had come through the bezel unit's microphone and the hotel's network and the tablet's small speaker, they had been thinned into the sound a microwave makes when it has finished with your soup. I heard it twice that Thursday, at 7:51. But you should have the history first. You don't start a chart at the last line.

At twenty past seven the War Room was mostly air handling, a low exhalation the bezel unit had been sending me for six weeks, with the clink of the urn lid over it and a roomful of talk squeezed through the speaker until it sounded like a party heard through a floor. Beside the tablet, against the fruit bowl, a black coffee I had made and was not drinking had stopped steaming and begun to smell of the cup. I wasn't due on the table until ten. My hands were flat on the counter either side of the tablet, the way you rest them at a case conference when it's somebody else's turn to present. I was waiting for Cam Brandt to come in and not sit down.

I had been taking the history for three days, the way you take it from a patient who doesn't know he's giving one. On Monday morning, in front of the room, he told Theo Akers, the boy in digital, that he had leaked the photograph.
