The boy said he'd deleted it, like he was told, and as he said it a clean pallor went up under his hair as if a blind had been drawn. Brandt went home before eleven. Since then he had stood: at the end of the table, at the window, at the urn, carrying a coffee he didn't drink.

The room had noticed before anyone said anything. A ward knows which bed's cultures have come back before the consultant has read them; the nurses' routes go a step wider round the foot of it, and none of them could tell you they'd done it. The War Room had made the same adjustment. In three days nobody stood within an arm's length of him. I was counting. Diane would have managed it: a side room, a hand at the elbow, one pause of the kind her staff mistook for thoughtfulness, and fixed before lunch. Diane was not available. I knew the reason exactly, and the time. 10:47, by my watch.

On Wednesday evening, at 9:14 by the feed's timestamp, he bent to Holt's ear. Holt stood and put a hand on his shoulder, and the two of them went out of the glass room together and out of the lens. It was the one conversation of the week I wanted, and the camera gave me the doorway, a rectangle of corridor light with nobody in it. Holt came back alone four minutes later and was charming about something on the wall screen. Brandt came back nearly two minutes after him and did not sit.

I have read the file. Holt's dossier, in my drawer, has a section for the men he has stopped knowing, and read in a row they are a clinical course. First the meetings stop including you. Then the doors. Then the systems. At no stage does anybody say the word, any more than a transplanted kidney is told it is being rejected. The host just stops sending it blood, a vessel at a time. Nobody in that building had the authority to dismiss Cam Brandt, and so nobody did. They simply stopped letting him in, which is how a hospital handles a death it would rather not certify.

At 7:42 the huddle started without him. On his way to the head of the table Holt touched the deputy communications director on the shoulder, and the deputy sat down at the end of the table, in Brandt's chair. A hand on a shoulder and a man sitting where he was put. I have seen a bed handed over with more ceremony.

At 7:51 Brandt came into the frame outside the glass, in charcoal, and held his card to the reader. The two notes. The light went the colour of a brake lamp. He looked at the card as if it had said something rude, turned it over, and tried again. The two notes. At the head of the table Holt did not look up. It takes years of practice not to look up through glass. Holt did it with his whole face. A man from IT came along the corridor and let Brandt in with his own card, and smiled at him, and the smile was sorry about something.

Brandt went to the laptop at the end of the table. Somebody was sitting at his place, so he logged in standing, one hand flat on the table. The units can't read a screen; they were bought to watch people, and they are good with faces and useless with fonts. So I read his hands, which are the best thing about him. The password went in once, at the speed of a man who has typed it ten thousand times. A pause. Again, a little harder on the keys. A pause. Then a third time, slowly, a key at a time, each finger lifting clear, the way you type when you have begun to suspect it isn't the keyboard. A man types his password a third time the way the bereaved ring a number they know has been disconnected. Not to get through. To hear it not answer.

His hands stopped on the trackpad, the right over the left, fingers a little flexed. He looked up the table at Holt, and Holt was laughing at something the deputy had said, head back, a bright crackle on the cheap microphone. I watched the rejection run its course in Cam Brandt, a procedure I had only ever read in a dossier, performed now by Holt himself, by the book, without his ever being seen to hold the book. I gave it full marks. Something moved under my sternum, a small warm displacement, and I am not going to call it sympathy, because it wasn't. I filed it as the feeling of watching a colleague receive the diagnosis you would have given him yourself: correct, and in good time, and it should have been you who said it. I didn't let the sentence go any further. My left hand had gone under my shirt to the dressing on my flank, the nylon ends a stiff line under the tape, six days old and itching, and it stayed there.

* * *

At ten past one I was in my car in the ME's car park with the engine off. The canteen is on the far side of the car park, and I had come out the other way. Crossing the tarmac I checked my coat pocket, the way you check for a wallet. There was nothing in it. I'd known there wouldn't be.

The sun had been on the car all morning, and the seat was hot through my scrubs at the backs of my thighs, like a bath someone else has run. Over the warm vinyl was the smell I can't smell in the suite, formalin and the unscented soap that doesn't shift it and, under both, the sweetish note fifteen years of corridors have set into my skin like a watermark; in a hot car it was like being shut in with a colleague.

I took the tablet out of my bag and opened the tap's summary, as I had twice a day since I fitted it. It sends me, over Brandt's own uplink, a thin digest of what goes past it: headers, and the bodies of mail up to a couple of kilobytes, nothing attached. It's like being sent a patient's observations instead of the patient.

Holt had locked Cam Brandt out of the house that morning. He had not locked him out of the plumbing, because officially there was no plumbing. The farm was Brandt's private copy of the campaign, three companies deep, with a dead woman's name on the paperwork, and its copy of the mail ran under her compliance authority, not his login. Holt couldn't close a door he had never been told existed. So counsel's correspondence came to me the way everything in that building eventually comes to a pathologist: through the drains.

At 11:12 counsel had sent an email to Holt's office. Not to Brandt. Brandt had been copied on every email of counsel's I'd read for a week. This one went past him the way the huddle had. Its first line was the instruction: *Hold until after the third.* Under it was counsel's reply to a letter, sent at 11:05, and under that the letter itself, received on Wednesday and quoted in full, because a lawyer will not paraphrase anything he may later be quoted on. The digest cut off a line into the firm's disclaimer, with the letter just inside the limit: its words, and no letterhead. I read a body from the outside before I read it from the inside, and I'd been handed this one already opened.

The letter requested *all agreements entered into under matter LC-0311, together with associated signatory records, in connection with the death of Diane Pell.* It was signed *Special Agent E. Reyes.*

Counsel's reply said the campaign was *reviewing the scope of the request and attendant questions of privilege* and *will respond in due course*. I gave him an A. He had it by heart. I have seen *in due course* on an insurer's enquiry into a cause of death, and both parties understood it to mean after the body was cold. Stalling is the one bodily function a law firm never loses, even on the table.

I checked the matter number. I know LC-0311 the way I know the timestamp on a voicemail I deleted. It sits under a thin rule in the footer of every page of my agreement. I had cut copies of those pages to the size of a fingernail and packed them into Diane's mouth so that the footers would be there for whoever opened it. Her teeth closed at the last on the tip of my glove, and I can still find that pressure in the fingertip when I want it. I had pictured the footers followed as far as counsel, where paper goes to wait, embarrassing a man in a good suit on the way. I had not pictured anyone with a name pushing past him. Someone had. All agreements under the matter: staff, vendors, donors, whoever rents them their buses, and mine in among the bus contracts, eight pages and a signature. Somebody with an initial was asking for the lot.

I searched the name the way I pull a chart: name, rank, office. There was nothing I could use. The Bureau doesn't publish its people, which I respect, and an initial and a surname turned up a hundred strangers. I have a dossier on everyone who matters, in a locked drawer, with string and a button. I knew the boy in digital by his hairline. Reyes had a signature block and an initial, and an initial is not a person. It's a case number on a toe tag, waiting for the family.

Out of habit I opened the War Room. At 1:40 counsel went into the glass room and spoke to Holt with his head inclined, the way a registrar talks to a consultant at a bedside when the patient can hear, and Holt nodded twice. Brandt was at the far end of the table, ten feet away, standing with a coffee, and nobody called him over. He may not have known. For the first time that week I knew something about his campaign before he did, and I waited for it to feel the way it should have, and it felt like reading a colleague's results over his shoulder, and my mouth was dry.

My hand had gone to the dressing through my scrub top, and I was thinking, so it stayed there, the tails a ridge under the tape, the skin round them itching the way healing skin does. The car was warm. I noticed the warmth, and I noticed that it didn't change.

I went back to the letter and read it a second time, all of it, from the signature block up to the first line, the way I read a chart, latest entry first. *Special Agent E. Reyes. In connection with the death of Diane Pell. Associated signatory records. All agreements entered into under matter LC-0311.* There was no page for Reyes. There was no subject line, no function, no Evaluation typed two years ago at my desk. In the warehouse the Friday before, I had done something that was not on the syllabus, but that had been a thing I did. This was a thing I was in. For the first time, something was happening that I had not written.

* * *

On Friday at ten past five in the morning I had the desk lamp on. It ticks as it warms, like a fingernail on a specimen jar. I unlocked the drawer and took out Lesson Two, my master: *Excellent.* in red beside the subject line, *Tessa Quinn* in black beneath it, ruled through once. I didn't read the Evaluation. I know what it says. I did arithmetic.

The plan had the Architect in the third week of October, close enough to the election that the campaign couldn't absorb him. You can bury a Chief of Staff in September with a statement at 5:58. Nobody can bury the man who writes the statements a fortnight from the vote. It was a good plan. I had liked it for two years. I gave it up in the time it takes a kettle to boil.

My reasons, in order. Counsel could stall, and stall well, but paper wins in the end; I work for it. A man Holt has cut loose has nothing left in the building and a reason to walk into a federal office and trade what he knows for himself, and what Brandt knew was my name. Brandt walled off was Brandt with a reason to talk to E. Reyes. Third, which was the second again: inside the week. Nobody revises a syllabus in the middle of term unless the examiner is coming early. I gave the logic full marks. I squared the page to its own edge and put it back and locked the drawer, and while I was squaring it I thought, for no reason I put into words, of a letter with an initial at the foot of it reaching him before I did.

At half past nine, at work, I booked Wednesday the fourteenth off through the leave system. One line. A day booked five days ahead is wallpaper in a personnel file; a sick call on the morning itself is the line an investigator underlines. *Reason for absence* is a box you are allowed to leave blank, and I left it blank. On my side of the building nobody gets to. That was administration.

I ate less that week. I'm telling you because I tell you the facts about lunch, and nothing is also a menu. Thursday: a black coffee, made at seven and left to go cold beside the tablet. No lunch. In the evening, one egg, boiled, eaten out of the cup with a teaspoon at the counter. The yolk had gone past soft to chalk and left sulphur at the back of my tongue. Half a slice of toast; the other half I left on the board. Friday so far: water from the kitchen tap at five, which had stood in the pipes all night and tasted of them, flat and mineral.

The body's version, in its own notation. Appetite reduced, onset Thursday. No nausea. No pain. Afebrile. Weight not taken. Patient reports her mood as good. Noted. Unexplained. Not discarded. The body knew something the plan didn't, and I didn't ask it what.

Mara and I washed up side by side, a basin apart, before the case. She told me about her lunch, which was an egg-and-cress sandwich she'd had in the fridge since eight and plainly meant to eat the minute we were done, and she offered me half across the sink. I knew what it would be by one o'clock: white bread gone soft and sweating in its clingfilm, the egg's sulphur coming up through the mayonnaise, the cress wet and peppery.

"No, thank you, Mara."

She looked at me for about as long as it takes to read a label twice. Then she went back to the soap. I'm telling you what she did. I'm not telling you what it was worth.

The case, at a quarter past eleven, was a man of seventy-one, found in his armchair with the radio on. He had been there about two days. When we unzipped the bag his room came up out of his clothes: dust cooked onto the back of a radio, the sourness of a chair that has held the shape of one man for longer than most marriages, a close, sweet, unaired smell like the inside of a biscuit tin. He died with the radio on. Somebody had been playing to him for two days, which is more than most of my patients get.

The extraction fans in the suite run all day at the pitch of an aeroplane cabin at altitude, so constantly that they stop being a noise and become the air, and anything quieter than a dropped instrument goes into it and doesn't come out unless you are standing close. That's why I didn't notice it at first.

I took the heart out. It came into my gloved hand heavy and firm and cold, four degrees out of the cooler, slick, the left ventricle thick under my fingers like the wall of a cistern. I laid it on the scale and the scale thought about it and said four hundred and eighty grams. That was his whole history in one figure: blood pressure, years of it, unremarked, and then an armchair. I felt it before I heard it, a vibration in my chest behind the sternum, like a phone going in a coat pocket.

I was humming.

It was the Mingus. I'd been hearing it all week through the unit on the top shelf of his record wall, side one, every night since the page, from the chair by the piano. I had first heard it in September through laptop speakers, and turned it off. I had never hummed at work. Not once in fifteen years. I didn't know how long I had been doing it. The fans had been keeping it for me.

Mara had leaned in over the scale to read the weight. Her pen stopped on the form, and she looked up.

"What is that?"

"Mingus," I said. "*The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady.*"

At twenty to ten that night my phone lit up on the kitchen counter. Mara. No message, only a link, bare, the album's small square under it. I didn't reply. I played it. The phone lay face-up beside the bread board, where Thursday's other half-slice had gone stiff and curled at the edges like a page left in a window, and the music came out of the little speaker thin and far away, the whole band squeezed down into something you could have held in one hand, and I stood at the counter and listened. When it came to the place where a needle would have lifted at the end of side one, nothing lifted. A phone has no sides. I reached out and stopped it there myself.


# Chapter 15

The stitches came out at a quarter past six on the Monday, at the bathroom basin under the fluorescent, because it was the tenth day. I keep appointments, even my own. Seven simple interrupted, four-nought nylon, the tails left long so I could find them. I am the only surgeon I know who has ever had to read her own handwriting off her own flank. I lifted each knot with the Adson forceps, slid the suture scissors under it, cut on the side nearest the skin and drew the stitch out through its own track, which is the only direction a stitch ever wants to go, and each one came with a small ticklish drag, like a hair pulled from a hem. The seam underneath was the pink of boiled ham, and shut, and a little proud at its lower end, where the skin had torn when I landed. Healing by primary intention. I put the seven black commas in a tissue and went to send an email.

At twenty to eight I was in a bus-station café on its public wifi, with my back to the dome camera over the till, the clean laptop open and an americano that tasted of the bottom of the pot and of the paper of the cup, which had got into the coffee the way the cup's shape gets into your hand. Around me people were eating eggs. I filed a complaint with the state's data-privacy regulator, anonymously, alleging unlawful interception of communications by Lantern Data Services LLC, and copied it to the abuse desk of the upstream provider that carried Lantern's line. I attached the annual report from the state's own register, the public one, with her signature at the foot. Officer of record: Diane Pell. Under it I attached her death notice. Then I sent the notice and the report again, to the bereavements desk of the bank whose statements came through his mail. Every account in that chain had one signatory and one contact, both of them her, and nobody in compliance keeps a dead woman's account open for longer than it takes to read the notice.

I had killed the officer of record, which in my experience is the fastest way to get a compliance department to return your email.

I didn't need the inquiry to succeed. I needed him dark for forty-eight hours: no feeds, no cameras, no eyes in the warehouse district or anywhere else his systems reached, starting before Tuesday night and running until well after it. The Gatekeeper was still keeping the Architect's door. She was doing it now by being dead across the threshold.

I closed the laptop, and a thought came in without knocking. I had just written to the government. Somebody else was writing to it too. E. Reyes, a signature block and an initial, had asked for every agreement under a matter number I knew by heart, and I wondered for a moment which inbox my complaint would land in, and whether one day the two would lie on the same desk. I decided it didn't matter for forty-eight hours. Forty-eight hours is a long time in a body and no time at all in a federal building. I finished the coffee, because I had paid for it, and went to work.

The suite smelled of formalin and of the floor cleaner the night staff use, which is pine, if pine had been arrested. Mara had the next table. She asked without looking up, with her blade an inch above a sternum, whether I had listened to it.

"Yes," I said, and then, because she still hadn't looked up, "Yes."

I had played it as far as a needle would have lifted, and no further, and I didn't tell her that. She said nothing else. The tops of her ears went pink, the helix first and then the lobe, the way a blush climbs when its owner is pretending it isn't there. I mark everything. I had marked the café's coffee and the regulator's online form before nine. I didn't mark that, and I didn't ask myself why.

My own case was a man of eighty-one, natural causes. The heart came out of him at four degrees, with the cooler's breath still on it, and I set it in the pan of the hanging scale and felt the cold of it through my glove the way you feel the weather through a window, a thing not yet worth a sentence. Four hundred and sixty grams. He had a big heart. It is the commonest thing in the world, and more of them die of it than of anything else I open.

At ten to five, out of the gown and out to my car in the ME's car park, I took the tablet from my work bag. The tap I had wired into Lantern's uplink sent a heartbeat every few minutes, and I had set the tablet to tell me if it ever missed one. It had missed one, at 4:41. At the same minute the two units in his house had gone dark, and a tap and two cameras have nothing in common but the line they share. So it was the line. The abuse desk had found a dead woman as its only contact and put the account on hold. Nine hours from filing. I graded the abuse desk: prompt, frightened, a B-plus, docked for not checking whether I existed.

Then the other half of it arrived, the half I had not priced in. My tap had gone dark with his line, and my eyes in his house with it. The War Room feed still ran, but Holt had seen to it that he was no longer in that room. For two days I would have no wire into him at all. I would have to see him with my own eyes, like a person. I touched my side, where the seam was.

Nothing on the syllabus had ever gone faster than I planned it. In my experience, when things go early it is because somebody at the other end didn't fight. I didn't let myself finish the thought about who.

I had more time than I needed, and I didn't trust it, and so I used it to cook. On the way home I went to the butcher's and bought a whole free-range bird with its giblets in a paper twist, and at the stove I was quick and happy. Salt went into the hot water with a sound like a breath let out through the teeth, and the water went clear. Bay, black peppercorns, three curls of lemon peel, and the kitchen smelled of a hillside somebody else owns. When the brine had cooled I put the bird in a lidded tub, breast down, and weighed it under with a plate, and it went into the fridge for Wednesday, for whoever I would be on Wednesday. The neck and wingtips went into a pot for stock. The liver I fried in butter until it was seared outside and pink in the middle, sweet and ferrous and soft as a bruise, and ate on toast standing at the counter, and that was all I ate. I am a professional. You always taste the liver first.

* * *

Herding is mostly a matter of which gates you leave open. Sheepdogs know this. So does every HR department I have ever filed a complaint with.

With the feeds gone I herded him blind, on the dossier, his mail and a card. I shut every other door on him in order, enjoying each one like the last click of a zip-tie's ratchet, and left one on the latch, the way you leave a light on for a guest who doesn't know the house. It was the one company I hadn't named to the regulator, a media consultancy he owned silently, with an editing suite on the third floor of a converted printworks east of the river. Its lease, alarm and building cameras were paid through a holding company of their own, and the cameras routed through Lantern, so they were dark now too. Facilities mail is the least guarded mail in any company, and eleven days of his had given me the stairwell code and the suite keypad as plain text, like a shopping list. On Monday night I read the keypad code again at my own counter, and the heat came up my neck into my face like the first glass of something. The card I had carried out of Lantern on the night I bled on his floor gave me the rest: the fit-out drawing, with the cable grommets along both long edges of the edit desk, and the acoustic contractor's specification for the walls.

At a quarter past ten on Monday night I rang his personal phone from a second burner. It went to voicemail, as I had meant it to. I left a message in my own voice, in the register of a business reporter from a wire service, calm and polite and a little bored, asking about Diane Pell's directorship of Lantern Data Services, and I said I would try him at the house tomorrow. I had the phrase from his own mail. It was what he wrote when he wanted somebody to stop sleeping. It put the voice I give evidence in onto a phone the police would be holding within the week, and I priced that, and paid it. I had never left a message I wanted returned less. Now his house was a place where a stranger might be standing on the step in the morning, holding a notebook.

On Tuesday morning, before my shift, I counted the car among the shut doors. His car service billed a card on the shell's account, and the shell's bank had read my notice by now, and a frozen account does not pay for cars. I would know by the evening. Holt had walled him out of the War Room on Thursday, by procedure, without a raised voice. Holt was herding for me, for nothing. He has always been good with staff.

The one thing I left alone was the Tabard Club. It was Tuesday, his night, the set from nine to eleven. I wanted to know whether, with every other door shut, he would choose the room where people listened to him or the room where nobody could.

Then I worked a whole day on competence: two cases, a report signed, my initials on every specimen label. At the sinks in the afternoon Mara hummed four bars of the Mingus, scrubbing, the tune coming out of her nose more than her mouth, and stopped in the middle of the fifth when she heard what she was doing. I didn't say anything. I didn't mark it either. That was twice.

At half past five I parked across the street from the printworks and turned off the engine, and for a while it ticked as it cooled, like a slow clock losing interest. Four storeys of soot-dark brick, the consultancy on the third. One stair served every floor, behind a column of frosted glass on the street side, with a door at its foot onto the street and another at the back onto the car park. I could not run the clap test this time. For the first time on the syllabus I was trusting somebody else's paperwork. I had read the acoustic contractor's report twice and given it a B. The seals were good. The prose was not. The tub of wipes was in the driver's door pocket, where it lives.

At ten past six the streetlights came on, the sodium warming through pink to that dense orange that makes everybody under it look embalmed. The car smelled of cold upholstery, and my skin smelled of the morgue under the soap, as it always does. I had my hands on the wheel at ten and two, and they stayed there for fifty minutes, still, bar once. I hadn't eaten since the liver. The wound pulled a little from my sitting twisted to watch the door, and my right hand came off the wheel to it, and the fingertips stayed there while I thought, and then went back.

At twenty past six, the hour he would have been sitting down to dinner before his set, a taxi pulled in across the street with its indicator going. Not his car. The shell had stopped paying for cars. My pulse went up in my throat with the indicator, on the same beat, as if the two of them were on one circuit. He got out in charcoal. He had a paper bag in one hand with the neck of a bottle showing, and under his arm a record in a card mailer. The last record of his I had touched, I had snapped in half and left on his pillow. He was bringing another. He looked up the street and down it once, not afraid, the way a man looks when he is early for a table and wonders whether she is too. Then he crossed under the sodium, and I let myself look at him the way I had looked at the heart in the pan: eighty-odd kilos, at blood heat all the way through, and under the charcoal, at the top of his breastbone, the sternal angle, the small ridge where the second ribs come in, which was where the shears would start. I stopped there.

He had a bottle. You don't bring a bottle to a place you are hiding in. You bring a bottle to a dinner.

He let himself in at the street door with his own key. Behind the frosted glass the light came on at the first landing, and then the second, and stopped at the third.

He had read the syllabus. He knew what a soundproofed room was for. He went willingly.

* * *

At seven I cleared the kitchen table and laid a clean white towel on it, and set the instruments out on the towel in the order I would want them, left to right, the way you lay a table for a tasting menu so that nobody has to ask which fork. The small handle and its blade first. The small retractor. The toothed forceps. The larger handle and its blade. The spare blades in their sleeve. The rib shears last, the handles cold enough to feel through the skin of my palm when I set them down. The rib shears were the cheese course: heavy, optional, and the real reason you book. The towel's nap took each piece with a small soft give.

Off to one side, like cutlery set out for the table, I put the rest. A box of nitrile gloves. The thin black leather gloves for the car and the suite door. The red marker. A steel pin. The black vinyl, with its dull shine. Zip-ties. Industrial tape. Two foil sleeves, one for his phone and one for my own. Ten towels, folded. The bags. I had laid a place for him at my own table, and he was not going to be there, which is the usual arrangement with my guests.

What was not on the table was a page. I had watched him on the feed find his copy on his pillow and laugh and keep it. I was going to a lesson without the lesson, and I was trusting him to bring it. I noticed that I trusted him, and I didn't take it back.

I rolled the instruments into the canvas roll the way a cook rolls her knives, tied it and set it in the bag. Then I showered. Hot and short. The forty-minute shower is for after. The seam on my flank was a line the colour of cooked prawn with seven dots either side of it, like a zip drawn on by a child. I washed around it, and then, deliberately, over it, and it took the water and the soap as if it had never been anything else.

I filed my nails short and square over the kitchen sink, where the dust could go down the drain and not onto the towel. Then I scrubbed at the same sink, with the nail brush and the soap, two minutes a hand by the clock on the cooker, palms, backs, between the fingers, every finger root to tip, up to the wrists and past them.

You don't eat before. It is a rule from a different kind of evening, and it is also, I noted, the rule for the patient and not for the surgeon. Nil by mouth is an instruction to the patient. I had never in my life followed one. At half past seven I warmed a cup of Monday's stock and salted it and drank it standing, golden, with a lens of fat across the top, and the faint sweetness the neck bones give up if you let them, and I ate one heel of yesterday's bread with it, the crust gone tough as a thumbnail and the crumb still soft enough to take the stock. When I opened the fridge for the stock I lifted the lid of the tub a finger's width, and the brine breathed out at me, bay and lemon peel and raw bird, and there was the chicken on the bottom shelf, pale under its plate, waiting for Wednesday. I put the lid down and shut the door on it. The waistband of my trousers, when I put them on, sat against a stomach that had been given almost nothing for two days, and I felt the pull of that against the wool, and I liked it. I am reporting that.

A black silk shirt with sleeves I could push to the elbow. It was cool going on, over skin that had just been scrubbed, and it hung loose over the seam. Black wool trousers. A black jacket over the silk, with a pocket deep enough for a phone. Flat shoes with a good sole, for stairs. Hair washed and pinned up tight, because I would be working, and because one hair of mine on his carpet would come home to me. There was no hair cover in this kit. You don't wear one to dinner. No jewellery. Rings tear gloves, and a woman who is going to be elbow-deep in something should not be wearing anything she'd mind losing in it.

Then lipstick, which I do not wear to work. Oxblood, and unscented, because I own nothing that smells. When I pressed my lips together it tasted of wax and nothing else. I can give you a reason for it. The mouth is the part of a face that gives least away, and a colour on it gives it something to do, so that whoever is looking at you looks there and not at your eyes. It is a good reason. I didn't believe it while I was putting it on, and I'm telling you that I didn't.

I went through the bag once more. The roll. The kit. Then the jacket: my own phone in its pocket, sealed into its foil sleeve at the counter, so that the last place it could vouch for was my kitchen and after that it would say nothing at all, which is not an alibi and is a flag of its own, and was still better than a line drawn from my flat to his door. The two codes, written nowhere, because I had them by heart. My car keys, with the drawer key on the same ring, as always. My pulse was up and my face was warm. I didn't put either of them down to nerves. I began to give the preparation a mark and stopped, because there was nobody to give it to.

On the way out, the hall mirror.

"You look like you've got a date," I told her, out loud.

I didn't correct it.

I left at a quarter past nine and parked in the rear car park at five to ten with the leather gloves on. His set was an hour old by then, and going on without him. The stair, by its code, from the car-park door. The corridor with its one working light. At the end of it, the suite's door, not quite pressed into its seals, and under it, faint, Mingus: *The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady*, turned down so that he would hear me knock.


# Chapter 16

I didn't knock.

The corridor had one working light, and it hummed. Under the hum, the Mingus, coming round the edges of the door at the far end, thin, a bass line and a muttering of brass, the way a party sounds from the stairs. He had turned it down so that he would hear me knock. It was the most considerate thing a man had done for me in years, and I ignored it, which is also traditional.

The keypad took the code I had carried in my head and nowhere else. The door came away from its frame with a small sucking sound, a fridge opened at night, and swung to behind me and seated itself into its seals with a soft pressure in my ears.

The room reached me before I looked at it. Rye, opened and poured, a dry grainy sharpness at the height of the table. The warm dust that comes off equipment left on all day. The new-foam smell of the acoustic panels, faintly sweet, like a packet of earplugs. And at the height of a seated man, the dry green note I had last met at his kitchen sink, worked into the webs of my own fingers. Then it had been a bar of soap. Now it was sitting down.

One lamp. A row of amber standby lights along the wall at knee height. The long edit desk under its monitors, matte as slate, giving nothing back. A low table, a leather chair beside it, a second chair across. In the leather chair, Brandt, in a charcoal shirt with no tie and the sleeves down, his jacket over the chair back and a glass in his hand.

He looked up.

"I wondered if it would be you."

Most men say that at a door with flowers in their hand. He had a rye in his. He said it the way you say a line you have chosen, and then he didn't add anything to it, which is the other half of choosing.

He was not afraid. I read him. Pupils appropriate to the light. Hands loose on the chair arms. Respiration slow. No sweat at the hairline or the lip. His head a little on one side, like a man being shown the movement of a watch. Curious. Composure: excellent. If he had been turning that sentence over all evening, it didn't show; he delivered it as if it had just occurred to him.

Then the *if*. An *if* has an alternative in it, the way a cut has a direction. Somebody else might have come through that door first: Holt's people, now that Holt had walled him out of his own campaign. He had been waiting to see which of us arrived first, and he was glad it was me. I noticed that I was pleased he was glad, and I let myself be.

The photograph was on the low table beside his glass.

It was mine. The eight-by-ten I had printed from the case file and left squared on his kitchen counter nine days before: the official close-up of Diane's mouth, the shredded pages showing at her lips, and in the footer, under its thin rule, half of LC-0311, which was enough. I read it from the outside in. The corners had gone soft, the gloss worn through to the fibre at the tips. A crease ran across the lower third where it had been folded once and opened many times, the line gone white. At the left edge, ghosted into the gloss, a thumbprint, his, the whorl clear enough to lift. I have seen less handling on a family Bible. I have seen less handling on some of my patients. Beside it stood the Mingus sleeve, and beside that, folded in quarters under the foot of his glass, my page.

He had kept it. He had been looking at it every day.

A War Room has read me, on a recording, on a Sunday night. Nobody had ever kept me. Nobody had ever looked at my work as long as he had looked at that photograph. Including me.

"Phone?" I said.

"Jacket. Inside left."

He leaned forward to give me the pocket. I took the phone out with two fingers of my glove, thin black leather, worn from the car, and slid it into the second foil sleeve from my bag. Mine was already in the first, in my jacket, asleep. Whatever he had kept on it went deaf in my hand. I folded the sleeve's mouth over twice and put it back in his pocket. I didn't need it. I needed it not to talk. He watched me do it and gave a small nod, one tradesman watching another use the right tool.

I stood by the door and let my body go over the room. One other person breathing; no second door; nothing behind the charcoal baffles but more baffle. The music was low and very close, with nothing to bounce off, like music in a padded case. My pulse was up against the silk of my collar, and I took the reading and filed it, and it was not fear. I had eaten nothing since a cup of stock and a heel of bread at half past seven, and the hunger was sharp and pleasant, high under the ribs. The wound in my flank was eleven days old and had nothing to say.

Beside his glass and the photograph, set square to the table's edge, stood a second glass. Heavy, clean, dry, nothing in it yet. It had been put out before I came.

He had set out two glasses.

* * *

He poured for me without asking how I took it. Two fingers, neat. He had either guessed or read it somewhere, and I had never written it down, so he had guessed, which was worse.

I took off my right glove to hold it, laid the glove across my knee, and told myself the inventory, the way you count swabs before you close: this hand would touch the glass and nothing else, and the glass would leave with me. The left glove stayed on. I sat on the arm of the second chair, not in it. Sitting in it would have been a different evening.

The glass was heavy and cool and wet at the rim, and my palm on it was the first bare skin of the night. The rye was dry. Pepper first, the char of the cask behind it, no sweetness anywhere, and the heat arriving late at the back of the tongue, the way the sting of a cut arrives after you have looked at your hand. I took the first mouthful slowly and held it, and my mouth stayed dry afterwards, which good rye does. It takes more than it gives.

He had drawn the page out from under his glass and laid it on his knee, still folded.

"Mine," he said. "Read it a lot."

"I can see."

He put it back on the table and stood his glass on it again, in the same place, as if the place had been measured.

"I read the other one too. In your drawer."

I said nothing. He didn't say what was at the head of it. He could have; it would have bought him my face for a second. He let the page be a page. I graded his restraint, and it was high.

"How did you come to it?" he said. "The pages. Not before. Not the..." He moved his hand an inch on the chair arm and put it back. "The pages."

"I reported it," I said. "I kept records. I signed, in Diane's office. Then the blind items came." I looked at him. "Yours."

He didn't look away. He didn't explain them and he didn't apologise for them, and I gave him a mark for each.

That was all I said out loud. Here is the rest, since you have been through my drawer as well. Two years ago, at about this time of night, at my writing desk, a sentence arrived. It came already addressed. Not *she*, not *I*. *You*. I knew who. I sat there until the first bus went past under the window, and kept writing.

Somewhere in the middle of my answer the side ended. I didn't hear the arm go home; my voice covered it, and his listening. I only noticed that the room had got bigger.

He was quiet for a while. Then he said, with every word set down where it belonged, "I would have done the same thing."

He didn't mean the agreement. He meant the pages.

I didn't know what to do with that, so I drank. I have a procedure for every kind of sentence a man can say to me in a room: threats, charm, lawyers, *I don't know what you're talking about*. I had come up those stairs with a joke ready for each. I had not prepared a joke for being understood. In fourteen hundred cases nobody has ever agreed with me. It isn't a thing the dead go in for.

"After the photograph," he said, "I went down the list. The signatory list, privately. I was looking for one name, and I found it, and that is how I found you. Not from your agreement. The pages in her mouth don't say whose they are. The footer is the matter, not the woman. You need the list for the woman." He turned his glass a quarter-turn on the page. "Counsel wrote the template. I want to be accurate about that. The only thing on those pages that is mine is the face. I chose the typeface. Century Schoolbook, because it looks like a judge wrote it."

"You think you're the only one," he said.

He stopped there. He gave me the pause the way you give someone a chair.

"There are eleven names on that list."

He said it flatly. Eleven. Not *about*, not *at least*. He didn't read them to me. He didn't say which one of the eleven knew where the larynx was, because he didn't need to. She was sitting on the arm of a chair across from him with a glass in her hand.

My left hand, the gloved one, went to my flank before I did, and pressed the wound through the silk, and I watched it go. I touch it when I am thinking. I wasn't thinking. It was doing that for me.

*We were the same kind of alone.* I had thought that a month ago, and liked it so much that I kept it. It came back now for the length of a clause and fell over. Ten others had signed what I signed.

My face was hot. The heat came up out of my collar and stayed, the way it does when you have been caught at something in a room full of people, and there was only one person in the room.

He looked as if he would say something more. His mouth began it. Then he decided, and I watched him decide, *not yet*, and let it go, and put his glass down on the page instead.

I had believed myself alone. I had built two years on being the only one, and every page in that drawer stood on it, and he had just told me the floor was a list. He watched it go in. He was kind about it. He got up out of the leather chair and turned his back on me, so that I could have my face to myself, and went to the turntable and lifted the arm and set the needle down at the start of side one again. The lead-in groove crackled like a fire being lit in another room. He turned it up a notch, so that neither of us would have to say anything for a while. He did it without being asked. It cost him nothing he would ever need again, and that was what made it unbearable.

* * *

The side ran out again a little after eleven, and the quiet after it was my cue.

I put my glass down beside the photograph and drew the right glove back on. I took off my jacket, with my own phone asleep in its foil in the pocket, and hung it over the back of the second chair. Then I stood up and cleared his desk.

The long edit desk was ninety-six inches, which is two hundred and forty-four centimetres. I always do the conversion. That night the furniture was going to hold a body, and I wanted them in the same units. The keyboards and the jog wheel went down on the carpet along the wall, the coiled cables laid down in loops. I pushed the monitors back on their arms, dark, showing nothing. The cable grommets ran along both long edges, a cable-tray rail under each, exactly where the fit-out drawing in his facilities attachments had put them. It is a deep pleasure when a building agrees with its paperwork. Ask anyone who has bought a house how rarely it does.

I unrolled the instrument roll on the low table between the photograph and the two glasses and fitted the blades there: a 10 onto the No. 3 handle, a 22 onto the No. 4, each slid down its bayonet with the forceps until it seated with a click. Then the small retractor, the toothed forceps, the spare blades in their sleeve, and the rib shears last, heavy, the cheese course. The box of nitrile and the towels in their bags went on the floor beside the second chair. The tasting menu, served.

He came to look at it with his hands in his pockets. He stood over it the way a man reads a wine list he can't afford, which, in fairness, he couldn't. He looked at each piece in order and didn't ask what any of it was for. Not one question. I noticed, and I graded it, and it was the highest mark I had given him yet.

"On the desk," I said. "On your back."

"One thing first."

He went to the turntable and lifted the arm and put the needle back at the start of side one, a third time. I let him. I wouldn't have known how. Then he sat on the edge of the desk, swung his legs up and lay down without being helped, his head towards the lamp, and laid his arms out along the edges of the desk, palms up, and waited. A man on a desk with room to spare at both ends. I have never in my life been so well supplied with margin.

I unbuttoned his left cuff and rolled the sleeve to the elbow, two neat turns and a third to lock it, and then the right. His forearms were long and lean, a little dark hair along the ulnar border and none on the volar skin, which was pale and fine as good paper, and when he settled his hands the tendons stood up in a fan at each wrist. I wanted my mouth on the inside of his wrist, there, over the tendons, and I wanted it the way I want to eat.

Then the tape, industrial, round each wrist, palm up, and down to the edge of the desk. Coming off the roll in that room it was enormous, a long rip like canvas, and the room kept every inch of it. Then the zip-ties: at each wrist down through a grommet and round the steel rail of the cable tray beneath, because a tie through a hole is tied to nothing. Halfway round his left wrist I thought of the other ten, ten signatures I would never see, and I folded the thought once along its length and laid it under the work, the way you fold a towel under a limb to keep the field dry. My hands didn't stop for it. Each ratchet went under the Mingus with its small quick sound, a watch being wound. The ankles to the legs at the far end, taped and tied. A band of industrial tape across his thighs, which was thoroughness and not need. I kept the leather gloves on for all of it. Tape adhesive takes a print better than glass does.

He tested each tie as I finished it, a small courteous flex against the plastic, the way a man tries a door after he has locked it for you, to show you that it holds. I have read a great many tags on a great many toes. None of them had ever helped.

His hands lay open on the wood, the fingers a little spread, the way hands rest on a closed lid. I looked at them for longer than I needed to, and I didn't touch them.

The smell had changed now that he was lying down. Rye on both our breaths, and the dry green note coming up off him into the heat of the lamp, no longer at the height of a seated man but at the height of a table. The height of my work. I read him from the top: the suprasternal notch in the open collar, a shadowed well above the manubrium, and beside it the carotid lifting the skin of his neck, slow and even, a man at rest.

My pulse was high and it was unhurried, which is the best combination there is and the rarest; you find it in surgeons and in almost nobody else. My face was hot and my knickers were wet, and I noted both in the same hand I note everything in, and went on. And I was hungry. I had not eaten since the bread, and that was not why.

"Take your time," he said. "I would rather it was you than anyone else."

Nobody has ever said anything like that to me. I want to say it plainly. Not a lover; the ones who talked never talked like this. Not Hannah, who loves me and wants me to take a holiday. Not one colleague in fifteen years of corridors, and not one of the fourteen hundred, who never said anything to me in any form. It is what you say to a woman you are in no hurry to be finished with. I had heard it said in films. I had assumed it was a special effect.

I was going to take him at his word. I wanted the night to be long. I wanted it to outlast me. You came up the stairs with me, and you could go back down them now, and you are still in the room.

I took off the leather gloves a finger at a time and put them in my bag. They were the last thing I had come in wearing that was going to come off. The nitrile was in its box. His jacket hung on the leather chair with his phone asleep in its pocket, and I didn't look at it again.

The record ended. The arm lifted and went home by itself, and in that room we could both hear it settle, and neither of us moved to change it. I rolled up my sleeves.


# Chapter 17

*Lesson Two. Subject: The Architect. Function: to design the room in which the thing did not happen. You draw the plans before the complaint is made. You set the walls where the complainant will be standing. You furnish the room with a better story, you leave a light on so that anyone who looks in can see there is nothing there, and you sign nothing yourself. You have performed this function for ██ years without once lying to yourself about what it is. Your colleagues at ████████ mistake this for integrity, and you have let them. You see every room exactly as it is. You have concluded that to see it plainly was the hard part, the unkind part, the part that cost, and that having paid it you owe nothing further. Sight is not a payment. Sight is the least that was owed. What you built after you had seen is the work under evaluation. Evaluation: You mistook clarity for cruelty.*

My sleeves were already at my elbows. I took the gloves from the box the way I take them at work, two at a time, left and then right, and snapped each cuff over the wrist, and in the morgue nobody ever hears that snap because the extraction fans are louder than God. In the suite there were no fans. The record had come to the end of its side a minute before, and the tone arm had lifted and gone home by itself, and the room, which a man had paid a great deal of money to make deaf, took the sound of the nitrile and gave none of it back. I heard my own gloves the way you hear yourself swallow when you have a cold. I heard him breathing. It did not sound as if it came from the desk. It sounded as if it were inside my ear, and had been invited.

Smell first. It always is, with me; I was a nose before I was a pair of hands, and fourteen hundred cases have only made the nose vain. He smelled of vetiver, the soap rather than the cologne, that dry green rootiness men buy because they have been told it smells of money and which in fact smells of a good cellar. Under the vetiver, rye, on his breath and on the rim of the glass beside the photograph. Under the rye, the warm dust that comes off amplifiers and screens that have been on all day, a smell like an ironing board left standing. And under all of it, new, rising while I stood there from the hair of his axillae into the charcoal cotton of his shirt, the sharp first note of apocrine sweat. Not the sweat of heat. The other one. On a decedent I would have written it down: fear, recent onset. On him I wrote nothing down. I breathed in through my nose, slowly, the way you smell a glass before you are allowed to drink it, and it was the first time in my working life that a body had been afraid of me.

He was lying on his back on the long edit desk, which I had cleared, with his wrists taped palm-up to its edge and zip-tied through the cable grommets, his ankles done the same at the far end, and a band of industrial tape across his thighs, because I am thorough and not because he was going anywhere. The one lamp was behind his head. The standby lights of the equipment ran in a row along the wall at knee height, small and amber and patient, like the eyes of animals at the edge of a road. I laid a towel under each of his arms and a towel along each of his sides. I opened his shirt with a blade from collar to hem, the way you open a letter you have waited a long time for and want to keep the envelope, and laid the two halves back, and there he was.

"Should I watch?" he said.

"Most of my patients don't get a vote."

"That isn't an answer."

"Watch, then."

I examined him first. I always do. You don't open a book at the index.

"The body is that of a well-nourished adult male," I said, in the voice I use for the dictaphone, "consistent with the stated age of forty-eight years. Length approximately one hundred and eighty-three centimetres. Weight estimated at eighty-four kilograms."

"Eighty-two."

"Clothed."

He laughed. It went through his abdomen in a wave, rectus abdominis tightening segment by segment and letting go, and I put my palm flat on him below the costal margin to feel the last of it, and he was warm. I had known he would be warm. I had known it for weeks, the way you know a fact about a country you have read about. It is a different thing to put your hand on the country.

"Well-healed linear surgical scar, right lower quadrant, approximately six centimetres, consistent with appendicectomy."

"I was nine."

"History is not usually available," I said, and it was true, and it was the funniest thing I had ever said at work, because fourteen hundred patients and not one of them had ever told me where it hurt.

I went on. Nails clean, short, filed square; he did them himself, and well, the cuticles pushed back. The pads of the distal phalanges of both hands broadened and flattened, with a fine callus at the fingertips of the right, consistent with forty years of keyboard. Chest hair sparse, going to ash at the sternum. No tattoos. No injection marks. He had showered before I came, and shaved, and there was a small nick at the angle of his jaw where the razor had caught, already sealed, the first wound of the night and his own work. I graded him for it in my head. Preparation: excellent. He had dressed for me too.

The forearm first. I had planned it for weeks and I am going to tell you why, because you will want to know and you won't want to ask. It was his hands. I had stood in his house and touched one key of his piano, and it had been in tune, and I had left before I could do anything else, and I had been thinking about the machinery ever since: what moves when a man like that sits down at an instrument, what it is in him that knows where the notes are in the dark.

I set the blade to the volar surface of his left forearm a hand's width above the wrist crease and drew it towards the elbow, ten centimetres, through the epidermis and the dermis and the thin pale web of the superficial fascia, and the skin opened behind the blade the way skin does, not like cloth but like a mouth that has decided to. And he bled.

I need you to understand about that. The dead don't bleed. The dead seep. There is no pressure behind them; what comes out of a decedent comes out the way ink comes out of a cheap biro left on its side, a slow, sullen pooling, gravity's work and nobody else's. He bled like a fountain pen, which is to say with intent. A bead stood up along the whole length of the line at once, bright as enamel, and the beads found each other and became a line, and the line overflowed and ran around the curve of his forearm onto my towel, and it was so warm that I felt it through the glove before it had properly reached the glove. Thirty-seven degrees. I had never in my life put my hand into anything at thirty-seven degrees. Everything I touch arrives at four.

It smelled of iron, but not the morgue's iron. Old blood smells of a coin that has been in somebody's mouth. His smelled of the coin being struck. Only then did I look, the way I always look last, and it was the colour of the inside of a cherry, and it was moving.

I put in a small retractor and opened the window, and there was palmaris longus lying over everything like a guitar string. Fourteen per cent of people don't have one. I had wondered. Of course he had one; he'd have had a spare in a drawer. I eased it aside, and flexor carpi radialis with it, and beneath them in the distal forearm the four tendons of flexor digitorum superficialis lay side by side in their sheaths, glossy, pale, the colour of the inside of a shell.

"Play something," I said.

He turned his head and looked at his hand. "I can't see the keys."

"You've never needed to."

He played a chord against the wood of the desk. I don't know what chord it was. Something with a lot of fingers in it. And under my forceps the tendons slid, each one drawing back into the forearm and coming forward again on its own errand, four cables in four lift shafts, each one taking its finger down to the wood and letting it up, and through the window I had made in him I saw the actual cords of music, the strings it is played on, which nobody sees, which the men who invented music never saw, and I said, "That is beautiful," and it was. I have never meant a word more. I am not going to use it again. You will have to take that one and keep it somewhere.

"Again," I said, and he played it again, and I watched, and I could have watched all night, and that was the problem with the night, and I knew it, and I picked up the larger blade.

There is a reason it is a Y and not a T. A Y keeps the neck clean. The family will want to view the neck. Nobody was going to come and view his neck, but I am a creature of habit and so I gave him one he could have been viewed in. I started at the right acromion, where the bone comes up under the skin like a knuckle, and came down beneath the swell of the pectoral to the xiphoid process, the little cartilage tongue at the foot of the sternum; then from the left acromion, to meet it; and then down the midline, the dermis parting and the fat beneath it standing up yellow on either side, curving around the umbilicus to the left the way we are all taught, for reasons nobody can remember, and on down to the pubis.

He made sounds. I am going to be exact about them, because they deserve it. They were not screams. A scream is a decision; it needs the diaphragm and the intercostals and the will. What came out of him was below decision: glottal, involuntary, the vocal folds closing and opening on air the body was pushing out of its own accord, a sound like a heavy door being tried from the other side. The room kept every one of them. That was what he had paid for. In another building those sounds would have gone into the walls and through them and into a neighbour's ear and a neighbour's phone. In this one they went nowhere. They stayed with us. I could almost hear them settle on the carpet.

I took the upper flap of the Y between finger and thumb, laid the blade nearly flat and began to reflect it, undercutting in short strokes, the way you skin a fish when you want the fillet whole. Under the skin, the subcutaneous fat, butter-yellow, and on it the blood beading up from a thousand small vessels like condensation on a glass, except that it was the reverse of condensation; it was the warm thing, sweating into the cool air of the suite. Under the fat, pectoralis major in its fan, the fibres running from the sternum and the clavicle and converging on the arm, the colour of the flesh of a plum, and it moved. It moved when he breathed. On my table it never moves.

I lifted the flap in my hand to feel what it weighed. A living weight. About what a good rib-eye weighs on the board before you salt it, and the heat of him came up through it and through the nitrile into my palm like something being handed to me across a table.

I would like to report my own findings now, since nobody else was going to. I put two fingers to his carotid and counted, and while I counted his I listened to mine, which I could hear clearly enough in that room without touching anything. His was ninety-six. Mine was a hundred and ten. One of us was having a harder night than the other, and it was not, physiologically speaking, him. My face was hot. There was a heaviness lower down, a frank, wet wanting, and I am putting it in the same column as his sweat, because it was the same finding: a body that had understood what the night was before the person inside it had admitted anything.

A drop of sweat had come out at his hairline and was running towards his eye. My gloves were not fit to touch him with, so I wiped it away with the back of my wrist, the one clean inch between the cuff and the blood, and he closed his eyes while I did it, the way a man closes his eyes when a woman touches his face. Of everything I did that night, that was the most obscene. I would like that noted.

I knew what came next, because I know what bodies do. Shock does what shock does. The skin goes pale and cold and slick as the blood is called in from the edges to keep the centre alive; the pulse climbs and thins until it is a thread you have to hunt for; and the voice goes early, because the voice is a luxury, and the body in shock is closing its luxuries one by one, like a house shutting its rooms for the winter. Fourteen hundred cases had taught me that, every one of them after the fact. I had planned the rest of the night around his silence. I had, if I am honest, looked forward to it, the way you look forward to the point in a dinner when the other person stops talking and you can eat.

"You're very good at this," he said. His voice was level. "I've been trying to think what it's like, and it's like watching someone who really knows her instrument, when you can tell from the first bar that she isn't going to make a mistake, and you can stop worrying and just listen."

It was a whole sentence. It had a subordinate clause in it, and then another, and it arrived where it had been going. I hadn't expected him to be able to. I slowed down so he could.

* * *

He talked the way he must have talked in the rooms where men paid him to, in sentences that knew where they were going before they set out, and I worked at the speed of the sentences.

"Do you know what the job is?" he said. "People think it's lying. It isn't. Lying is for amateurs and candidates. The job is making sure that nobody ever has to decide anything. Something happens. By the time it reaches anyone who could do something about it, it isn't a thing any more. It's a document, and the document is a matter, and a matter is something you manage, and nobody has ever had to look at a thing that is being managed. Nobody decides. That's the product. I was very good at it, and I want you to know I knew what it was the whole time."

"I know."

"Of course you do. You wrote it down."

I opened the anterior rectus sheath along its length. It is a glossy white aponeurosis, tough as the skin of a drum, and under it rectus abdominis lay in its segments, the tendinous intersections crossing it like the scoring on a bar of chocolate. Every man who has ever paid a trainer has paid for those lines. Here were his, and nobody was going to see them but me. I went down the linea alba, which is the body's seam, the place it was closed up before it was born, and which like every seam is where the garment gives most willingly. I went as far as the transversalis fascia and the soft fat under it and I stopped there, with the peritoneum shining beneath my fingertips like the skin on hot milk, unbroken.

I could have opened it. If I had opened it the conversation would have been over inside ten minutes, and I was not ready for the conversation to be over. If you are keeping a list, and I think by now you are, put that near the top.

He talked about himself, because I let him and because he wanted to. About the house, which had been too quiet for two years and which he had filled with records the way people leave a radio on for a dog. About his wife, without unkindness and without her name, which I noticed, and which I would have done too. About the Tabard Club.

"It's Tuesday," he said. "I'm missing my set."

"They'll get the house pianist."

"The house pianist plays like he's being paid by the note."

I laughed out loud. In that room it sounded like somebody else's laugh, played back slightly too close to the microphone. I picked up my glass, which I should not have done in that glove, and drank. It was good rye, dry and spiced like the crust of a dark loaf, and the iron in the air went into it, and the two tastes sat on my tongue together, and I would not have separated them for money. My mouth was watering. It had been for some time. I am reporting that. I am not going to explain it.

"You broke the Dolphy," he said.

"Yes."

"*Out to Lunch.* It was a first pressing."

"I chose it for the sleeve. There's a clock on it with too many hands."

That pleased him; I watched it please him, a small loosening at the corners of the eyes. "Do you know how he died?"

"No."

"Berlin, nineteen sixty-four. He was thirty-six. He collapsed on tour and they took him to a hospital, and the doctors looked at what he was and what he did for a living and decided he'd taken something, and they let him sleep it off. He was diabetic. Nobody tested him. He died of sugar."

I know that death. I have signed it, in other names. A coma that a finger-prick would have caught, a test that costs less than a sandwich, not run, because by the time the body was on the trolley everyone in the room had already decided what kind of body it was. Neither of us said what it rhymed with. He had chosen the anecdote. I would like you to notice that: a dying man with his rectus sheath open, choosing his material. I gave him full marks for it in my head before I could stop myself, and then I let myself.

"I told him," he said. "After you left me the page. I went to Holt and I told him there was a problem from the old files."

"What did he say?"

"He said, 'I thought Diane closed those.' Then he asked me what I thought of the numbers in the suburbs. The next morning I couldn't get into half the things I'd built, and nobody had told me. That's how he does it. I've watched him do it to other men for twenty years. You don't get fired. Your furniture gets moved, a little at a time, and one day you go to sit down and there's nothing there."

I had closed Diane. I didn't say that either. He was the first man in twenty years to tell me something true without counsel present, and he was doing it with his abdominal wall open to the lamp, and I was not going to interrupt him with a joke, even a good one.

"He takes one minute alone before every speech," he said. "His brother's photograph. Mark. He sits with it, or he stands with it, I've never been in the room, nobody has. The detail waits outside the door. Standing instruction. Nobody goes in, and nobody knocks, and nobody has ever asked him why, because everybody knows why, and it's the best thing about him, and it's true." He stopped for air. I waited for him. "Watch the old footage. Sixty seconds. He's never gone over." And then, as if he were handing me a key across a counter: "That's your door. It's the only one."

"Thank you," I said.

I meant it. I want to be exact about how I meant it: the way you mean it when you have come into a shop for one thing and the man behind the counter goes into the back without being asked and comes out with it in his hands.

I touched my side. The wound from the contractor's knife is on the left flank, below the ribs, and it had been quiet all night, and I touch it when I am thinking. My hands were not fit, so I pressed it with the inside of my forearm, the bare skin above the glove against the shirt above the scar, and held it there, and thought.

Something had changed in my hands, and I hadn't told them to. I had been cutting. Sharp dissection, the blade doing the separating, quick and clean. At some point I had put the blade down on the towel by his hip without deciding to, and now I was using my fingers. Blunt dissection: easing the planes apart along their own grain, the way you separate two pages that have been stuck together when you want to read both sides. It is slower. It does less damage. It is what you do when the tissue is something you mean to keep. Brandt, oral component: distinction.

I had my way into the room. It had come from the man I was taking apart, and I was taking him apart more gently now, and I noticed, and I didn't stop.

* * *

He began to go a little after one. I read it the way I would read a chart. The colour left his face first, and the skin of his forehead took on the sheen of tallow; his lips went the colour of putty; the pulse under my two fingers had climbed to a hundred and thirty and thinned to a cord. His breathing was faster and shallower, as if he were trying to get it done before something closed. I had used seven towels. I counted them, the old way, wrung weight against dry, and put him at a litre and a half, give or take what the carpet had kept for itself. A class two haemorrhage going on three. His sentences were getting shorter. They were still complete.

"There's one more thing," he said. "Then you can finish."

"All right."

"Three years ago a woman tried to get in touch with you."

I had the rib shears in my hand by then. I had moved to the chest, because the abdomen was done and the night was not, and I had set the jaws to the costal cartilages on the left, second and third, a centimetre in from the bone, the way I do it every working day.

"She left you a message. At your office. You never called her back."

"How do you know."

"I was watching her too."

He said her name. The shears went through the fourth cartilage at that moment with the sound they make, which is the sound of a stick of celery, and the name went under it. I heard that he had said a name. I am not going to give it to you here. You will have it when I had it, and not before, which seems fair. The air went into his left chest with a small, polite hiss, and the lung on that side let go of the wall and fell away from my fingers, and he took a breath with half of what he'd had, and used it.

"She died eight months later." He swallowed. "I'm sorry. I thought you should know."

They were the last complete sentences he said, and they were complete: subject, verb, object. He even got the contraction in. Three hours earlier he had told me there were eleven names, and watched me learn that I had never been alone, and been kind about it. This was the same kindness. I recognised it, in the way you recognise a voice in the next room without going in.

I finished. I took the cartilages on the right, second to seventh, and opened the sternoclavicular joints with the blade, the way you joint a chicken at the thigh: find the place, and it gives. I lifted the sternal plate away and laid it on the towel by his shoulder, and the right lung went down like the left, and the talking stopped for good. Not because he had decided to stop. Because the bellows were open to the room.

His heart was in its sac, the pericardium, fibrous and pale, and it was moving under it the way something moves under a sheet. I opened the sac with one stroke. And then I took off my right glove.

I want to be exact about this, because it is the only thing I did that night that was not in the plan. I peeled the glove down off my fingers inside out and dropped it on the towel, and I put my bare hand into his chest and took his heart in it. It was hot. It was slick and heavy and alive and much harder than you would think, a fist of muscle working against my palm, and as I held it the beat broke up and became something else, a shivering, a writhing, what the textbooks call a bag of worms, and the textbooks are right; the textbooks were written by people who had had their hands where mine was. Fibrillation. I held it through all of it. It went on for longer than you would believe, and then it slowed, and then it was still, and then his heat began to leave it, under my skin, a degree at a time. Everything I have ever touched had already done that before it reached me. I had never felt it go.

Three hundred and eighty grams, I thought, give or take. My watch said ten to two. It was Wednesday. He had missed his set altogether.

I closed him the way they close them at work when the family isn't coming: quickly, without sutures, the sternal plate set back, the flaps folded home, a tidying that is also, if you watch it often enough, a kind of love that nobody is there to see. Then the tape. Black vinyl, two strips, across his eyes. He had watched the whole time. I had let him. They were not going to close by themselves, and it seemed only polite to close them for him.

The page was on the table beside the photograph, folded in quarters, with a ring on it where he had been using it as a coaster. I chose to take that as a compliment. It was his own copy, the one I had left on his pillow beside the broken record, and he had kept it and read it until the folds had gone soft. I opened it out on his chest, smoothed it with my bare hand, and pinned it through the skin above the sternum. The marker squeaked in that room like a mouse. I circled THE ARCHITECT. The Evaluation was where I had typed it two years ago, under my own redactions. MISTOOK CLARITY FOR CRUELTY. It still read well. It had held.

Then the exit, which is the part nobody admires. The instruments into their roll, the blades into their sleeve, the rib shears last. The towels into the bags, double-bagged. My glass, with the print of my mouth on its rim. The right glove from the towel, and then I peeled off the left and put it in with its brother. The photograph of Diane I left where he had kept it. The Mingus sleeve I left beside it. The record was still on the platter, side one finished, the label turning under no needle. I didn't turn it over. I have never owned a record. I wouldn't have known how to hold it.

I opened the suite door with my elbow. When it unsealed, the corridor's ventilation came in on me like weather, a whole building's worth of moving air, and after that room it was like surfacing. I took the stairwell door with my hip and the door to the car park with my back, the way you go through doors in a hospital when you are scrubbed, and I didn't think about why, because my body was thinking about it for me and I was busy.

I was elated. I am telling you that plainly because I want it on the record before anything else is: I was elated. I came down the stairs two at a time with the bags knocking against my knee. The suite was in my hair, the vetiver and the rye and the warm dust, and him, and I could smell it every time I turned my head, and I turned my head more than I needed to. There was a chicken in my fridge that had been in brine since the day I cooked, and I had been saving it for tonight: out of the brine, patted dry, the oven as hot as it goes, the skin. I would sit down at my own table, for once, and eat half of it with my fingers. I want you to know I was thinking about the chicken. It was the best night of my life, and I was elated, and I was hungry, and I told myself the hunger was physiology, and I believed me.

I was in the car before the elation reached the part of me that had heard what he said.

The Mingus sleeve on his table, *The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady* still on the platter, side two never played. My hands on the wheel, red to the wrist. I had not noticed.


# Chapter 18

My hands were on the wheel, and now I looked at them.

The blood had changed colour since the suite. On the backs of both hands it had gone from the inside of a cherry to oxblood, and it had dried first where it lay thinnest, so that it was setting in the creases of my knuckles the way grout sets, darker in the lines than on the tiles. The right was worse, still tacky past the wrist where the glove had come off for the heart. There were prints on the rim of the wheel at ten and two, because I drive at ten and two, like a woman who has never once been pulled over. A thumb on the gear knob, the whorl clear enough to classify. I sat in the printworks' rear car park at a quarter past two and read my own car the way a scene examiner reads a kitchen, and it was the first time that night I had seen myself as evidence.

The tub of seventy per cent isopropyl wipes lives in the door pocket, as it has in every car I have owned. One wipe for the wheel, one for the gear knob, one for the indicator stalk, one for the inner door handle, each folded in on itself and bagged with the towels. It was a stopgap. The belt, the key, the outside handle: those would wait for the car's own night. Alcohol fixes blood; it doesn't lift it. The solvent bit my nose and went up behind my eyes, and the tub went into my jacket pocket with the phone. Then I pulled a fresh pair of nitrile on over the blood, left and then right.

I did not clean my hands. I covered them. Those are different procedures, and I know which one goes in the report.

The drive is thirty minutes. Sodium by the river, then the blue-white of the ring road, which makes everyone under it look as if they are waiting to be identified, and I kept to the limit exactly. The elation was still in me like a second drink, but it had stopped reaching my face. Under it, on a loop, his voice. *She died eight months later.* And before it, a name. I could not hear the name. I could hear exactly where it had been, the way your tongue can find where a tooth was.

Procedure is that the bag inside the bag goes where it goes. I put the bags down inside the front door, beside my shoes, like shopping, and walked away from them.

I went into the bathroom, turned on the fluorescent over the basin, and did not turn on the shower. I sat down on the tile with my back against the side of the bath, in the clothes I had worn to the suite, my knees up and my gloved hands on them, and I did not get up for six hours.

The weather had turned while I was inside, the first cold night. Tile is the only honest surface in a flat. It tells you exactly what temperature you are. It drew the heat out of me through my thighs and my palms, and the enamel of the bath drew it out of my spine, and I knew the cold when it came the way you know someone's step on the stair. It was the old cold, the one I had thought was gone.

I began to shiver. I reported it to myself as rigors, and then I corrected the report, because rigors want a fever and I had none. This was shivering thermogenesis: skeletal muscle contracting against itself to make heat, a sign that the body has not yet given up on the house.

That was the last clean sentence I had for a while.

Then the diaphragm went. I know the words for the rest. The vocal folds closing and opening on air the body is pushing out of its own accord, a sound below decision. I had used those words a few hours before, about him. I heard that they were the same words.

It went on.

I didn't stop it. I didn't try.

My nose ran and I wiped it on my sleeve.

It went on for a long time.

The suite had kept every sound he made and given none of them back.
