My bathroom is tile and enamel and a frosted window, and it gave back every one of mine, a fraction late, so that I heard myself the way he never got to hear himself in that room. I have attended a great many bathroom floors professionally. It is where the body goes when it wants to do something without a witness, and the body is always wrong about that.

And under it, the whole time, the thing I couldn't stop seeing. Not him. Me. I played it back the way you scrub footage, a frame at a time and in order, because order was what I had. The smell of the open chest first, warm iron and the butcher's-counter sweetness of opened meat, with the vetiver over it. Then the fourth costal cartilage going under the shears with the sound of celery, and a name going under it. The lung on that side letting go of the wall. *I'm sorry. I thought you should know.* And I had been happy. I need to be exact. I was happy while he said it, in the same seconds, with the shears in my hand: my pulse loud in my ears, my mouth watering, the heat low down that I had put in the same column as his sweat. Then the heart in my bare palm, and a thought in my own voice: three hundred and eighty grams, give or take. Then the stairs, two at a time. Then the chicken. I had been thinking about the chicken.

I did not grade any of it. There was nothing to write in the margin.

The cistern ticked. At some point I peeled the gloves off inside out, the way I had taken off the right one in the suite, and let them lie on the tile. Nitrile doesn't breathe. The blood under it had never dried; sweat had held it soft and pink and slick, and the skin beneath was pruned. In the air it dried at last, and when I made a fist it cracked along the knuckle creases and flaked the way paint flakes off a wet-room ceiling. At seven, through the wall, someone's shower started. Nobody would ring from work; I had booked the day off on the Friday, because I plan the day after the way I plan the meal after. My hair was against my face and the suite was in it, vetiver and rye and warm dust and him, with the isopropyl sharp on top. My left flank pulled where I was folded against the bath, the stitches two days out. I pressed it once with the heel of my hand. I didn't think anything. The frosted window went from black to dishwater, and the mirror was over the basin above my head the whole time, and I did not look up into it.

At about ten to nine I could stand, and I stood facing the door. The first thing I reached for was my phone, in its foil sleeve in my jacket pocket.

Not to call anyone.

* * *

I slid it out of the foil, and when I turned it over there I was on the glass: a thumb, and the side of a forefinger, ridges up, as legible as any print I have watched the lab lift off a decedent's spectacles. My hands had been on my wet face, and the blood on them had remembered it was blood. Procedure came back all at once, the way feeling comes back into a foot. I laid the phone face-down on the rim of the bath. I washed my hands at the basin, nails and webs and backs and up past the wrists, and then my face, the water running rust and then rose and then clear, my eyes on the plughole. I dried my hands and face. I stepped out of the suite clothes onto the tile and left them there, and put on a clean shirt. I cleaned the phone, and the inside of the foil, with a wipe from the tub in my pocket, and put the foil with the bags. Before I knew anything, I had been careful. You may make of the order what you like.

The flat was cold. The heating was off, and I left it off. My feet were bare on the boards and my knuckles were raw where the blood had cracked and the nailbrush had finished the job. I sat at the writing desk with the locked drawer under my knees and opened the county voicemail app, and then the folder I had never opened. Archive.

Two messages.

I keep everything. Mara once said I would archive a sneeze. In my years of county service I have returned every call I was ever left, or saved it, and I have pressed 7 exactly twice, and both times I was busy. Of all the statistics I have compiled for the county, that is the most damning.

The top one: Wednesday 30 September, 11:38 a.m., forty-seven seconds. Marked as read. Fourteen days old to the day. I did not play it. I knew what was on it: a woman saying sorry twice, men outside her house, and behind her a dog shifting its claws on a hard floor.

The second: 17 September, three years ago, 4:52 p.m. Thirty-four seconds. Marked as read.

I played it.

A breath first. Behind the breath an office: a phone ringing at somebody else's desk, a door. Then a woman's voice, young, careful with itself, coming small out of the speaker into a cold room.

"My name's Nora Wade."

There it was. I heard it in her own mouth, and I heard it at the same moment a second time, under the sound of celery breaking.

"I think we may have..."

She stopped. Once. I had heard a woman stop like that before, on a concrete floor, and I knew what it cost to get past it.

"...known the same person. I just wanted to talk."

Then a number, said slowly, for someone who might be writing it down. I was not writing it down. I have never rung it.

I know the rest of that day without remembering it, the way you know the route to work. I played the message the next morning, between cases. I pressed 7. I ate lunch, because I always eat lunch. I do not know what it was. I can tell you everything I have eaten since August. I cannot tell you what I ate on the day I put that woman in a folder. That is the finding.

On the clean laptop, never the phone, I searched her name. It took one page. An obituary in a newspaper in another county: Nora Elizabeth Wade, twenty-seven, died suddenly at home on the twenty-first of May the following year. Eight months. He had been exact. *Suddenly* is the word families use at twenty-seven when the answer is one of two things. I have signed both, many times, and spelled them correctly. I would not pick. I could have asked that county for the file. A request is a document, and documents are read. I closed the laptop.

I unlocked the drawer and took out the master of Lesson Two. *Excellent.* in red beside the subject line, in my hand. Below it *Tessa Quinn* in black, struck through with one line, ruled against the edge of his dossier. I laid the page beside the phone, the struck name next to the two messages, and looked at the three of them together, and it was very simple, the way the bad ones are always simple once the bloods come back.

I did it twice.

She had been one of them. Brandt had said eleven names, and I had counted myself in and stopped counting. A list of eleven had no room on it for a woman who had been dead two years and more. She had known the same person, and signed what I signed, and found the one other woman she could find, and rung her at work. The other woman was me. I had spent two years writing pages about the cabinet, and I had been one of its drawers. I had been running it. And I had been eating well the whole time.

I can tell you what I ate after the second one. Cold lamb, the fat set white and melting on the tongue, the salsa verde sharp with caper and anchovy and too much lemon, eaten sitting down at work, with the fork I keep in my desk drawer there, every mouthful. It was as good as it had been the night before. Fourteen days on, I could still itemise it.

The red master of Lesson One was in the drawer under my hand. I didn't take it out. I wanted to know whether I still had it by heart. I had. *Function: to convert the complaint into the file, the file into the folder, the folder into the cabinet, the cabinet into the room no one enters.* Key 7. The archive. The folder nobody opens. I wrote that page two years ago with the first message already in the folder. That is all I am going to say about it.

My stomach made a long, plain noise in the quiet. Borborygmi. I noted it and stayed where I was.

I had been the Gatekeeper. My page would read: *Confused process for protection.*

I took a blank sheet from the printer tray. I wrote it in black ink, in the small upright hand I keep for case numbers, and the nib dragged a little on the paper, and my hand was steady all the way down, which I would have preferred it not to be. My name was on it. I did not circle anything. My desk red stayed capped in the pen tray.

I didn't pin it to anything.

* * *

In the afternoon I sat with the page face-up in front of me and did what I do with a body I don't yet understand, which is lay it out.

The syllabus is not justice. It is a way never to face that I could have helped, before, twice, and both times pressed a key.

That was the first thing it was. The second I am going to say plainly. It was a way to have what I wanted, and what I wanted was this, all of it. The weeks of planning, standing at the counter. The suite. The record turning. The volar skin of his forearm pushing back against the blade, the way nothing on my table ever has. The heat of him coming up through the nitrile at thirty-seven degrees. Flexor digitorum superficialis sliding in its sheath when he played. Pectoralis major moving when he breathed. His heart in my bare hand, working, and then not. I wanted his blood on my wrists. I wanted to be inside him with the lamp on. I got it. I have listed it the way I list findings and I will not take back a word of it.

Then I laid out the other thing, the way I laid out the syllabus, in steps.

Stop.

Do not destroy the kit. Bag it, seal it, label it and keep it, the way you keep a specimen for the other side's expert.

Go to the field office and ask at the desk for Special Agent E. Reyes, the signature quoted in counsel's covering email, which I had read on a Thursday at ten past one.

Let it come out in a courtroom, where nothing can be managed, because a matter stops being a matter when a judge is looking at it.

Let the other ten be believed. The dead one too.

I touched the scar through my shirt while I wrote the last step, because I was thinking.

I graded it honestly. It was the best plan I had ever written. It had only one flaw, which was that it ended.

I made tea. Getting the milk meant opening the fridge, and the chicken was on the bottom shelf in its brine, under a plate, waiting for the oven I had promised it. The skin had gone white and puckered and loose on it. In the trade it is washerwoman's skin. We see it on bodies pulled out of baths, and I know how bad a clock it is. I took the milk and closed the door.

I didn't eat.

I made the tea strong, with milk, and drank it standing at the window. It was too hot, and took a layer off the roof of my mouth; then it was tannic, the dry furred grip of it on my gums; then it was cold, and I finished it. The heat went out of the mug into my palms and out of my palms into the room, and the flat took it, and gave nothing back.

I turned my head and the suite was in my hair.

On the stairs I had turned my head more than I needed to. Now I did it on purpose, slowly, the way you smell a glass before you are allowed to drink it, and the vetiver came up, and the rye, and the warm dust, and under them him, and my mouth did what it had done in the suite. I wanted it. My body had decided before I had a sentence for it, the way it always does, and the sentence came after, like paperwork.

Not because the philosophy held. I had watched it fail on my own desk, in my own handwriting.

I was going to go on because I wanted to.

That is the most honest sentence in this book. Keep it next to the other word, the one from the suite. They are a pair.

Then I did what you do when you have decided. The clothes from the suite came up off the bathroom tile and went into the bags with the towels, not into my machine: heat sets blood, and a drum keeps everything it is given. The bags inside the front door went where they go. The instruments I cleaned at the sink and dried and rolled, the rib shears last, the way they had gone in, and then enzyme cleaner and peroxide down the sink and the basin, because I had run blood down both. The car would get peroxide and a night of its own. I did not wash my hair. I graded the exit: competent. I did not grade myself.

Early in the evening I dressed for the bar in the business hotel by the river, the one I had gone to after the fight. I got as far as the front door. My hand was near the deadbolt and the hall mirror was at my shoulder, and I did not look into it, and I did not go. Going meant washing the suite out of my hair. I wasn't finished with it.

I took the shoes off in the hall and went back to the desk.

Outside, the light went to sodium, block by block. My stomach made its noise again and I logged it as a finding: hungry, refused. I put the master of Lesson Two back in the drawer and locked it. I turned the page face-down on the desk and left it out. It isn't part of the syllabus, so it doesn't go in with the syllabus.

At twenty to seven the phone lit up beside it. Mara.

*Hope the day off was nice. Saved you a coffee, drank it for you. x*

I didn't reply.

The screen went dark on its own. I knew what was behind it: in the archive, the two voicemails side by side, thirty-four seconds and forty-seven, both marked as read. The page with my own name on it lay face-down on the desk.


# Chapter 19

It was Sunday, the twenty-fifth of October, early in the afternoon, and I was standing at the kitchen counter in a jumper and socks. The heating had been off since the small hours of the fourteenth and I had left it off, and the flat had settled at the temperature of a room nobody is expected back to. My phone lay face-up on the counter beside a mug of tea I had made at noon. The tea had gone tepid and then cold, and grown a skin of tannin that wrinkled when I breathed on it, a fine film the colour of weak liver, like the fibrin on a wound that has been left open to the air.

The syllabus had cost me everything outside it, and I could itemise the bill. I had, at least, kept the receipts.

Item. Hannah had called three times that week, on Monday, Wednesday and Friday, and left no message.

Item. Yesterday was June's sixth birthday, at Hannah's house. I did not go. I sent no word.

Item. On Saturday evening the phone had lit with a photograph and no caption. The table after the opening. Paper torn and heaped at one end, and June in the foreground holding a child's microscope in both hands, the way you hold something that might go off, with a little round mirror under the stage catching the light. On top of the heap of paper lay the gift tag, in Hannah's handwriting: *from Iris*. My sister had bought my present for her daughter, and signed my name to it in her own hand, and sent me the photograph so that I would know what I had given.

I spread two fingers on the glass, the way I open up a wound photograph to look at the margins. Hannah has wrapped presents the same way since she was nine, as if the paper might try to escape. Every seam had been taped twice over, and where June had torn through, the clear tape had held on and taken strips of paper with it, so that the heap was ridged with little glossy flanges standing up off it and catching the flash. I have occluded an airway with less tape than that, and mine held.

Item. Mara had stopped texting. The last one had come at twenty to seven on the evening of the fourteenth, about a coffee she had saved me and drunk for me, with a kiss after it, and I hadn't answered, and none had come since. At work that week she said "morning" to me each day, and meant morning. She took her coffee to her own desk. On Thursday we scrubbed at the sinks a basin apart, and she did not hum, and then she stripped her gloves off inside out, the way I taught her, and dropped them in the bin, and went. Those are the findings.

The tablet was propped against the fruit bowl, which had no fruit in it, and the War Room was on with the sound low. On a Sunday the War Room is a skeleton staff and the urn. Counsel stood at the glass with the city behind him and his phone to his ear, on a call about what he called the Bureau's records request. It was the second such call that week, by my count. At the far end of the table a young woman said "Reyes" to the boy beside her, in the voice people use for a diagnosis in somebody else's family. The request was still stalled. Counsel had written *Hold until after the third* at the top of the email that quoted her letter, and the third was nine days off. I have signed enough certificates to know that stalls end. That is the whole of my job, more or less: writing down how something that had been holding stopped.

Brandt had been found on the morning of Thursday the fifteenth, by staff from his own consultancy. The Chief did the post-mortem, and Mara assisted him. I was not asked. On the feed there was no vacant chair for the room to look at. The deputy comms director had been sitting in Brandt's seat at the end of the table since the eighth. The campaign carried it without breaking step.

The page with my name on it was face-down on the writing desk, where I had left it eleven days before. I had not turned it over since. I took my keys from the counter; the brass key lives on the ring with my car key. I unlocked the drawer, and it came out on its runners with its long wooden sigh. The third page was on top, still unpinned. I didn't take it out. I slid the fourth in under it, face-down, without turning it over. On the fourteenth I had refused to put it in with the syllabus, because it is not part of the syllabus. Now it was in with the syllabus. I locked the drawer, and put the keys in my pocket.

The work was the only thing I still wanted. I wanted it the way you want to finish a post-mortem you have stopped learning anything from: because the body is already open, and you have come this far, and the report is the only part of it you haven't written.

On the board by the toaster lay half a slice of toast from noon. The butter had set opaque on it in the cold, a waxy skin the colour of tallow, and the crumb under it had gone hard. I eat standing up when I'm planning. I had been standing up all week. This is not the same as eating. I picked the half up, and looked at it, and put it down.

At four o'clock the phone rang on the counter and said HANNAH.

I registered the hour before I read the name. Sunday at four is when Diane Pell called her mother, and on the thirteenth of September she hadn't.

On the first ring I pressed the scar through my jumper with the fingertips of my left hand. Then I put both hands flat on the counter on either side of the phone, and left them there. The laminate was cold. I could feel the heat going out of me through the heels of my hands, steadily, the way it goes out of a body into a steel table. Second ring. Third. Fourth. Then the small click of the call being taken somewhere else, by something that would listen to her when I wouldn't, and the screen said she had called, and went dark.

I knew what came next. A voicemail of any length is somebody talking into a quiet room. I stood in mine and watched the dark glass for a minute and a quarter, which is a long time to watch glass.

It lit. *Voicemail. Hannah. 1:14.* A name and a duration and no more, because I turned off transcription years ago. I don't like a machine reading my messages before I do.

I picked the phone up. My thumb went to the play arrow and stopped above it, a few millimetres off the glass, and stayed there. I hadn't decided anything. The thumb had.

I found that I couldn't listen to voicemails any more. There were two others, in the archive folder, thirty-four seconds and forty-seven, and I had played them both, and I knew to the second what each had cost. This one had gone into the same column before I could stop it. I put the phone back on the counter, face-up, with the message on it.

It is still there. I have never played it.

* * *

The laptop fan was the loudest thing in the flat.

That evening I stood at the writing desk with the clean laptop open on it and the desk lamp on, and every other light off. I plan standing. Sound first, for once, because there was so little else to go on: the fan's thin, continuous note, the radiator under the window not ticking, and the trackpad, which clicked under my finger like a tongue against the teeth.

The papers had come off the card from the night before: the advance team's run-of-show for Monday the second of November, a security annex behind it, and the floor plans behind that. I read the run-of-show first, top to bottom, the way I read a referral, without skipping to the interesting part, because in a referral the interesting part is wherever the person writing it thought it wasn't. The Calloway. The grand ballroom. Doors at six. The Senator's remarks at nine. At about ten to nine, the principal to the green room, a converted meeting suite off the ballroom corridor.

Then the annex, which was the fortress described from the inside by the people who had built it. A Secret Service perimeter around the building. Magnetometers at the ballroom doors. Hotel staff vetted, listed and badged. A press pool escorted from the riser to a holding room and back, like a patient between departments, and with about as much say. Layer under layer, each one there to stop the next one being reached: skin, then fascia, then muscle, then the peritoneum shining under all of it, and inside, the thing it was all for. Whoever designed it had either studied the peritoneum or been very frightened of something.

At the foot of the annex was its distribution list. Names, titles, a line each, and among them: *SA E. Reyes, FBI (campaign protection liaison).* I read it twice, as I had read her name in counsel's covering email, in my car, on a Thursday at ten past one. She would be in the building. There was still no page for Reyes. Now there wasn't going to be one.

I mapped it for the third time. The first map I drew two years ago, in the abstract, before there was a venue: a man in a room somewhere, with a door. The second was in the week after Brandt's suite, from the floor plans the hotel publishes for people planning weddings. This was the third, with the real run-of-show and the plans from the card, in pencil, on squared paper set square to the edge of the desk. My desk red stayed capped in the pen tray. I worked from the outside in, perimeter, magnetometer, badge, escort, and I tried every approach I had against each layer in turn, and every one of them ended at a man with an earpiece. I used the plans for the layers and for no more than that. Each layer held. I graded the security sound, and the grade gave me about as much pleasure as signing for a parcel.

By ten the side of my right hand was silver with graphite from the wrist to the little finger, where it had dragged across the paper. The only warmth in the room came up out of the laptop into the heels of my hands, and I kept them there.

I checked myself the way I check everything. Two fingers of my right hand on my left wrist, out of habit, and the radial pulse under them was fifty-eight, resting. My mouth was dry, the tongue tacky against the palate. The last time I had planned a body my mouth had watered for hours. I waited for the metal, the coin held too long at the back of the tongue, which had come every time the old injury surfaced. It didn't come.

For the first time in two years of planning, I could find nothing. Not dread. Not hunger. Paperwork.

There were two rallies. I had gone through every recording of him from the autumn that anybody had left uncut, station by station, looking for one angle, a door seen from the outside, and I found it twice. The first was Thursday the eighth of October, at a convention centre: the pool camera's raw feed, which a regional news site had posted whole, three hours of it, with the camera parked on a corridor while the crew waited, so that it caught the detail closing a door and taking up its position outside. The second was Thursday the twenty-second, at a university field house: a local station's raw livestream, which had started early and left its camera on a holding-area door for anyone with the patience to watch a door. I have never been to one of his rallies. I have only ever watched them on a screen. I had the patience.

I scrubbed both, frame by frame. The door shut. A hand flat on it from outside, then an agent's back to it. Then the player's counter ran, and its numbers were the only thing on the screen that moved. On the livestream, behind the door and the wall and the agent, I could hear the field house filling, a sound like surf with a PA over it, and over that the station's own microphone picking up somebody's cough. Sixty seconds, give or take a frame. The door opened and he came out already smiling. Then the other rally, and the same minute, as near as a frame lets you say. He had never gone over.

When the second door opened I touched the scar through my jumper.

Detail out, door shut, sixty seconds. Brandt's gift, confirmed.

I thought it in a whole sentence, with a subject and a verb and an object, the way he would have said it.

I went to the bathroom and pulled the cord, and the light over the mirror came on hard and white. I hadn't looked into that mirror since the morning of the fourteenth, when I washed my face with my eyes on the plughole. I looked now. I said the sentence aloud to the woman in it, to find out whether it sounded like a joke.

"He told me the truth while I was inside him."

It didn't.

* * *

I stayed where I was. The tube over the basin is a strip of flat white light like the one over intake at work, and it does to everyone what intake does: it lays them out, takes the colour out of the lips, and finds every shadow under the eyes and makes a note of it.

Smell first. The bathroom smelled of unscented soap and, under the soap, faintly, of chlorhexidine and cold iron, the smell of work, which lives in my skin and which no shower has ever removed. Then of something else, close, and new. I cupped my hand over my mouth and nose and breathed out into it and breathed in, and there it was. Pear drops. Nail varnish. The sweetish solvent note of acetone, coming off my own breath. I know it from a hundred decedents, the diabetics and the starved, rising off them when the bag is unzipped. Ketosis. My body had run out of what I was giving it and had started on its own stores. In my professional experience, that is how you know a body has started on the furniture.

I looked for a long time. The woman from August was not there: Hannah's sister, who ate two sandwiches in a park and wanted the third, and Mara's mentor, whom Mara watched the way people watch a chef through the pass. I am not being dramatic. I am describing a change of tenant. The person in the mirror was a replacement.

I took off my jumper, and then the rest, and put them on the lid of the laundry basket. The tiles were cold under my bare feet, and the cold came up through the soles into the bones of the ankle. Gooseflesh rose along my upper arms as the arrector pili took hold of each fine hair and stood it up, a reflex older than clothes, doing its best, in its small furred way, to keep me warm with a coat I haven't had for a long time.

I drew breath to dictate, in the voice I use for the dictaphone, and the sentence was there already, the one I have said over nearly every body I have ever examined: *the body is that of a well-nourished adult female*. I got as far as the *well*, in my head, and had to amend the report.

"The body is that of an adequately nourished adult female, consistent with the stated age." I said the whole sentence, and it held.

The clavicles stood proud, two struts with a shadow under each, and the suprasternal notch between them had deepened into a pit you could have pooled a teaspoon of water in. The anterior superior iliac spines showed at the front of the pelvis, a knuckle of bone either side, where for years there had been a soft margin. On Thursday the twenty-second I had put a new hole in my belt with the point of a scalpel blade. I'd like to say it was the only tool to hand. It was the only tool I trusted. About four kilos since the start of October, I put it, by the belt and the clavicles. I have not stood on a scale. I didn't need to. The skin over my knuckles was dry and cracked from the nitrile, fine fissures across each joint like the glaze on an old plate.

The scar is on the left flank, below the tip of the twelfth rib. It was a little over three weeks old: pink, still raised, a line of three point two centimetres with seven pairs of small marks, one of each pair either side of it, where the nylon had gone in and come out, not yet silvered. With the fat gone from around it, it sat closer to the rib than it used to. I put my fingertips on it. It was the only part of me that hadn't changed its opinion of me.

Periorbital darkening, the skin under each eye gone to slate. I pulled down my left lower lid with a fingertip. The conjunctiva should be the pink of the inside of a cheek; mine was nearer bone than rose. Haemoglobin down, or on its way: the flank's three hundred millilitres, and not much put back since. I put out my tongue, and it had a white coat on it, thicker towards the back. I have never written *very tired* on anybody's form. It isn't a finding, it's an opinion. I wrote it on mine.

Then I held both hands out flat in front of me, palms down, fingers spread, the way I would ask a patient to if I had ever had one who could. No tremor. Not in the fingertips, not at the wrist. They were as steady as they have been since my second year of residency.

I looked at her the way somebody else would have to. Not a pathologist. A witness, being asked by a woman with a notebook, because paper is what Reyes has. Height: somebody's guess. Build: slight, and slighter than last month. The jaw. The widow's peak and the parting, which a stranger had once been chosen for. The hands. I read it back to myself, and it would have fitted on half a page, with room for the signature.

The replacement was competent, very tired, and had lost weight. I had graded my lies, my plans, my exits and most of the people I had ever stood in a room with. I had written her an Evaluation once, on a page now face-down in a drawer. I had never given her a mark. Competent. It is the mark you give a resident who has done everything right and ought to be sent home.

Then I checked the replacement for flaws the way I would check a body on the table: the scalp, the face, the neck, the chest, the abdomen, the flanks, the limbs, the backs of the hands and the soles of the feet, region by region, in order, omitting nothing but the back, which nobody can examine for herself. I found them. A split at the left angle of the mouth, which is what a body does when it has been going short for a while. A bruise on the right shin that I could not account for. I didn't look away.

Dressed again, in the kitchen, I watched the phone on the counter light once, Hannah's voicemail still waiting under her name, and I turned it face-down. Then I opened the fridge and stood in its light, with the chicken on the bottom shelf under its plate and the cold coming out over my bare feet, and closed it.


# Chapter 20

The weak point was not the Secret Service. I want that down first, because it took me three maps to see it, and when I did see it I was standing at my own kitchen table at ten on a Friday night with a cup of coffee gone cold at my elbow. The Service guards the man. That is the whole of its brief, and it is very good at it. Nobody guards the paperwork about the people who stand near him.

The plans had come off the printer at the desk still warm, and I had carried them through against my chest and laid them out edge to edge under the one bulb, the way you lay films on a lightbox, so that the floors met at the stairs. They smelled of hot toner, dry and scorched and faintly sweet, the smell of a library photocopier at closing time. They were the only warm thing in the flat. The heating had been off since the night of the suite. I put my palm flat on the top sheet before I read it, and took it off again when the warmth went, which was quickly.

Everything on the table had come off the card I brought out of Lantern the Saturday before, after dark, on the cloned badge and the midline. The farm was humming in its warehouse again since the abuse desk's review, copying the campaign to itself for a dead man under the signature of a dead woman, and my tap was still on its uplink, sending me the thin summary of the campaign's mail, near enough live.

The credentials first. The Service screens bodies and bags. It does not make badges. Back-of-house press and volunteer credentials were the campaign's own business, printed on the campaign's own badge printer in the volunteer office, from one template, and checking who had been issued what against who ought to have been was an audit, and the audit was Compliance's job. I opened the file. *Last modified by D. Pell.* Friday 11 September, the Friday before the reservoir. Nobody had changed a cell of it since. In the War Room they said Compliance had been dead two months. That was rounding up. It had been seven weeks. I graded the audit anyway, out of habit: thorough, current to the day it stopped, and stopped. Fail, for reasons beyond the candidate's control.

The template was in the card's copy of the volunteer drive, and so was the roster. I went down it slowly, the way I go down a list of medications, looking for the one nobody is taking. Ruth Calder. Signed up in July, worked one phone bank, made eleven calls, never came back. Still marked active, because marking people inactive had also been Compliance. Ruth had made eleven calls and gone home. I liked her. She knew when to stop.

The credential desk's instructions were in the same drive. The door list for Monday would be printed from every name marked active, sorted by surname, and the desk was to find the name and tick it. I changed nothing in their database. I printed. My own card printer was secondhand and paid for in cash. The white PVC in its hopper came from the supplier on the campaign's own invoice, over a trade counter, on the Monday, for cash. It took a blank on a small ratchet of rollers and gave it back to me warm: the navy-and-gold header, the gold band along the foot that means back of house, and three lines. RUTH CALDER. VOLUNTEER. PRESS ASSISTANT. No photograph.

At eleven the tablet gave me counsel's email to the War Room, sent that afternoon. The subpoena fight was lost. The signatory list was to be produced on Saturday. Special Agent E. Reyes would have eleven names by the weekend. I read it twice and gave it a B: clear, short, and dishonest only in the word *regrettably*, which a man uses when he means expensive. I found my fingertips on my left flank, through the jumper, on the ridge of the scar below the twelfth rib, and left them there while I did the only arithmetic there was. Ruth Calder had made eleven calls. Numbers do that. They don't mean anything by it. I didn't think about the other ten. I thought about Monday.

The Calloway files floor plans for its ballroom level, on three, and the advance team had a copy. I read it the way I read a body. Hotels are built like people, a face for the guests and the plumbing behind it, and I have spent my whole career in the plumbing. In front, the grand ballroom, the foyer, the press riser. Behind, Stair C, a service stair beside the green-room corridor, its door about ten paces from the green-room door. One floor down, on two, a housekeeping closet the hotel's plan marked open during functions, below the sweep, which the security annex gave as the third floor and his route in. At street level on the cross street, the loading dock, with a vendor gate beside the shutter and a green button inside it, on a timer. I traced the route with the desk red marker from the pen tray, capped, the way you point at an X-ray without touching the film.

Then the minute, as a protocol, standing, in pencil. Sixty seconds, from the footage. The door closes. The detail turns its back, a hand flat on the wood. Count to five, for him to sit down with the photograph. After that I wrote nothing, because what came after was the lesson, and the lesson had been written for two years.

The green room was the Alder Suite. The annex showed one way in. The hotel's plan, drawn for banquet staff and not for anybody with an earpiece, showed two: the corridor door, and a narrow pantry door onto the back corridor, so the coffee could come in unseen. On the annex the second door was a hatched line with one word against it. *Sealed.* Eight paces of back corridor from the stair door to the hatched line; with the ten from the green-room door, twelve seconds at a working walk. I wrote that down and didn't use it. I had sixty. Then I found the annex's walk-through photographs, taken by the advance team to show the post what each door would look like on the night, and found that corridor, and that door, and enlarged it to four hundred per cent until the grain of the paint came up like skin under a hand lens.

A white label ran across the gap between the door and the frame at shoulder height, corridor side.

I knew it before I could read it. It is the same make of tamper-evident seal I put across the mouth of every evidence bag at work, initialled across the join, the kind that leaves VOID standing in the paint if anyone so much as lifts a corner. I have sealed thousands. For a moment the old click came, the one a finding makes when it seats, the small sound of a thing going exactly where it belongs. Then it was gone. I noted that it had gone, and I noted the time.

The green room was a converted meeting suite with a service door the Service had sealed but the hotel floor plan still showed. The seal was a sticker.

* * *

The floor plans had emptied the printer tray, and the only other paper in the flat was the stock I had printed the syllabus on. Heavy, cream, a little toothy, bought two years ago in a single ream because I had not wanted to be seen buying it twice. I noticed that, sitting at the desk on Saturday night with the pen uncapped, and I used it anyway.

It took ink slower than writing paper does. The nib dragged, and the line sat wet on the surface for a second before the fibres took it in, so that each word stayed shining behind the pen for the length of the next. I had never written on it by hand. The syllabus had been typed. Everything I had put on that paper had been put there by a machine, at my instruction, and now it was going to be my hand, at nobody's.

The phone was face-down at my elbow. Hannah's voicemail was on it, six days old now, one minute and fourteen seconds, unplayed. I was writing back to a voicemail I hadn't played. It is a thing people do at the end, I'm told. I had always assumed it was the bereaved.

It was Halloween. By then, if counsel had done what the court told him, Special Agent E. Reyes had eleven names on a page. Down the corridor somebody had a lantern carved on the landing, and the smell of scorched pumpkin flesh came under my door with the cold. The knocks came along the corridor, a door, a door, a shriek, a door, and reached mine, and waited, and went on. There was nothing in the flat to give them. Hannah had sent a photograph in the afternoon. Astrid in a cape she was too old for and knew it, the hem held up with clear tape, every seam doubled. June as a skeleton, the bones printed white on black jersey. I counted the ribs. Ten on the left, twelve on the right. Twelve pairs, I thought, and it didn't come out funny. It came out as a number. I turned the phone back over.

I have read a great many of these, most as photocopies in a scene file, a few out of coat pockets on my table. More than anyone I know. I know what they say and the order they say it in. The practical things, then the apology, then the memory, then the apology again, longer. Most of them mention the cat. As a form it is underpowered and overworked, and I would mark it down for structure, and I could feel mine falling into it as I wrote, line by line, the way a body falls into the shape of the table, and I couldn't write it out of me.

So I wrote what had to be there. The spare key, and who has the other one. The lease, and the clause about notice. The bank, and the drawer of the kitchen where the statements are. Then a sentence I wrote and read and left: that if anyone tried to hand her my things in a clear plastic bag with a label on it, she was to say no, and make them bring them some other way. I know what those bags look like. I have filled them. And one memory, set down plainly and not underlined. The crusts at the park in August, the ones June wouldn't eat, and how Hannah ate them without noticing she was doing it, as if she were tidying. I didn't say why I remembered it. I didn't know how to put that on paper without making it the point.

What I left out, I can give you exactly, because I went down it like a list.

The agreement, eight pages and a signature page in blue ink. Holt, whom Hannah likes, and the veterans thing, which she believes is real, and which is. June's sticker, and my thumb. Diane. Brandt. The two voicemails in the archive at work, thirty-four seconds and forty-seven, and the third, face-down at my elbow, a minute and fourteen. Any sentence that could be read as sorry. I wasn't, and I wasn't going to start in ink.

I wrote it to be read twice. First by whoever came through the door if I didn't, with gloves on and a camera, who would read it for what it gave away. Then by Hannah, later, at a table, for what it didn't. So I redacted as I went, the way I had redacted the syllabus, a word lifted here, a clause left short there, and I heard the voice it was doing it in. It was the same one. It was good at this.

I squared it on the desk above the drawer, where it would be found. Then I thought about who would open the drawer after that, and what they would find in it, and in what order, and I stopped.

I signed it with the name Hannah used for me. She would have said it in the voicemail. I hadn't played it. My hand stopped on its own, with the nib still on the paper and the ink spreading into the tooth of it, and I had to put the pen down.

* * *

The clocks went back that night. The country gave everybody an hour. I used mine to be early.

My body woke me at what was now four, and lay there knowing it was five. The flat smelled of cold radiators and the coffee I hadn't drunk on Friday. I made a fresh pot and drank it black, standing, and ate the heel of a loaf that had gone hard at the cut end, tearing it, chewing it a long time, because the crust was the only thing in the kitchen that asked for any effort. It tasted of flour and of the fridge. That was Sunday morning.

On set-up day the Calloway brings its volunteers in through the loading dock, because the lobby is being dressed and nobody wants a woman with a lanyard walking across a freshly laid carpet. I came in at eight on Ruth Calder's. The dock smelled of wet cardboard and diesel from a truck idling at the bay, and of chafing-fuel gel, sweetish and blue, from somewhere further in. I walked the corridor in from the bay, past the service-lift lobby, to the glass booth at the far end, where a receiving supervisor sat with a breakfast sandwich in its paper and a hotel radio in its cradle on the desk beside him, and he looked at the lanyard, not at the badge, and not at me. Nobody held it against a list. A lanyard means somebody else has already checked. The stair door was beside the booth, and I knew as I reached it that I had walked the route backwards.

They gave me cable mats. Long black rubber runners, ridged, heavy enough to want two hands, laid over the cable runs along the front of the press riser so that nobody would trip on a senator's last night. I laid them square and taped the seams down with gaffer, tearing it by hand off the roll, pressing each strip with the heel of my palm until it had decided to stay. Gaffer tape is the only adhesive more trusted than a lanyard. I was good at it. A young man with a clipboard said I was a lifesaver, and I said, "Thanks," and taped the next one.

At eleven I took my break. Stair C, from the ballroom level down one floor: concrete treads, a painted steel rail, a cage of emergency lighting at the landing. I went at a working walk, the pace of a woman sent for more tape, and counted. On the turn of the landing I felt the scar under the waistband, there and not hurting, and logged it. From the stair door on three to the closet door on two, thirty-one seconds.

The closet opened on its lever, as the hotel's plan had said it would on any day the floor had a function, and on Monday the floor would have one. A par sheet was taped inside the door, the restock schedule by shelf and by day, and I read it the way I read a chart at the end of a bed, for what was due and what wasn't. Round ivory banquet cloths, bottom shelf, not wanted until a wedding on the Wednesday. The closet smelled of starch, and of bleach under the starch, and of warm lint breathing out of the laundry chute in the wall. I knelt and lifted the stack and put the catering jacket at the bottom of it: black, with a slate collar and the outside caterer's wordmark stitched on the breast, bought off the uniform supplier's seconds rail, for cash and for the wordmark. Folded over a plain black tote. The kit in its inside pocket. All of it went there that day. On Monday the magnetometer would get me, a badge, a bag, and a page. I set the cloths back down square, and looked at the stack from the door, and it was a stack of tablecloths.

At three I went out by the dock. The vendor gate is beside the shutter, and you can't see either from the booth. I pressed the green button with a knuckle, and the lock dropped, and I didn't go through. I stood inside and counted it back to locked. Nineteen. Nobody watched it close. Then I pressed it again and went out into the cross street, where the November air came off the river and got in under my coat where I had stopped filling it.

That night the chicken went down the chute. Three weeks in brine. I had brined it for afterwards, the night of the suite, and afterwards had come, and I hadn't sat down to it. When I lifted the lid the brine had gone cloudy as dishwater, and what came up out of it was sour and sweetish and close, the smell of a thing that has been waiting too long for a reason. I bagged it with its plate and tied the bag and carried it down the corridor at arm's length, and heard it go a long way down. After that, tea, and water.

On Monday I had taken the day as leave. At noon I began, and I did it in the same order as the night of the suite, move for move. Shower, short; the bathroom cold enough that the steam stood in the air. Hair washed and pinned up tight. Nails short and square over the sink. No lipstick. On the bed I laid out what I would carry, and it didn't take long: the lanyard, the bag with the press folder and the charger and the tin of lip balm, the printed run-of-show. There was nothing to lay out beside them. Everything else was under a stack of tablecloths on the second floor.

The belt went to its last hole and was loose on it, hanging on the anterior superior iliac spines, which had come up since October like the corners of a box under a sheet. I took the trussing needle out of the kitchen drawer and pushed a new hole through the leather an inch further on, and turned the needle to open it. It was the first thing I'd used it for in weeks. The navy blazer, the press assistant's jacket, went on over a shirt and sat away from me at the shoulders, looser than it had in the shop.

"You don't eat before," I said, out loud, in the kitchen, to nobody. It had been a rule about wanting. Now it was a rule. I didn't eat.

The hunger was still there under it, the way a pulse is still there in a cold wrist if you know where to put your fingers: faint, regular, aimed at one man. No pleasure in it. I recorded it as a finding and didn't feed it.

I unlocked the drawer with the brass key. The third page was where it had been, a clean print, never handled. Under it, face-down, was the page in my own handwriting, and I didn't turn it over. I took the third page and nothing else, and folded it in thirds without reading it, and put it behind the run-of-show, and put them both in the inside breast pocket on the left. The other page stayed. I locked the drawer. I switched the phone off, because a phone keeps a list of where it has been and somebody was already reading lists, and left it face-down on the desk. My hand went to the pocket.

On the way out, the hall mirror.

This time I didn't look like someone going on a date. I looked like someone going to work. I preferred the date.

Behind me, the letter squared to the edge of the desk. The drawer beneath it locked. The third page in my jacket, where my hand kept finding it.


# Interlude — Reyes

Reyes had the two pages side by side under the desk lamp. It was Saturday, and Halloween, and anyone who could be somewhere else was. The floor had its weekend sound: the ventilation, and down the row a copier on standby that cycled every few minutes.

Photocopies; the originals were in evidence, and she had read the copies until the toner had worn smooth at the folds. *Confused process for protection. Mistook clarity for cruelty.* Above each line a job title was circled in red marker, the same stroke both times, each circle closed in one movement. Whoever drew them had not hesitated.

She read the two lines once each, the way she would have read an evaluation of herself, and wrote nothing next to them.

The pattern took two sentences. Two bodies, a month apart, each with a page pinned to the chest, a circled role, one typed line of judgement and tape across the eyes. Diane Pell's mouth had been full of paper from the campaign's own agreements, and every footer the lab could piece together carried the same matter number under a thin rule. Carmichael Brandt had been opened on the edit desk in his suite and closed again, roughly, by someone who had not needed to look anything up. In her copy of the Pell file one word was underlined twice. *Practised.*

The profile had three terms, and she knew where each had come from. Medically trained: the two autopsy files. A signatory: the agreements in the mouth. Female: counsel, whose brief against the subpoena had argued for the privacy of "former female staff and volunteers". He had protected them so carefully that he had described them for her. She had held all three terms for a week, and in her head the killer had been *she* for most of it. What she had not had was a name.

The order had come down on Friday. Counsel complied at twenty to six on Saturday evening, two days before Election Eve. In a law firm's mouth, *promptly* meant the last hour before it became contempt.

The courier envelope was sealed with red tamper tape. She slit it with the letter opener, and the tape tore louder than anything had all afternoon.

One page. Weeks of motions for one sheet of paper, on better stock than anything the Bureau bought. Matter LC-0311 in the header. Eleven lines: a surname, an initial, a date of signing, a control number. Every date fell inside the same eight months, three years ago. The page did not say why. Clipped behind it was the protective order. The signatories were to be approached in a fixed and non-prejudicial sequence, which counsel had helpfully specified as alphabetical. Any departure went back to the judge. She read that paragraph twice.

The weekend heating was set low, dusk had come up against the window, and her hands were cold. She warmed them one at a time on the metal shade of the lamp, the biro mark on the right middle finger dark against the knuckle.

She laid the lab's reconstructions against the header, wet footers pieced back together. Three of them carried LC-0311. The number she had been carrying since September had eleven names under it now. In another job she might have called what she felt pleasure. In this one it was a match, and she wrote it down.

The list had two holes, and counsel's covering note named them in a paragraph that was careful to be helpful. One signatory had only a forwarding address abroad. For the eleventh the firm held a disconnected number and an address years out of date. She marked both lines for counsel, and put in for background checks on all eleven. The system said three to four days. It was election week, and every agent in the building had been pulled onto it.

She stood at the window while the copier cycled and ate a fun-size chocolate bar from the bowl at the lobby desk, and noticed only when she was folding the wrapper. The clocks went back tonight. She would work through the extra hour, and she knew already which squares it would fill.

She opened the paper desk calendar. Sunday. Monday morning, before she reported to the hotel for the Election Eve detail. Tuesday, between the polls opening and the returns. She skipped the woman abroad, since no approach could be made, and logged it anyway, because the paper was what would be read later. Then she filled the squares in her own hand, block capitals all the same height, the biro scratching between the copier's cycles, eight squares across three days, a ninth name noted and passed over, until the sequence came to Wednesday.

Vale was tenth. Reyes booked her for the day after the election, Wednesday at ten, and wrote the name in the square in block capitals, the same as all the others.


# Chapter 21

The loading dock smelled of diesel. November diesel, which is not the summer kind; it hangs lower and gets into wool. At twenty to five on Monday, Election Eve, I was standing in a line of staff and vendors that ran along the dock wall under the shutter, and I could smell, in order, a catering van idling at the kerb, somebody's hairspray, the coffee on three or four people's breath, the sizing in the navy blazer I had bought for this, and last, because I am always last to my own body, a sweetish note on my own breath, like pear drops left in a warm pocket.

Ketones. I had eaten nothing since a black coffee and a heel of bread on Sunday morning, and the liver had done what the liver does when nobody feeds it, which is break down fat and send the acetone out on the breath. My stomach closed once, hard, a fist on nothing, and I logged that too, and didn't want anything. It is the smell we're taught to check for on a collapsed man's breath, before anyone in the room decides what kind of body he is. Nobody in that line was going to check mine.

The badge on my lanyard said RUTH CALDER, and under it, VOLUNTEER, and under that, PRESS ASSISTANT, on white PVC with the navy-and-gold MARCHETTI header and a gold band along the foot that meant back of house. No photograph. I hadn't written a record. I'd borrowed one.

I read the line while it moved, because I can't not, because fifteen years of intake have left me unable to stand behind a man without taking his history: the sound tech two ahead of me with the square outline of a nicotine patch showing through his shirtsleeve when he hoisted his flight case; the field organiser beside him, whose hands lay on his clipboard too still for the hour and the day, a stillness somebody had prescribed for exactly this kind of evening, propranolol, I'd have said, swallowed with a glass of water at teatime; a woman of about twenty-five on a staffer's lanyard, pale at the lips, sipping ginger ale the way you sip when you are holding something down, her free hand going to her lower belly and away again before anyone could see it go, who had not told anyone yet; and at the arch ahead the agent on the post, the upper rim of his left ear thick and folded in on itself, cartilage healed lumpy over an old bleed nobody had drained, which is what a wrestling mat does to a boy at sixteen, and his earpiece in the right.

It was the only pleasure I had left. A habit. My mouth did nothing while I did it.

The young women I read twice. I found I was checking each of them for one more thing, a thing there is no sign for on a body: whether anyone had already put a page in front of her, and a pen beside it, laid clip-away.

The Service had the first screen: an arch, a table, a bag check. My bag held a press-kit folder, a phone charger with no phone, and a tin of unscented lip balm. My own phone was at home on the writing desk, face-down, switched off. Everything else I would need tonight had been in a closet on the second floor since Sunday, because none of it could have walked through this arch, and the only thing on me that I could not have left behind was folded in thirds in my inside breast pocket, behind a printed run of show.

The arch said nothing. Every fifth one of us got the wand anyway, and I was a fifth, and the wand said something at my belt. I had put a new hole in that belt that afternoon with a trussing needle, and the buckle sat further round my waist than its maker had meant it to.

"Arms out for me," the officer said. She had short nails and the patience of somebody a long way into a long shift. Her hands went across my shoulders and down each arm to the wrist. Over the left outer pocket, which had nothing in it, and on. Down my back. Then down my left side, under the ribs, and there she stopped, and pressed flat.

It was a month old. Three buried stitches, seven out on day ten, a scar I could find in the dark. Under the heel of her hand it pulled, a hard bright pull right through the wall, and the flinch started in the external oblique before I had decided anything at all. I felt the first fibres take up.

I have stood still for fifteen years while things were done to bodies. I let my breath out the way I tell families to in the viewing room, all the way, through the mouth, and let the muscle go soft under her palm, and kept my face where it was, which was pleasant and slightly bored. If she felt anything, it felt like a woman who was ticklish. She was thorough. She'd have made a good mortuary technician, if she could have learned to be thorough about the people who didn't mind.

Her hand moved on, up, over the left breast of the blazer, and the paper in the inside pocket crackled under it.

"Run of show," I said, before she asked.

"Everybody's got one." She waved me through.

On the far side of the arch I put two fingers of my right hand on my left wrist, the way I've always done it, and counted to the second hand of my watch. Sixty-four. My resting rate, to the beat. I noted it. I didn't yet know what kind of finding it was.

Past the Service line the campaign had its own desk, at the head of the back-of-house corridor. There had been no desk like it at the fundraiser, where a lanyard had meant that someone else had already checked. Somebody had learned something since August, and put a girl at a folding table to prove it.

She was nineteen. I'd have put money on it. I've signed certificates on less. The skin along her jaw still had that faint hormonal unevenness that settles by the early twenties, and she had told the man in front of me that it was her first time voting tomorrow. She had a laminated list, sorted by surname, and a dry-wipe marker with its cap posted on the end. Since Saturday there had been another list of this campaign's names in somebody else's hands. I supposed it ran the same way.

"Name?"

"Calder. Ruth."

She ran her finger down the C's, properly, skipping nothing, and found CALDER, RUTH, and ticked it in marker, and looked up at me with total sincerity and said, "You're all set."

Diligence, full marks. Authority, none.

I passed because the checker was a campaign volunteer, nineteen, with a laminated list. Her list checked names, and the name was real. The hole was exactly where I'd said it would be.

* * *

Radios. Every few metres a radio on somebody's shoulder said something short and broke off in a squelch, and another answered it, a building talking to itself in a language it had invented that afternoon. Under the radios, the hum of the service corridors, which is the hum of every hospital basement I have walked through. A room-service trolley went by me with a flat spot on one wheel, and I heard it all the way to the corner, tick, tick, tick, like a clock with a limp.

I knew the corridors. The plans had come off the card I took from the server farm on the twenty-fourth, and I had read them on my kitchen table like a body: the public rooms in front, the plumbing behind. Service level. Ballroom level, on three. The green-room corridor. Stair C beside it.

I walked them carrying the last box of press folders from the press table, because nobody stops a woman with a box. I could have carried a body through that hotel if I'd thought to put it in one. I'd lifted it by its outer flaps, which a box that light will bear, with my sleeves pulled down over my hands, and I carried it that way the whole walk, the cardboard edges biting through the cuffs into the heels of my palms. Nothing of mine was going to be on that box.

Doors opened to the public at six. I heard them open, a long way off, the way you hear the sea from a car park. At a quarter past I went into Stair C and stopped on the half-landing, wedged the box against the wall with my hip, and took out the run of show to check my times.

Page four was security contacts. USSS shift lead. Venue security. Hotel engineering. And at the bottom, FBI LIAISON: SA E. REYES.

I had first read that name in October, at the foot of a letter quoted in a lawyer's email. Since then it had been a rank, an initial and a surname, and it was still a rank, an initial and a surname. I folded the page back in front of the other one.

Somebody else in this building read paper for a living.

Down one floor. Thirty-one seconds from the stair door on three to the closet door on two, the same as Sunday. The box made me take the stairs at an angle, and on the turn the scar pulled again, a long draw from the flank round towards the back, like a seam being tested by somebody who wanted to know if it would hold. It held.

The closet smelled of starch and bleach and the warm lint that comes up out of a laundry chute. The par sheet was still taped inside the door, and behind it, flat to the door where I had slid them on Sunday, a pair of pale nitrile. I propped the box on my thigh against the shelf edge, got them out one-handed and on, left and then right, and the snap of each cuff was small and flat in there among the linen. Then the box went down squared beside the cloth stack, and my bag beside it.

The round ivory cloths weren't due out until a Wednesday wedding. The catering jacket was where I had put it, folded at the bottom, over the black tote. I slid it out and found the kit in its inside pocket with my fingers before I looked at it, and spread it through the blazer, each thing where my hand already knew it would be. The hard case, the marker and the pin, into the left inside breast pocket, beside the page. The roll of black vinyl into the right outer pocket. The long zip-tie flat along the lining. The scalpel, No. 4 handle, guard on, into the right inside pocket, for the seal and for nothing else. The black nitrile, for later, into the right outer pocket with the tape. A fresh pale pair, folded right side out, into the left outer.

Then I stripped the pair I had on, inside out, the way I teach it, one into the other, and dropped them into the mouth of the tote. The jacket went back over the tote and the cloths back over the jacket, and I did that with my cuffs pulled down over my fingers. A press assistant in surgical gloves is a story somebody tells afterwards.

My hands were cold. The closet was warmer than my own flat had been for three weeks, and my hands were cold in it.

Up again. The Service post at the Stair C landing took my gold band at a glance, and I went past him and looked, the way anyone would look, down the short service passage that ran behind the green room. Eight paces of painted block wall and a plain hotel door at the end of it. Across the gap between the door and the jamb, at shoulder height, a white tamper-evident sticker with the Service's legend printed on it, and under the print, invisible until somebody lifted it, the word VOID.

I have taken tape off more faces than anyone in that building. I knew what it would do. It would say so.

I counted them every time I went past. One on the landing. Two at the green-room door. The shift lead walking through every so often, a word with each post, a handoff you could set a watch by, and I did. Competent. Not omniscient. I'd have passed them, narrowly.

His first event was the volunteer thank-you in the mezzanine ballroom, the room where I had first watched Diane work a crowd in ninety-second loops. His minute before it was in the green room. At seven nineteen I was on the stair below the landing when the radios said the principal was moving. I heard the post's shoes go out through the stair door, and I went up the last flight and into the passage. When the detail arrived, the stair post stepped forward into the corridor to widen the cordon at the door, and while he was out there the landing had nobody on it. I had come up to see that.

At seven twenty-one I stood with my ear near the gap of the sealed door, not touching the label. Through it I heard the main door of the green room on its closer, the long hydraulic sigh and the click of the latch, and I began to count.

My heart went along under the count, slow. My stomach had gone quiet some time in the afternoon. I noted that, and didn't like it.

The latch went. Voices. He was out.

I was down the stairs before the post's shoes came back to the landing, and on the half-landing I counted it through again in my head, from the sigh to the latch, and got the same number. I didn't know why he had cut it, whether it was the schedule, or the night, or nothing. I wasn't going to get to ask.

The rhythm was tighter than the footage. The minute alone had become forty seconds. My plan had been built for sixty.

* * *

The carpet in the green-room corridor gave nothing back. It was the dense, low hotel kind that takes a footstep and keeps it, and the air conditioning came down out of a grille somewhere above me and lay across the back of my neck like the draught off a drawer at four degrees. The rally speech was at nine, in the grand ballroom. At ten to nine I was standing near the door to Stair C with my badge out and a clipboard against my chest, wearing the face of a woman waiting to be told where to stand. I had stood on that spot with that clipboard on the shift lead's last three passes, and by the third he had stopped seeing me. I ran my thumb along the edge of the badge. The PVC was slick and faintly warm from my body. It was the only warm thing I had on me.

Ten paces from the stair door to the green-room door. Behind the stair door, eight more paces of passage to the sticker. Twelve seconds, door to sticker, at a working walk. I had worked that figure out on Friday night and not needed it, because on Friday night I'd had sixty seconds and could afford to walk from anywhere. I had forty now. So I would already be at the stair door when he went in, and when the latch went I would have only the eight paces left.

I did the sum again. It still worked. I had decided it worked the way you decide which queue to stand in at a supermarket, and my hands were exactly as cold after the second sum as they had been before the first.

At eight fifty-two the radios changed tone.

They came down the corridor together, in the shape I had counted all evening: the shift lead in front, two agents, an aide with a tablet talking up at an angle, and Holt in the middle of them with his wife at his elbow. None of them was carrying paper. Wherever E. Reyes was in the building, it wasn't here. Sloane Marchetti had her hand on his sleeve. Her jaw was calm. Under the angle of it, where the collar of her dress stood off the skin, her carotid was going hard enough to show.

He passed within a few metres of me. It was the nearest I had been to him in three years, if a corridor counts. In those three years he had been footage, a face on a tablet propped against a fruit bowl, a man at a rope line in somebody else's video. He didn't look at me. He was listening to the aide, head inclined, giving the aide the whole of his attention, and even sideways, even walking, even with his back half to me, you could feel it come off him, the warmth he kept for whoever was nearest. Cedar went by in his wake. Under the clipboard I had two fingers on my left wrist before he was past. Sixty-four.

I kept my eyes on the clipboard. He was the only man in the building who didn't read my badge. He'd have known my name if I had been his staff. That was the whole problem, in a sentence.

At the door Sloane's hand left his sleeve. She went on down the corridor towards the ballroom with a staffer at her shoulder, and she didn't look back. Her pulse said she knew. Her back said she had decided not to.

The cordon tightened at the door exactly as I had counted it. The stair door opened beside me, and the post came out through it and took two steps up the corridor and turned his back to me, which is what he was for. He was watching for anyone coming towards the principal. I was behind. I put the heel of my hand on the push bar of the stair door, through the cuff of my sleeve. It was cold steel, and I felt the cold of it through the wool into the bones of my wrist.

The detail stopped at the door. He went in alone. The door began to close on its closer.

In the gap before it shut: a framed photograph of a Marine on the dressing table, already turned to face the chair. From ten paces, side-on, I had a shoulder in dress blues, the white of a cover, the edge of a plain black frame on a cardboard easel, angled to where he would sit. Somebody had set it out for him. The dressing table was a banquet table with a skirt and a lamp. There was no mirror over it. A breath of warm air came out of the room into the corridor, smelling of the lamp, and then the door met its frame.

My mouth watered, once, thinly, the way it does at somebody else's dinner through a wall, and was dry again before I could take its history.

The latch.

I was already counting. I was through the stair door while the post's back was turned, and I took its weight on my shoulder and let it home against the closer, so that the latch went in under the radios and not over them. The scar pulled as I turned into the passage, one hard draw, and I let it. Eight paces. The pale nitrile came out of my left pocket and went on by feel as I walked, left and then right, and I didn't snap the cuffs. Somewhere on the other side of the wall he would be sitting down with his brother, and the detail would be looking at the corridor, which was the plan.

I counted to five and put my hand flat on the sticker. My hand was steady. It had been steady in the suite too, and then it had been happy. I wished, briefly, that it weren't steady now.


# Chapter 22

My hand was flat on the sticker, and the count was at five.

It was a white label the size of a luggage tag, laid across the gap between the door and the jamb at the height of my shoulder, with the Service's legend printed along it in navy and, under the print, down in the adhesive where nobody could see it yet, a word I knew was there. It was the same make I put on evidence bags at work. I have sealed thousands of them and signed across the seal, and I have never once thought of it as a lock. Tamper-evident is a modest promise. It doesn't say nobody will get in. It says somebody will know. A sticker is what you have left when you can't have a key, and I had looked at this one twice that evening and noticed its honesty.

The plan had been to lift it. A No. 22 blade slid flat under the label along the jamb edge and drawn down in short strokes, the way you lift tape off a decedent's cheek without taking the epidermis with it, so that the label came away whole and could be laid back down afterwards. It would not have been clean. Nothing lifts that make clean; the word stays ghosted in the adhesive for anyone who puts their face to it. But it would have read as untouched to a man glancing down a passage, and a glance was all the passage would get. I had allowed fifteen seconds for that. I had allowed fifteen because I had sixty. The plan had been built in the last week of October on two clips of a closing door, and on neither of them had the minute gone over.

I had forty, and five of them were gone.

I put the clipboard flat down the back of my waistband, under the blazer, out of the way. I thumbed the guard off the scalpel and ran the blade down the gap instead, top to bottom, through the label where it bridged the door and the frame. A cut where there should have been a lift. Then I put my shoulder to the door. It went inward the way pantry doors go, heavy and grudging on its closer. As it moved, the face of the label caught on the door's edge and parted. It made a small dry sound, a zip. It sounded exactly like a plaster coming off a heel. I heard it and kept going. The guard went back on the blade by feel. The blade went into its sheath, and the sheath into the right inside pocket of the blazer. I turned sideways to go through the gap, and the scar on my left flank pulled once, hard. I pressed it through the blazer with the heel of my left hand. It quieted. I was thinking.

The closer brought the door to behind me and it touched my heel as it came home, a soft push, like a nurse's hand in the small of the back. On the suite side there was a thumb-turn, small and plain. The annex had a line on it, under the walk-through photograph: the hotel wanted that door kept off the lock from the inside, for the servers, and couldn't lock it from the corridor at all, so the Service had sealed it. I turned it. It went over with a click I felt in the bones of my thumb more than I heard it, and then nobody could come in behind me, and I had chosen that. Through the wall the ballroom was playing something with a lot of brass in it, warming the room up, and the brass took the click and kept it.

I was behind a screen. Hotels stand one in front of every service door, a folding banquet screen in three hinged panels upholstered in something the colour of weak tea, so that the guests don't have to look at who is bringing the food.

I smelled the room once I had stopped moving. The carpet had been shampooed that afternoon, and the whole floor gave off the cleaner, a synthetic floral that has never grown anywhere, sitting on top of the wet-dog note of nylon pile that hadn't quite dried; under it, in this room only, cedar, and the warm dust coming off the lamp. That was all. I had my own breath too, in a room that still, the faint sweetness of a body that had been burning itself since Sunday morning. Nobody else would have caught it. I caught it.

Then I looked. Through the gap at the hinge I could see a slice of the room. The one upholstered chair. The navy jacket already off and hung on its back, square at the shoulders, the flag pin on the lapel catching the lamp. His shoulder in a white shirt, and the knot of a tie, and the dressing table, which was a banquet table in a skirt, and on it the corner of a plain black frame, turned away from me, towards him. He didn't turn. He hadn't heard anything worth turning for. Across the room the main door was shut on its closer, and its privacy latch was thrown. He had thrown it himself when he came in; it was part of the minute. So both doors were locked, and he had locked one of them, and I had locked the other, and we were, for the moment, the only two people in the building who could say that.

I was hungry. Not in the stomach. The stomach had gone quiet somewhere in the back corridors that afternoon, and I hadn't asked it to start again. This was further up and further forward, behind the face, the hunger you feel in a restaurant when the door to the kitchen swings and you see your own plate on the pass. It was the first thing in me since the bathroom floor that had leaned forward, and it was leaning at the back of a man's head through the hinge of a screen.

I ran the ways out the way I'd run a differential, most likely first. There were two. The main door went through the detail. The service door was clean until forty, when the post would step back to the stair landing and the stair would be watched again. Everything else in the room was ruled out by the walls.

Somewhere in this building a woman had a list with my name on it, and from everything I knew of her, which was a signature and a line on page four, she was the kind of person who reads labels.

I looked down at my right hand. On the tip of the index finger, stuck to the nitrile, there was a tongue of the label's face film the size of a fingernail, curled at one edge, and on it the letters stood up pale where the adhesive had let go. VOID. The rest of the word was on the far side of the door, split down the middle and lifting from the frame, telling anyone who walked down the passage exactly what it had promised to tell them. I peeled the scrap off my glove and put it in my right outer pocket, beside the roll of tape. Evidence control. My hands did it without me.

The sticker was torn. Someone would see it. My clock had become a shorter clock.

* * *

I stepped out from behind the screen.

It was four paces of shampooed carpet to the near side of the dressing table, and the carpet gave a little under each one, damp in the pile, and the lamp came onto the side of my face as I cleared the screen, a small local warmth like standing next to someone who has just come in from running. My fingertips stayed cold inside the gloves. I didn't go round to his side. I stopped where the table was between us, and so the photograph was between us too.

The table had been dressed by somebody who did this every night of the week. A lamp with a pleated shade. A bottle of water with its cap still sealed. A copy of tonight's remarks, printed, clipped at the top left corner with a black bulldog clip. And the photograph of his brother the Marine, in a plain black frame on a cardboard easel, turned to face the chair. From my side it was a rectangle of black felt and a single cardboard leg holding it up. I couldn't see the Marine. He could.

He was sitting forward in the chair with his forearms on his knees and his hands loosely together between them, in shirtsleeves, the tie knotted, looking at his brother. That was all he was doing. I had watched that minute from the outside on two clips, frame by frame, as a door with a man behind it, and I had supposed that inside it he did something: prayed, or spoke, or wept, or rehearsed. He looked at a photograph. His thumbs lay one over the other. His breathing was slow and even and came from low down, from a diaphragm that had been trained to fill ballrooms. I report it because it is what I saw.

Then he looked up from the photograph.

I looked first. I never look first. In every room I have ever worked in I have smelled and listened and touched and weighed before I let my eyes do anything, because the eyes will tell you a story and you have to have the facts in hand before you let them. This was the face I had come for. I looked at it first.

I read it the way I read a face on intake, because I have never been able to look at a face any other way. A startle: the brows went up, frontalis drawing the skin of the forehead into three soft lines and letting it go again, perhaps half a second.
