His face was turned into the lamp, and the pupils didn't change. They were small and they stayed small. There was no flinch at the eyes, no narrowing, no blanching at the lips or the alae of the nose, and his hands, loose between his knees, did not come apart. It was the ordinary startle, the one a man gives when a door opens that he thought was shut. I had waited two years for a second expression to come in behind that one. I stood there and waited for it.

It didn't come.

He saw me. He did not recognise me.

His eyes went to the lanyard. They went to the white card on it, and the gold back-of-house band, and the navy-and-gold header with his own name across it in block capitals, and the three lines under that, somebody else's name, VOLUNTEER, PRESS ASSISTANT, and they stayed there a moment, reading, and then they came back up to my face with the matter settled. They didn't go to my hands. A woman with a lanyard had appeared in a room he had locked, and his face had filed her the way rooms like this one have always filed women with lanyards: staff, here for something, about to tell him the time. I had imagined this face for two years, in every state but the one it was in, which was pleased to see me.

Two years of rehearsal should have left me a mark ready for this face, and I reached for one the way you reach for a pen you always keep in the same pocket, and nothing came to hand.

Through the wall the ballroom had gone up a notch. The brass had given way to drums, and over the drums a man on a microphone was telling a room full of people to get ready, it wouldn't be long now. Under it my count went on by itself, and behind me, past the torn label, the stair was still unwatched. My hands were at my sides. The left one wanted the inside breast pocket of the blazer, where the page was folded against the hard case and the marker and the pin, and I didn't let it go there. I didn't take my pulse. I have taken my own pulse in most rooms that mattered since August. I didn't take it in this one.

Then he smiled.

It was the smile from the debate: the whole face he had turned on the man at the other lectern in the seconds after he touched his cuff, the whole face the break-room television had shown me on the morning of the debate, smiling at the camera. Zygomaticus major drew the corners of his mouth up and back. Orbicularis oculi came in under it, the ring of muscle round each eye tightening, the skin at the outer canthi gathering into fans. That second muscle is the one most people can't do on purpose. It was a true smile, by every sign I know how to read, and it was for me, and it meant nothing.

No water came, and no heat, and not the coin at the back of the tongue either. I had been hungry for this for two years, and I found there was nothing in my mouth.

* * *

I stood across the table from him and waited. I held still, as if the next line would arrive if I gave it long enough.

Thirteen gone, of forty. The scrap of label in my pocket. The rest of it split on the door. A corridor that was about to be watched again.

I know what recognition looks like on a face. I have watched it arrive through the glass of the viewing room when the sheet is turned back: half a second of a stranger, and then the face falls into the one they knew, and the face at the glass falls with it. Diane had done it on the concrete with a small dry click at the back of her throat, before she had decided to.

The drums through the wall. The low burble of an earpiece radio on the far side of the main door, somebody saying something short and somebody else saying something shorter. The lamp, ticking very faintly as the shade heated. His breathing. In the crease beside his nose there was a fleck of the powder they had put on him for the cameras, the colour of milky tea, sitting on the skin where the brush had missed. On a table I would have noted it. I noted it. I let it go.

He stood up.

It was courtesy. A woman was standing, so he stood. It was in him the way the startle had been, below thought. As he came up out of the chair his right hand crossed his body. His thumb and forefinger took the left cufflink, plain silver, and gave it a quarter-turn. His eyes went down to it. Two seconds. With the jacket off there was nothing over it, only the white cuff and the small silver bar, and I saw all of it from a metre away. Then his eyes came up. His hands came open a little at his sides, the palms half-turned towards me, the way a man holds his hands when he is about to ask if he can help.

"I think there may have been a mix-up," he said. "Are you with the network?"

He said it helpfully. His voice was a baritone at the volume of a small room, unamplified, warm, unhurried, the question turned up at its end the way his palms were, and it was offering me a way out of my own embarrassment before I had shown any. The badge said PRESS ASSISTANT. He had read it, and been kind about it.

There was a joke in it. I could see the whole shape of it. The network. Eleven of us on a list, my name among them. And one more, who had signed what I signed, and who had left me a message on a Sunday afternoon three years ago, from an office, that began *I think we may have... known the same person*. That was a network. I didn't make the joke. I am only showing you where it was.

My left hand went to the blazer. This time I let it. Through the cloth I found the run of show, and behind it the page, folded in thirds, warm from lying against me all evening. I didn't take it out. I know every word on it.

He waited the way he waited at a lectern, with interest.

I had no line for this. The syllabus began with his recognition. I am telling you that. I can hear how small my voice has got.

He stood there in his shirtsleeves. He waited, politely, for an answer about the network.

Between us on the table, the Marine, facing him. All I had of him was the back of the frame.


# Chapter 23

I told him my name.

"Iris Vale."

He sat down again. He had stood up when he saw I was standing, and now he lowered himself back into the chair by the dressing table, the one upholstered chair in the room, with his jacket on the back of it behind him. He put his hands on his knees. He looked up at me.

I was on the other side of the table. Between us on it was the photograph, facing him. Behind me was the screen, and behind the screen was the door I had come in by. My hands were in the pale gloves. My right hand was in the right outer pocket of the blazer, beside the roll of tape. It was not holding anything. My left hand was flat on the outside of the blazer, over the inside pocket. I did not take either of them out. I did not touch the table. I did not touch anything in the room.

He looked at my face. Then he looked at the badge on my chest. Then he looked at my face again.

The badge had someone else's name on it. He did not say so. He did not say anything about the gloves. He did not ask how I had come in. He did not look past me at the screen.

He was looking for me. I could see him do it. His eyes went over my face, the eyes and the mouth and the hair, and back to the eyes. His head went a little to one side. He opened his mouth once and closed it again. He was careful about it.

I let him look. I had come for him to look.

Nothing came.

Through the wall the ballroom was playing music that was mostly drums, and the bass of it came up through the floor into my shoes, and under the music there was a crowd, a great many people talking at once and waiting for him.

He sat back. He breathed out through his nose. He smiled, a smaller smile than the first one, and shook his head at himself.

"I'm so sorry," he said. "I meet a great many people, and I'm afraid it shows."

He let that stand.

"It's the one thing I'm supposed to be good at."

He did not raise his voice for any of it. The room was small, and he knew how to talk in a small room. He talked to me and to nobody else.

I did not say anything.

He leaned forward in the chair and put out his right hand. He put it out across the dressing table, past the photograph, low, with the palm turned a little up. He held it there. He was patient about it.

I didn't take it.

My right hand stayed in my pocket. My left hand stayed where it was. He drew his hand back and put it down on the arm of the chair. He did not say anything about it. He did not look at it. He looked at me, and he was still pleasant.

I had built two years around him. He had looked. There was nothing there.

I did not look away from him. He did not look away from me.

I stood where I was. The carpet was damp. It had kept my four paces in its pile. The photograph faced him and not me. His jacket hung on the back of the chair. His tie was knotted. Both his feet were flat on the floor, square under him, as he sat on a stage in the footage before they said his name. He was waiting.

I had said my own name out loud in this building. Somewhere in the building there was an FBI liaison who was a line on page four of the run-of-show. Somewhere there was a list with my name on it.

He said it back to me.

"Iris."

He used my first name back to me, warmly, as if he had just learned it.

He had.

* * *

I had always assumed the syllabus would begin with his face. Every rehearsal of it had begun there, with him knowing me, and the heat coming up into my face when he did, the way it had come up on a concrete floor and over a desk, and the rest following from that the way a report follows from the external examination, in order, nothing skipped. It had not begun there. So I would give him the evidence, and he could examine it himself.

I said it the way I give evidence. In order. No adjectives. The voice I use in court, where they ask you to keep your answers short and you do, because short answers are the ones that hold. I had written it out at length, in the drafts, over two years, and cut it, and cut it again, the way you cut a report you expect to defend on the stand, until nothing in it gives the other side a handle. What was left was four sentences.

"It was June, three years ago."

He was listening. He leaned forward a little, his forearms coming to rest on his thighs, his hands loosely together between his knees. He did not interrupt. I had watched him listen on a feed for two months, to donors and to aides, and in a small room, close to, he was better at it. He listened with his whole face. He listened to me across his brother's photograph, and his brother faced him the whole time I talked.

"A hotel room after an evening function, not this hotel."

Between sentences there was the ballroom, a muffled thudding through the plaster, and a man on a microphone saying something I couldn't make out, and the crowd answering him. Somewhere on the far side of that plaster my name was on a page I had never seen. I could hear my own voice in the room. It was flatter than his. It went out across the table and stopped at him and did not come back.

"You closed the door."

Then I told him the fourth.

My right hand closed once inside the pocket while I said it, on nothing, and opened again. While I said it, his right thumb and forefinger went to his left cuff and gave the cufflink, plain silver, a quarter-turn, and his eyes went down to it, and stayed down for two seconds, and came up again.

That was the only place his eyes went. He did not look at the door, or at the photograph, or at my hands. He looked at me the way he had been looking at me since I said my name, attentively, with his whole face, and when I stopped he went on looking for a breath, as if there might be a fifth.

That was all of it. Two years of drafts. Out loud it took four sentences. I have dictated longer findings on a hand.

I had reported it once, three years ago, on paper, to the people whose job it was, and signed where they showed me. I had written it into the drafts. I had never said it to him. I had said it now, across a table, in the voice I use for the strangers I open, and the room took it and held it, and nothing in the room changed.

He frowned. It was a small frown, troubled and careful, two short lines drawn in at the glabella, the frown of a man being asked about a case nobody had briefed him on. He held it for a breath. Then he spoke, gently. He said it as a correction, and as kindly as a correction can be said.

"I think you may be thinking of someone else."

I heard the shape of it before I heard what it meant. *I think you may.* A woman had begun a sentence to me that way once, on a Sunday afternoon, from an office, into a machine. *I think we may have... known the same person.* She had stopped once, before *known*. He did not stop anywhere.

I waited for the taste. It had come every time that June came up, in a ballroom, on a feed, on a concrete floor: the coin at the back of the tongue, held there too long. It didn't come. My mouth was dry. My tongue stuck to the roof of it and came away.

My left hand came off the blazer and went to my side without my sending it, to the place below the ribs, and pressed there through the blazer, and stayed a moment, and went back.

I smelled for him before I let myself look, out of habit, across the table: cedar, and the floral cleaner coming up out of the damp carpet, and the warm dust off the lamp. None of it told me anything. Then I looked at his face for the finding. That is what a face is for, on my table. It is where the body keeps the part it couldn't help. The lamp on the dressing table lit him from the side. His eyes were steady on mine. The pupils did nothing. Below the face, his hands lay loosely together between his knees, and the knuckles had kept their colour. The frown was already easing, the two lines going out of the skin. The face gave me nothing I could have sworn to. I have sworn to a great deal. I could not have sworn to that.

My eyes went, once, past the corner of the table to the chair. To the place where its arm met the front leg, beside his knee. There was a gap there, under the arm. I noted it the way I note where the door is in any room, and looked back at him.

He was waiting for me to say something. I was waiting for something too. Neither of us got it. Through the wall the man on the microphone said something, and the crowd answered him again, louder, the long rising noise of a room being promised a thing.

His eyes went past me to the main door, the one with the latch he had thrown himself. It was forty, on my count. Out in the corridor the post would be stepping back to the landing. I had rebuilt the night on forty earlier that evening, on a stair, from his first minute, counted through a sealed door. In all the old footage he had never once gone over.

He glanced at the door. Not for rescue. For his minute. He was worried about the time.

* * *

I was cold. I had been cold since the bathroom floor, and in the room I could feel exactly where: the nitrile against the palms of my hands, cool and slightly tacky where it had warmed and gone cold again; the back of my neck under my hair where it was pinned up; my shins. I report it because it was mine. I don't know what the room was doing. The cold was coming from me.

He brought his eyes back from the door. It took him an effort, and he let me see the effort, and he did it courteously. He sat up a little in the chair. He was giving me time he did not have, and he did not hide that he was giving it.

"Whoever did that to you," he said, "I'm sorry. I mean that."

I could not have told you whether he meant it. I could tell you which voice it was. It was the voice he would have used for a woman at a rope line with a story about her son, the warm, unhurried voice that stops the line for her and lets everybody see it stop. He was offering me his sympathy as a bystander, with his whole attention, and the way he offered it there was nobody in the room for it to be about.

He did not ask me which function. He did not ask who else had been there, or what I had done afterwards, or why I had waited three years and come now, tonight, through a door he had not heard open. He asked nothing. A man who had forgotten would not have known what to ask. A man who remembered would not have needed to. I could not tell which kind of nothing it was. He had already given his answer, kindly, and the rest of the conversation, the way he held it, was about me.

Then he went further than I had expected, further than he went anywhere on the footage. He told me he had done a great many things in his life he would do differently, if he could have them back. He said he was not a man who pretended otherwise. He said he could name them. He leaned back while he said it, his hands loose on the arms of the chair, and he looked, for a moment, almost amused at himself, almost tired.

"Most of them were votes."

He was not defensive. He was not braced for anything. He was at ease. It sounded as honest as anything I had ever heard him say. The ones he could name were on the record, under his own name, with the yeas and the nays.

"Let me get someone for you," he said. "There are people here who are very good at this."

He meant staff. A quiet room off a corridor. A woman with a clipboard and a soft voice, who would take some details and give me a number to call in the morning, and would call me, once, to follow up. A file. The campaign had always been very good at finding someone. A file was the one thing in the building I already knew I was in. There was a list somewhere in it, in a federal file, with my surname on a line of its own, and the list had not needed anybody to be kind.

I heard Diane in it. The whole of her function, the complaint and the file and the folder and the cabinet, in one courtesy, from a man with his hands loose on the arms of a chair.

Two women had rung me once, looking for someone who knew. I had found them somewhere to go. It had taken one key, twice.

"No," I said.

He accepted it at once. He inclined his head, a small nod, and let it go, the way he had let his hand go back to the arm of the chair.

He did not fill the quiet after it. He stayed as he was in the chair and left it to me, for as long as I wanted it. The upholstery gave under him as he settled his weight, a small sound in a small room, and the drums came through the plaster and took it.

I had prepared for denial. I had prepared for rage, and for bargaining, and for tears, his or mine, and for a hand going under the table to a button. I had prepared for him to call out, and for nobody to come. I had not prepared for manners.

My hands were where they had been. The right in the outer pocket, beside the roll of tape and the scrap of the label I had taken off my glove at the door. The left flat on the blazer over the inside pocket, and under it, through the lining, the hard case. I could feel it through the nitrile and the cloth: the edge of it, the ridge where the hinge ran, the small weight of it against my ribs. Beside it the marker, and the pin, and the page, folded in thirds. I knew what was in every pocket I had on.

He glanced at the door once more, briefly. Then at the photograph, once, as if for permission to be late. Then back to me, pleasant, waiting, kind. He was letting his minute go for me a few seconds at a time, and he wanted nothing for it.

I stood very still. I had been standing still for a long time by the clock in my head, and for almost no time at all by anybody else's. The bass came up through the floor into the soles of my shoes. The crowd on the other side of the wall had not been told anything yet.

I didn't yet know what I was going to do, and for the first time in this book you were ahead of me, and I knew it.

Thirty seconds over, on a clock only I was counting. I put my hand inside my jacket and checked for it the way you check a pocket. There was the case. There was nothing else.


# Chapter 24

My fingers were cold inside the pale gloves I had worn since the service door. I took the second pair out of my blazer, the black ones, and put them on over the first, left and then right, and snapped each cuff over the wrist, and they went on over the cold like a lid on a pan that has gone off the boil. In Brandt's suite a room had taken that sound and given none of it back. This room gave it back. It was a converted meeting suite with one lamp and a carpet somebody had shampooed that afternoon, and the snap came off its walls small and domestic, the sound of a woman in a kitchen getting ready to joint something. Through the wall the ballroom was playing something with a great deal of bass in it.

He watched me do it. He watched the way he must have watched staff do things for twenty years: politely, his hands loose on the arms of the chair, waiting to be told what it was for.

Lesson Three, as written, was simple. Immobilise him. Keep him conscious. Pin the page. Leave him alive, and let the women finish him.

I had written that two years ago at my own desk, before I knew there was a number. Since October I had known it: eleven names. Since the bathroom floor I had known the arithmetic was wrong. Eleven, less the one standing in the room. And one more, who had signed what I signed and died suddenly at home, and would finish nobody.

I came round the end of the dressing table and took his left wrist the way I take a pulse, two fingers and a thumb, and he let me. He held it out the way men hold out a wrist for a watch, to a woman who had told him her name. I have lifted the wrists of fourteen hundred people who couldn't refuse. He was the first who didn't think to. The zip-tie came out of the lining of the blazer flat, a long one. It went round his wrist and under the arm of the chair where it met the front leg, through the gap I had looked at while he was telling me I was thinking of someone else. I let go of his wrist. I drew the tie through its own head, tight, before his face had caught up with his arm. He looked down at it as if it were a lanyard somebody had handed him. From where I stood, at his shoulder, I could see the photograph the way he saw it: his brother in dress blues in a plain black frame, propped on its cardboard easel, looking at the chair.

The hard case was in the left inside breast pocket, where it had been since the closet, against the page. I opened it one-handed, the way you open a spectacle case, and took the syringe out capped. It had come from the office's toxicology lab, out of a locked cabinet with a signed register beside it, and somewhere in that register there was now a line that did not agree with the shelf. Somebody would count. Counting is what offices are for. The count would go into a file, and the file would go where the eleven names were going, to SA E. REYES, who was a line on page four of a run-of-show and nothing else to me.

I took the cap off with my thumb and dropped it into the case, and the case went back into the pocket.

He saw the needle, and he flinched. I heard it before I saw it: the upholstery taking the back of his head as far as the chair would let it go, and the plastic pulling once at the arm of the chair, and stopping.

And there it was, coming up through everything else in the room, through the aftershave, which was cedar and money and had been chosen for him by somebody whose job it was, through the shampooed carpet, floral cleaner over damp nylon pile, through the warm dust off the lamp: the sharp first note of the other sweat, apocrine, rising from his axillae into a shirt that had been ironed for television, the same note I had last smelled rising off Brandt on his desk, fear, recent onset, and my mouth filled with water the way a mouth fills at a door with the kitchen on the other side of it, and it was the first thing my mouth had wanted since the bathroom floor, and I am reporting it.

All of it came before he had a breath to use. I looked last, the way I always do. It was a real flinch. Orbicularis oculi had closed hard around both eyes, the whole ring of it, the skin of the lids puckering. He was frightened. I had watched him on a stage and at a rope line on rally footage, and on a feed, and at a funeral in the papers, and he had never once been frightened, and nobody had ever made him be.

I put the needle into him through his shirt, a small resistance and then none, the way a fork goes into something that is done. I put my thumb on the plunger and pressed, and my mouth was still full of him.

I had been counting since the green-room door closed. I had counted through my name and through his not knowing it, through the four sentences and through his kindness, to seventy, and on through the gloves and the tie and the case, and somewhere in it, with my mouth wet, there was no number. I have counted things my whole working life. Ribs, towels, grams, the paces from a trail marker to a steel door. I had never lost a count. I did not feel it go. I found it gone.

Nobody opened the door. I looked at it once, the main door with the latch he had thrown himself, the door I had built the whole night on nobody opening, and it was only a door, and I went back to my thumb.

He didn't shout. Even then his syntax held. He went to the only move there is in a small room, the one he must have made across a thousand tables with donors and with men like Brandt, and he made it with a needle in him.

"All right," he said. "All right. What do you want?"

My thumb stopped on the plunger with the barrel part-given. I drew the needle out, and stepped back round the table to my own side of it, and stood there holding the syringe needle-up between us, my thumb off it. The water had gone out of my mouth. What was left there was ordinary spit, and not much of it.

He had asked what I wanted. Not what I was going to do: what I wanted. It was the first question anyone in the campaign had ever asked me, and I didn't have an answer any more.

* * *

I watched what was in the syringe reach him. I had no number for how long it took. The tied wrist turned in the zip-tie with nothing behind it, the fingers opening on the arm of the chair, and his shoulders went down into the upholstery as if somebody had laid a wet coat over them. He looked at the tie. He looked at me. He could still talk, and the lesson had always needed his voice, but when it came it came slower than I had ever heard it, each word set down a little after the one before. Then he worked it through, visibly, as I had watched him work a room on a feed, reading the faces from the back to the front, finding the one person who mattered and going to them, and he arrived, by himself, at the only loss in the campaign he was able to feel.

"Is this about Diane?"

His voice broke on her name. I had heard that voice on rally footage, through a wall of loudspeakers in front of thousands, and through a camera the size of a button under a credenza, and in this room telling me he met a great many people, and it had never broken. It broke now in the middle of the word, the vocal folds letting go of the note as if somebody had cut a string, and what came after it came in pieces.

"She was family. I haven't slept since. Her daughter — Olivia — I was at the —"

He didn't finish it. The word was funeral. I had seen the photograph in the papers, him beside the girl, who was taller than her mother had been, both of them looking at something below the edge of the frame. I didn't say it for him.

He wept. The face went in. Corrugator drew the brows together and the inner frontalis lifted them, into that peaked shape almost nobody can produce on purpose; the corners of the mouth were pulled down; the chin dimpled over mentalis like orange peel. The tears came from both eyes at once and ran from the outer corners, which is where they run when the head is back, and his free hand came up, slow, and covered his face, and the breath went in and out of him behind it wet and uneven, catching at the top. I have stood with the bereaved more times than I can count, and I know what is performed and what is not. This was not.

Through the wall the ballroom stopped its music and somebody on a microphone told them to practise their applause, and they practised it, a long roar that came through the plaster at exactly the wrong moment, the sound of a room that was not ready for him yet, and he wept through it.

He gave her back to me in pieces, slowly, and every piece was accurate: that she stopped before certain words and people thought she was choosing them; that she had told him once, on a plane, about the stammer when she was a girl, and how she had beaten it out of herself; that Olivia was a sophomore, at Wellesley; that Diane rang her mother every Sunday at four, no matter what. I had known about the stammer. I had it from a dossier, from a profile, from the pump station. He had it because she told him. The rest I had too, every fact, from a lens and a feed and a trade magazine. We had both done the reading.

I looked for myself in it. I looked the way you look for a name on a list, from the top, and then from the top again. He was grieving the woman who had filed me into a cabinet. There was nothing in it for me, and it was not meanness; it was not even forgetting. There was nothing available. And it came to me then, not as an argument, but the way a finding comes at the bench before you know what it means and you write it down anyway: the machine I had built those pages around had never failed to see women. It had seen Diane down to the stammer. It had chosen which ones.

His wife was in the ballroom, on the other side of the applause. He didn't say her name. He said Diane's again.

My left hand had gone to my side. I didn't send it. It was there, through the blazer, through two pairs of gloves, flat on the scar below the ribs, pressing, and it stayed there while he talked. My right hand held the syringe with the needle up and my thumb off the plunger.

"They still don't know who," he said.

They would. They would know from a list of eleven names in a federal file, and the list would answer the question in its own order, with nobody's grief attached to any line of it, because a list doesn't stand beside anyone at a funeral. Nora Wade had signed what I signed, and had died suddenly at home, and her obituary had run in another county's paper. I had no reason to think anyone from the campaign had stood beside anybody at hers.

He was grieving the Gatekeeper. Something in me began, out of fifteen years' habit, to write it up, the reflex going for the pen as a hand goes for a light switch in a room it knows, and finding bare wall.

Something was running down my face. I noticed it the way I had noticed Brandt's blood, by temperature first: a line of warmth a degree or two under blood heat, coming from the inner corner of my eye and down beside the nose, and then another, and they were mine. It surprised me that I still ran that warm. I had been cold since the bathroom floor.

He took his hand away from his face and looked at mine. His own was wet and swollen at the lids and still kind, and he nodded at me, slowly, as if I had said something and he agreed with it.

I understood then that he would never be taught. There was no page that reached him. The syllabus shattered from the inside, and I was crying too, and he thought we were grieving the same woman.

"You knew her," he said.

* * *

I abandoned the protocol.

I am telling you plainly, so that you can't say later you weren't told. There was no next step. Lesson Three as written went on from here, and I knew every line of what came next, and none of it was addressed to the man in the chair. It was addressed to a man who knew who I was.

I set the syringe down on the dressing table beside the photograph, so that my right hand was free. I did not recap it. In fifteen years I had never put down an open needle at a scene, and I put this one down and took my hand off it. I didn't know what I wanted the hand free for. The black nitrile made a small dry sound against the wood as I let go.

The tape was still in my right outer pocket. I had not touched it all night. Lesson Three kept his eyes open and his mouth open; it was a lesson about being seen and being heard, and the tape had been for the other two, who had needed to be looked at without looking back. I looked at my hands instead. They were black to the wrist, and clean.

I took the page out of the inside pocket. It had been in thirds since I folded it at home, and it came out in thirds, and I opened it along its creases with both hands. The creases held. Good paper keeps a fold the way skin keeps a line. It had the weight good paper has, more than you expect from one sheet, and the glove dragged on it a little as I smoothed it. Nothing on it was marked. I had not circled anything. I am not going to give it to you. You have had two of them. This one stays where it is.

The pin was in my right hand. I had taken it from the same pocket without telling my hand to, the single steel pin, the same as the other two, and I held it between finger and thumb and held the page in my left. Its point pressed through the black nitrile and the pale beneath it and broke neither, and I felt it in the pad of my thumb, the smallest exact pressure in the room, and I looked at his shirt where it would go. Over the top of the sternum, where the skin lies on the bone with almost nothing between them, where I had put the others. Manubrium, I thought. The word arrived, and stood in me, and went no further than that.

There was one other page I had written since the syllabus. It had my own name on it. It was at home, face-down in the drawer under the place where this one had lain. Neither of them had been pinned to anybody. Somewhere there was a list with my name on it, and that one would be read.

My hand put the pin back into the inside pocket, beside the capped marker and the hard case with only the cap in it now. I watched it go in. It was not a decision. I would like to say it was. My hand went in under the lapel and came out without the pin, and there was nothing in it, and I looked at that too.

Whatever happened next was not curriculum.

You know what it is before I do.

He was watching me through the wet on his face. He was heavy in the shoulders and slow in the hands, and his head moved as if it weighed more than it had before, and he was still the best man in any small room he had ever been in. In this one he had found the person who needed looking after, and it was me. He leaned against the tie as far as it would let him, and the plastic creaked against the arm of the chair. He was trying to say something to me, in short pieces that came slower than his pieces ever had, the way people speak at a bedside, and what reached me was not the words but the tone, low and level and patient, the voice of a man who has sat with a great many widows in a great many front rooms and knows that what you say matters less than that you stay. He was good at it. He had always been good at it. The air conditioning was running under the window, and the room was cold, and I was cold in it, and the warmth was coming off him.

I did not know what the count was. I had not looked for it.

I was kneeling. I had not sent myself down there, any more than I had sent the hand to my side; I found it done, the way I had found the count gone. I was on the carpet on my own side of the table, the damp in the shampooed pile coming through to both knees, the table's edge across my ribs, and my face a little below his.

I put the page on the table, beside the Marine. Face-down. My right hand stayed on it, flat, the black glove on the white back of the sheet. He didn't look at it. It had his title on the other side, and his eyes stayed on me.

He reached for my hand, to comfort me. His free hand came across the dressing table, slow, past his brother's face, the same right hand he had offered me when I told him my name, and this time it didn't wait to be taken. It covered mine. His fingertips found the gap at my wrist, between the cuff of the glove and the cuff of my sleeve, and rested there, skin on skin. I knew what that inch would give a swab. I left it where it was. Nothing came into my mouth.

His face was still wet, and he was crying again, without any sound, the tears running from the outer corners and nothing done about them. "Olivia," he said. Only the name, slowly, over our two hands, the way you say the name of the one who is left.

His hand on mine, warm, over the glove and on the one inch of wrist it didn't cover. The syringe on the table beside his brother, with what I had never finished giving him still in it. The first time in this book I had been touched by someone who didn't know who I was, and it was him.


# Chapter 25

His hand was on mine. It was warm and dry. It was the hand of a man comforting someone, and he left it there, and his thumb moved once across my knuckles, the way you would do it at a funeral. What was in the syringe had reached his face. His eyes were slow to follow anything. "Take your time," he said. He said it slowly. He meant it kindly. I took my hand back. I stood up. I had not decided anything. Something came up in me then, and it was rage, and it had no heat in it at all. I waited for it to turn into the other thing. It did not. I noticed that.

Then my hands were on him. He was heavier than he looked. The chair went over and we went with it, and the dressing table rocked when his shoulder hit it, and the photograph went off the edge onto the carpet. I had one knee on the floor. I had my hands on his neck. He did not fight much. What I had given him was in him, and he did not understand what was happening, and he looked up at me as if I had asked him a question and he wanted very much to get it right. There was no sound I wanted to keep. There was no heat. I kept waiting for it, the way you wait for a stair in the dark that isn't there, and it wasn't there, and I went on. My side pulled, hard, where the knife had been. That was the only thing my body told me. Nothing else in me said anything at all.

He was supposed to live. That was the lesson as written: kept alive, and found, and finished by eleven women with paper. He stopped. I took my hands away. It was over before the minute was. I checked for the taste. There wasn't one.

* * *

He was on the floor.

He was on his back between the chair and the dressing table, with one arm under him and the other out across the carpet, palm up. His tie was still knotted. His jacket had come off the chair back with him when the chair went over and was under his shoulders, and the flag pin on its lapel had turned on its post so that it hung sideways. One shoe had come half off. His left cuff had come open, and the cufflink was on the carpet a little way from his hand. I looked at the cufflink for a while. Then I looked at the rest of him again.

His eyes were half open. His mouth was a little open too, as if he had been about to say something else and had been interrupted. That was what he looked like. A man who had been interrupted.

I didn't kneel.

I have knelt beside fourteen hundred bodies, give or take. I have knelt on tile and on gravel and on a kitchen floor in August and on the wet bank of a river. Kneeling is how it starts. You get down to where they are. You look at the face from the side, the way the light falls on it, and then you begin. I stood over him with my hands at my sides and did not get down to where he was. He was the first one I didn't examine.

I stood there and took stock of myself instead, because somebody had to. My knee hurt where it had been on the floor. The pull in my side had stopped as soon as my hands came away, and the place was quiet again, the way it is most days. My breathing was slow. My face was dry. I was cold, and I had been cold for weeks, and that was not news.

Something in me started anyway. "The body is that of," I said, out loud, in the voice I use for the dictaphone, and stopped. I heard it in the room. It sounded like somebody reading from a card. I didn't finish it. The rest of the list went on for a few words in my head without me, the colour of the face, the set of the hands, how long, the way a radio goes on talking in another room after you have stopped listening to it, and then I turned that off as well.

I have written a time of death for every body I have ever stood over. I did not look at the clock. It was right there on the wall.

The room smelled of the carpet cleaner they had used on the whole floor, and under that, of him.

He was the second man who had told me to take my time.

I put my tongue against the back of my teeth. Nothing. My mouth was dry, and clean, and tasted of nothing at all, the way a glass tastes when it has just come out of the dishwasher. I checked my jacket pocket with my hand, the inside one, the way you check for your keys. The marker was there. The pin was there. I don't know what else I thought would be there.

A little while before, on that floor, with his hand on mine, he had said Diane's daughter's name. He had said it crying. He had known it the way he knew all of them. He had never known mine.

He had asked me, earlier, what I wanted. Not what I was going to do. What I wanted. I still didn't have an answer. I had thought for a moment, with my hands on him, that the answer might be down there, the way it had been down there before, in other rooms. It wasn't there either.

The page was on the table. It was lying where I had put it down, beside the place where the photograph had stood, and the place was just a place now, a square of polished wood with nothing on it. I went and picked the page up. I didn't read it. I knew what it said. I had typed it two years ago, and I had read it so many times since that the words had stopped being words and become a shape. I held it the wrong way round, and through the back of the paper I could see the place where the circle would have gone, reversed, like something seen from the other side of a window.

I took the pin out of my pocket. I went back and stood over him with the page in one hand and the pin in the other, and I held the page above his chest, where it was supposed to go.

My hands didn't.

I want to be clear about this. They weren't shaking. They were steady. They have been steady for most of my life, and they were steady then. They held the page over his shirt, flat and level, a hand's width from the cloth, and they held the pin, and they did not move down. I waited for them. I watched them the way I had watched my left hand in the lift the night of the fundraiser, as if they belonged to somebody on my table and I was only there to write down what they did. They didn't do anything. They stayed where they were. After a while I could feel the muscles in my forearms beginning to ache from holding still, and that was all.

The tape was in the kit as well. I didn't take it out. His face was turned a little towards the dressing table, and I left it like that. There was nothing over his eyes and nothing over his mouth. The lesson was that he would be seen. He was going to be seen, now, by everyone. It was not the lesson.

At work we cover the face. We do it for the family, and for ourselves, because a face looking at the ceiling is hard to work beside. I left his uncovered. I didn't decide that either.

I put the pin back in my pocket.

I folded the page once. Just once, down the middle. It was a single sheet of paper, and I have folded harder things. I opened my jacket, the press assistant's jacket with the lanyard and the borrowed name, and put the page in the inside pocket, against my ribs, on the left, and closed the jacket over it. Then I kept my hand flat on the pocket. I kept it there longer than I needed to. The paper didn't move. My hand didn't either.

At home there was a page with my name on it, face-down in the desk. Now there were two I hadn't pinned.

The lesson as written had needed him alive. It had needed a man who could be asked questions, and eleven names in an agent's hands, and a story that came out in the right order, with the paper first and the body nowhere. That was the whole design. I had built it for two years. There would be no page now, and no Evaluation, and no living man to be asked anything. There would be a body on a hotel carpet and a hole in the place where the reason should have been, and somebody would have to file that.

I know who files things.

Somewhere there was a woman with a list of eleven names, and she would go down it in order, and she would get to the end of it. Mine is near the end. I thought about that the way you think about weather in another city.

The folded page was the first thing in two years I had not been able to make myself do, and I didn't want to anyway.

* * *

The sound came in through the main door.

It had been there the whole time, probably. I hadn't been listening for it. Now I was. The corridor outside was filling up. There were footsteps, more than before, and voices, low and quick, the voices people use when they are working and want to sound as if they aren't. A radio close to the door said something short and was answered. Further off, through the walls, I could hear the ballroom. There was music playing, the kind they play to warm a room up, all brass and drums, and over the music a man's voice on a microphone, telling a crowd to get ready, telling them it wouldn't be long now.

Somewhere along from me a room full of people was being told to welcome a man who was not coming. His wife would be at the side of the stage by now, in the shadow of the curtain, where they always put the wife. I didn't think about her for long.

I was counting. I had been counting since I came into the room. Nobody else in the building was counting the way I was, and the time he was allowed had gone a long while ago. It had gone before he took my hand. Out in the corridor somebody said, not loudly, "We're over." Somebody else said, "Give him a second." The minute became two.

I could not hear myself breathing. Once, in another room, I had heard a man breathe as if it were happening inside my own ear. Here there was nobody breathing but me, and I couldn't hear that either. The band in the ballroom came round to the start of the same song and played it again.

The room was cold. It had been cold all evening. The air conditioning ran under the window with a low, steady hum, and I could feel it moving across the backs of my hands and the side of my neck.

I stood still. I was between him and the door. Behind me, on the far side of the room, was the service door, with the sticker across the seam where I had torn it. Someone was going to see that sticker. Not yet.

That's your door, Brandt had said. It's the only one. He had been right about that. He had been right about most things. He had said it with his chest open on a desk, and I had thanked him, and I had meant it. I thought about that for as long as it took the man on the microphone to finish his sentence, and then I put it down.

The knock came. Two knuckles, polite, the knock of someone who respected the man on the other side of the door and did not want to be the one to hurry him.

"Senator Marchetti."

I didn't move. I watched the handle. It was a long brass lever, the hotel kind, and it stayed level.

I have opened a great many doors that other people did not want to open. I had never stood on this side of one.

There was a pause. The music in the ballroom changed to something louder. The man on the microphone said a name, and the crowd answered him with a long rising noise, like a wave coming in over shingle, and went on making it.

The second knock. Firmer.

"Senator. We're at time."

I kept watching the handle. It didn't move. Nobody tried it. I could hear them, just on the other side of the wood, two of them, maybe three, shifting their weight. One of them said something I didn't catch, and another one said, quite clearly, "Standing instruction."

A different voice. "How long's he been?"

"Too long."

"Your call."

"It's not my call. It's his."

Then nothing. My hand had gone to the inside pocket again. I left it there. Just the radio, and the brass through the walls, and the air conditioning, and his cufflink on the carpet by his open hand.

I knew what they were thinking, out there, because I have stood outside enough doors. They were thinking that he was a man who had lost his brother, and that this was the one minute in his day that belonged to him, and that nobody wanted to be the one who walked in on a man like that with his head in his hands. They were thinking that he would come out in a moment and straighten his cuffs and thank them. They were good at their jobs. They were doing them.

The detail didn't open the door. Standing instruction: he was not to be disturbed. The system protected him for one more minute, and I used it.

I didn't hurry. Hurrying is how you leave things behind. I went to the dressing table and found the syringe where I had put it down beside where the photograph had stood, with the rest still in it, and I capped it. I took the case out of my inside pocket and put the syringe in it. I closed the case and fastened it, and put it back in the pocket. I went back over the room the way I go back over a room at work, in strips, the carpet first and then the surfaces. The carpet by the chair. The table, where the page had been. The arm of the chair. The edge of the dressing table where my hip had hit it. I didn't touch him again. Whatever of mine was on him was going to stay there. I knew exactly what it was. I didn't go back for it.

I checked myself the same way. The lanyard was on its clip, the borrowed name facing out. My hair had come loose at the back and I put it up again by feel, the way I do it in the changing room at work. I straightened the jacket. Down one floor, the closet. The other jacket. Then the dock. I went through it once in my head, in order, and it was all still there, and I didn't feel anything about that either.

I touched the inside pocket once on the way past. The page was there.

The Service is very good at standing instructions. It is what they are for.

I went to the service door. I put my hand flat on it, beside the torn sticker. I didn't look to see if my hand was steady. It didn't matter to me whether it was. Through the door I could feel the stairwell, colder than the room, and hear nothing at all.

The knock came again behind me. It was harder this time. It wasn't polite any more. Somebody said his first name. Not the title. His name.

I looked back once, from the service door.

The chair on its side. Him, between the chair and the dressing table, with his palm turned up. The cufflink. The table, with nothing on it. The square of polished wood where something had stood.

The Marine's photograph was face-down on the carpet where it had fallen. I didn't turn it over. I thought about it.


# Chapter 26

I drew the service door shut behind me with the flat of my left hand, and the torn sticker was beside my palm, white on the paint, split along the seam where I had split it and lifting at one corner like a nail that has caught on something. The knock came through two doors and the room between them. It was the third knock. Through two doors it sounded like a man knocking somewhere else, about something else.

I took my hand away. That was the last time I touched the sticker.

The passage was eight paces and a door. I had counted them going in and I counted them coming out, because the count had not stopped when he did and nobody had told it to. I had been counting since the service door.

The Stair C door opened to my shoulder. Nobody was on the landing. Its post had stepped forward into the corridor to widen the cordon when he came, and the whole cordon was facing the other door. The stairwell air came up at me, colder than the passage, smelling of concrete and old paint and the dust that lives on the top of fire extinguishers. Concrete treads. A painted steel rail. At each landing an emergency light in a wire cage, throwing a flat white that made the treads look like something laid out on a steel tray.

I went down at a walk. A run is a sound, and a stairwell is a throat; it passes any sound you give it up and down to whoever is listening. On the flat my side said nothing, the way it had said nothing since my hands came away from him. On the steps it pulled, on the left, low under the ribs, once for every tread, counting them with me. My knee remembered the carpet. My thighs were tired in the long muscles at the front, and the backs of my knees with them, the kind of tiredness no decedent has ever been able to show me.

Thirty-one seconds to the closet door. I had paced it on the Sunday, at a working walk. It was thirty-one again.

I opened the closet with my wrist on the handle and the gloves still on, which is what they were still on for. It smelled of laundered cotton and the bleach-and-citrus that housekeeping chemicals all share, and under that the rubber matting on the floor, warm and faintly sour. Shelves of folded linen to the ceiling. The par sheet taped inside the door. Every stack was folded to one width, labelled on the shelf edge and counted on the sheet.

The jacket was under the bottom ivory cloth, folded over the black tote; my bag and the box of press folders were on the shelf. I noted it the way I note an instrument on a tray. I did not feel anything I would call relief.

Then the change, which went the way a change goes in the locker room at the end of a shift, without anybody watching it, me included. I had run it at home against the cooker clock until it came in under a minute. The gloves came off, both pairs together, one turned inside the other, black into pale, and into the tote. The blazer came off. The page came out of its inside pocket and went into the inside pocket of the catering jacket, which had held nothing since a quarter past six, and lay against the left side of my chest, folded once down the middle with the old thirds still in it. I didn't open it. Then the jacket went on, black, with a slate collar and the caterer's wordmark stitched on the breast, which is what I had bought it for.

I drew the pins out of my hair one at a time and caught it as it came down, so that it came into my palm and not onto the floor, and tied it again low at the nape with the band off my wrist, the way the women who carry trays wear it.

Into the tote: the blazer, folded, and inside it the lanyard and the RUTH CALDER badge, face-in, the borrowed name against the lining. The hard case, with the capped syringe in it. The marker. The pin. The pins from my hair. The roll of vinyl from the blazer's outer pocket, never used. The scalpel from the inside pocket, guard on. The clipboard from the back of my waistband. The fingernail of seal label with VOID on its face. Last, my bag off the shelf. Nothing else. The long tie was still on his wrist, with the chair on it.

I squared the cloth stack the way I had found it. The box of press folders I left squared on its shelf. It was the only thing in the room I had brought, and it had never been mine.

I checked the closet the way I had checked the green room. The shelf. The floor, for hair. The handle. Then I closed the door with the side of my wrist, the sleeve pulled down over it. You open doors with your elbow when your gloves are dirty. There was nothing on my hands now, and they did it anyway. They have been doing it for fifteen years.

The second flight. Halfway down it, the sound above me changed. The knocking stopped. There was a gap of four seconds, and then a voice said his name in a register I knew from corridors, from relatives in the anteroom, the register for a door you have already decided to open. Then several voices at once, and a radio squalled and was answered, and the shaft carried all of it down to me as if it were being poured. That was the door. I was a floor and a half under it. I did not speed up. Somewhere in the building, on page four of the run of show, was the Bureau's liaison, and in a minute somebody would tell her which room.

At the bottom the fire door had a push bar. I put my hip into it and my left forearm, the sleeve over the wrist, and stepped through, and the closer took the door off me and drew it home with a long hydraulic hiss and a latch. I started a new count from the latch.

Back of house, street level. A concrete corridor painted slate to the waist and bone above it. Roll cages of linen. A stack of hotel pans. A time clock on the wall. On my left, a dozen paces up from the fire door, the glass booth of the dock office, and in it a man in a hotel security fleece, sitting with his back to the corridor, and on the desk in front of him the radio in its cradle. On Sunday the receiving supervisor had sat in that chair the same way, with his sandwich and the corridor behind him. I had counted on the chair. A chair can be turned. At the other end of the corridor, forty metres off, the loading bay: the dock shutter half down onto the night and November coming in under it along the floor; the Service's arch dark beside it with its table cleared, its agent gone up to the cordon for the speech as the annex said he would be; and beside the shutter the steel vendor gate. The shutter and the gate could not be seen from the booth. I had checked that on the plan.

I had built the route so that the stair would be behind me before anybody upstairs had decided anything. I hadn't known by how much. I was level with his glass when the radio spoke.

"Security, all stations. Lockdown, lockdown. Stairs and lifts are locked. Hold all staff where they are."

Behind me the maglock on the stair door went home. It is a dull, total sound, like a fridge door closing on a full fridge. From the stair side the bar would still have let me out, because it has to, but only after an alarm and fifteen seconds of standing at it, at the foot of a stair the detail was about to come down. Eleven.

I was on the wrong side of it by eleven seconds. I kept walking.

* * *

Past the booth the corridor ran straight. Painted block. Fluorescent tubes in cages, one of them going, ticking at the end of each flicker like a fingernail on a tooth. A mop bucket against the wall with the mop standing in it. I walked at the pace of staff going off shift who would very much like not to be kept back for anything. The tote was on my left shoulder. My left hand was on its strap.

A porter was coming up the corridor towards me, and behind him two servers with trays, in the same jacket I was wearing. Ahead of them, from the mouth of the service-lift lobby, radio voices carried out into the corridor, several sets saying the same thing half a beat apart, and the three of them flattened against the wall with the trays up at their chests, the way people do in a corridor when they hear a tone of voice they don't have the rank to ask about. I passed them. Neither side looked. That is what the wordmark is for.

Behind them, out of the lift lobby, a woman came the other way.

She was walking fast, a stride short of a run, which is a skill: the corridor had people in it and she didn't yet know what she was running to, and a running woman in a corridor full of trays is a woman who arrives late. Mid-forties. Flat shoes. A dark trouser suit, cut a little long on the right. A visitor lanyard, turned round backwards.

I read the walk the way I read anything that comes through the doors at work, before the paperwork. The right arm swung less than the left. The right elbow stayed close to the body, and the hips were faintly bladed, the right side carried as if it had something on it that it had carried for twenty years and would carry tonight whether or not it was there. I have opened three police officers. All three had the same thickening of the skin over the right iliac crest, where a holster had sat for twenty years. I wasn't going to get to check. I'd have bet on it.

No earpiece. No pin on the lapel. Not hotel, not campaign, not Service; the Service would have been on the channel, and she was not listening to anything but the corridor. I did the arithmetic before she was level with me. There was one person in this building on the security annex and on page four of the run of show who would not be on the Service's channel, and I had read both more than once.

Then we were level, for half a second.

She looked at my hands. I looked at hers.

I can tell you what she saw of mine only by where her eyes went, and they went down and to my left and stayed a moment longer than a woman looks at a stranger's hands in a corridor, and I don't know which of my hands it was that held them. I know about hers. Short nails, unpainted, filed rather than bitten, with no varnish ever lifted off them; a callus in the web between the right thumb and forefinger, yellowed and smooth, where a grip has closed around the same shape at a range, year after year, for twenty years, to keep a qualification; on the inside of the right middle finger, at the last joint, a small blue mark where a biro sits when you write a great deal by hand and press harder than you need to; on the left, no ring, and at the base of the ring finger a faint pale line where one had been, a while ago, long enough for the skin to start forgetting it. They were the hands of someone who writes things down. I felt it the way I feel a finding, low and certain and before I had the words for it.

Her face I took in without marking it. It was a face that had been awake since early and expected to be awake much later, and had made its arrangements. As she went by she gave off cold air on wool, a suit that had been outside more than once today, and coffee. Urn coffee. I had watched a whole campaign drink it for two months.

My face was in no file she had yet. The Medical Examiner's office does not release photographs of its pathologists. The badge in my tote had no photograph on it, and nothing behind it in any database. Since Saturday, if counsel had done what the judge told him, she had had my surname on a page. Tonight she had my face, and nothing to attach it to.

Something in me reached for a mark for her, out of habit, the way a hand reaches for a rail on a stair. It didn't find one. There was no page for her.

My mouth was dry. There was a pulse in the side of my neck that I could feel without touching it, the first thing my body had volunteered in weeks that was not a complaint. I didn't name it. My legs kept the pace. I didn't slow and I didn't step further aside than staff would.

For one stride our flat soles came down together on the concrete. Then they were out of step again, and she was behind me.

She didn't stop.

I didn't look back. A few paces on I heard her soles change, the walk breaking into a run, now that there was nobody left between her and the stairs, and I didn't turn round for that either.

I knew exactly who she was. I am telling you now, before she does: Special Agent E. Reyes. I had never known what the E stood for. I still don't. An initial is not a person, I had thought once, reading her signature in somebody else's email. Now it had a callus.

* * *

The vendor gate was a steel pedestrian gate beside the dock shutter, with a card reader on the street side and, on mine, a green button for getting out. I had found it on the floor plan. It ran on a nineteen-second timer. I pulled the cuff of the jacket down over my knuckles and pressed the button with the knuckle of my left hand, and the lock dropped with a click like a tongue against teeth. The press went into the gate's log with a time against it, and there would be a camera over the bay; there always is. What they would have was a server with her hair low and her eyes down, in a wordmark half the building was wearing tonight. I had priced that on Sunday, the first time I pressed it. I pushed the gate with my shoulder and walked through it. I didn't wait to hear it close.

Fire code requires a building to let you out. I have done the autopsies on the buildings that didn't.

The cold was the first thing. It came through the jacket as if there were no jacket, five degrees and dry, no wind. I hadn't brought a coat. I didn't do anything about it.

The cross street behind the hotel was bins and a catering van with its back doors open, nobody in it, and steam coming out of a grate in the road in a slow white column, lit sodium from underneath. Then the avenue. Traffic. A bus with its interior lights on, three people in it. Two men outside a bar arguing about something with great pleasure, their hands going. A woman walking a dog. Nothing in any of it had changed. A woman in a catering jacket walking home at nine o'clock on a Monday is the least interesting thing in any city, and I knew it.

I didn't take a cab. A cab is a record. A ride from an app is a record with a map attached. I know what investigators look for because I have spent fifteen years telling them, in writing, with diagrams. So I walked. It is about nine kilometres, and it took me just under two hours, by streets I chose for being ordinary.

Through the window of the second bar there was a television over the optics, and on it the ballroom. A lectern with nobody at it. Flags. A crowd from behind, the backs of heads. A banner along the bottom: REMARKS DELAYED. Nobody in the bar was watching it. A man at the end of the counter was eating crisps out of the bag and reading his phone, and the barman was drying a glass with the absorption of a surgeon. I didn't stop either. Somewhere behind me a room full of people were being asked to be patient with a man. They were very good at it. They'd had practice.

There were sirens, once, a long way off, going the other way. I heard them the way you hear a tap running in another flat.

Her hands came with me. At the kerbs I had them again: the callus, the pale line, the blue mark where the pen would sit when she wrote me down.

The cold settled into me and I let it. It had been there for weeks; it simply had more room now. It came up through the soles of my shoes from the paving and in at the cuffs, and the back of my left hand on the tote strap went the colour of a cold kidney, slate under the pink. I watched it do it and did not put it in a pocket. My side went from pulling to a dull regular ache, low on the left, keeping a time I wasn't keeping. My shoes were the flat black ones I had worn under the press assistant's navy, which pass for anything, and somewhere in the second hour a blister began at my left heel, where the service stair had given me one in August.

A kebab shop's vent breathed onto the pavement as I passed it: lamb fat on the spit, cumin, the char on the outside of the cone where the knife had been and would be again. Then a bakery, shut for the night, the shutters down, and through its grille the smell of yeast, of tomorrow's dough proving in the dark. Further on, exhaust, and a gutter packed with wet leaves going to mulch, the smell of a ditch in a park. I identified all of it correctly. It went by. Nothing followed it. My mouth stayed as it had been since the green room, clean and dry, a glass out of the dishwasher.

I was walking towards a locked drawer with my own page in it, face-down, and I was carrying the third page, folded once and still creased in thirds. That was where the walk went.

About halfway, at a crossing on a wide road, I stopped for the light. I pressed the button with my left knuckle through the cuff, and the steel of it was cold through the cloth. There was nothing coming either way. I waited anyway. Then I looked down.

My right hand was flat across my chest, bare, over the inside pocket. It was pressing the page through the jacket. Under the palm I could feel the fold down the middle, and under that, fainter, the two older creases running across it. It had been there since the closet. In the green room it had lain on the other jacket, over the same page, and I had known it then. Somewhere after that I had stopped knowing, and it had stayed. I had been holding it the whole way, the way I had held my own left wrist in the lift at the fundraiser, two fingers across it, counting. Except that this was the whole palm, and it wasn't counting anything.

The light changed. I left the hand where it was and crossed.

I was not hungry. I noted that I would once have called that a finding.

Behind me, an hour back, the vendor gate had closed on its timer, unhurried. I hadn't counted it.


# Interlude — Reyes

The notebook was in the inside pocket of her jacket, and she could feel its spiral through the lining as the service lift went down. Eleven names in block capitals, alphabetical. Dates against the first eight. The Sunday ones ticked, and this morning's. VALE against Wednesday, ten o'clock. Two question marks.

The first was a woman who had left the country. Counsel's covering note gave only a forwarding address abroad; the rest of her contact details he held and called privileged. The second was the eleventh name, the one after Vale: a disconnected phone number and an address she had left within a year of signing. Every database she could reach had ended where the paper did. Neither of her two voicemails to counsel about them today had been returned.

She was here as campaign-protection liaison, on page four of the run of show. Her visitor lanyard had turned itself round again. She was in flat shoes, and she had not eaten since a sandwich at four.

A staffer upstairs had told her counsel was at the bottom of the service lift, taking a call where there was signal, so she had come down to ask in person. The doors opened on the lift lobby, and past it on the dock-level corridor. Painted cinderblock under a strip light. A laundry cart and a catering trolley against the wall. It smelled of fryer oil and bleach, and cigarette smoke drifting in from the bay. A cold draught came down from the dock, and warm wet air came out of the kitchen to meet it. The ballroom was two floors up. She felt its drums through the soles of her shoes more than she heard them.

Counsel was round the corner of the lobby, at the far end, with a coat over his shoulders and his phone to his ear. He saw her and lowered the phone.

"After the election, Agent Reyes."

He said it pleasantly. It was the answer he had given her to everything since the records request, except what a judge had made him give. She had it in writing three times, in longer words.

She opened her mouth.

The radios went. Every radio in earshot at once, and none of them hers. A banquet captain's, through the kitchen door. And far up the corridor, the set in the glass booth of the dock office, half a beat behind, so the same words came from two places. "Lockdown, lockdown. Stairs and lifts are locked. Hold all staff where they are." Then a second voice, faster, giving a room. The Alder Suite. Third floor.

Counsel still had the phone in his hand. He was still smiling. The smile had not caught up with the news.

Her right hand was on the holster before she had decided anything. Twenty years of that. She turned from him and went up the corridor towards the dock office and whoever in it could open the stair, fast, a stride short of a run, because the corridor had people in it. She did not look back at him.

People flattened against the walls. A porter. Two servers holding their trays at chest height. Up in the booth the security man had his back to the corridor and was shouting the room into the radio. Everyone in the corridor was stopping, or turning towards the noise. Reyes had stood in enough corridors to know there was a third thing, and that it was rare.

One person was walking the other way, towards the vendor gate. A catering jacket. A black bag on her left shoulder. She was not hurrying and she was not stopping.

They passed close.

Reyes took her in the order she always did. The jacket first: the caterer's black, the same as the servers'. Then the hands. The woman was already looking at Reyes's hands, before her face, the way police do, and for the length of a stride Reyes read hers back, the left one lying on the strap of the bag, open and still, while the trays shifted against the wall behind it, and the right one flat on the jacket over the left side of her chest, holding something there through the cloth. Flat. Fingers together. Not a grip. They were the only steady hands in the corridor.

Then the face. A woman in her thirties. Thin. No make-up. Pale under the strip light, shadowed under the eyes, the hair pulled back. No expression. It was not a blank face. It was the face of someone at work.

She was already putting it in the notebook in her head. Wrong hands. Wrong direction. Then the booth radio said the room again, and she was the Bureau's person in this building on somebody else's schedule. The woman was behind her. Reyes broke into a run. Her thumb lay on the holster strap and did not open it. The notebook knocked against her ribs. Her breath went short, and her shoes were loud on the concrete.

The hand came up the corridor with her. Flat on the chest, on Election Eve, in a building full of flags. Like a woman saying the pledge.

She would spend the rest of her career remembering that face. That night she didn't stop.


# Chapter 27

The leaf mould came in on his trouser cuffs. Everything else in the suite was the old smell, cold iron and chlorhexidine and the faint sweetness a fresh case gives off in its first hours, before the room has made it its own, and I had stopped noticing any of it years ago. The leaf mould was different. It was wet and loamy and it had come from outside, from under a hedge, from a lawn somebody had been keeping, and it was the only thing in the room that had been anywhere.

It was twenty to eleven on a Monday, a week after, and I had a case open. He was seventy-four. He had gone down in his own garden with a rake in his hands, and his palms agreed with the history: calloused across the heads of the metacarpals, the skin of the right thumb web polished by a wooden handle. I had the heart out and on the board, and I was cross-cutting the coronaries every few millimetres with a fresh blade, and in the left anterior descending artery, not far from its origin, I found it: a fresh thrombus, dark as the inside of a plum, seated on a plaque that had split. The widow-maker. I said it into the dictaphone by its proper name and nothing else. I weighed the heart and wrote the number down. I worked well. I would like that on the record.

Mara was at the next table. When she came round to my side for the saw she stopped farther from me than she used to, and I heard the place she stopped: the soft squeak of her clogs on the wet floor, a full stride short.

When I went out to the anteroom for fresh gloves, the break-room television was on through the office door, the same set, on mute, the captions crawling under a picture of a podium. INVESTIGATORS BELIEVE SENATOR'S ATTACKER ACTED ALONE. Then: SOURCES DESCRIBE A LONE EXTREMIST. NO EVIDENCE OF WIDER PLOT. NO DESCRIPTION HAS BEEN RELEASED. At *acted alone* I put my wrist to my left side, over the scar, through the gown and the apron, which I should not have done, and took it away again.

There had been no page on him, because I had not pinned one. Two of the lessons had been found with paper pinned to their chests, and the third had not, and a story that is handed a body with nothing pinned to it will file it wherever it likes. It had liked a lone extremist. It had one.

The result had come in on the Tuesday within a point of the last poll, with a dead man's name still on the ballot. On Friday the party named a replacement. I read his name on the captions twice to keep it, and I haven't kept it. The machine was intact. Only the man was gone.

The same day a single lawyer put out a statement for three women, unnamed, former campaign staff. Three. I counted them the way I count towels at the end of a case. I was not one of them.

I closed the gardener. Surgical tape on the specimen labels, my initials, the date. Mara passed me the needle holder across the table, handle first, at the full length of her arm. She had not hummed since October. When my back was turned she looked at my hands. I knew by the clogs. They stopped, and the hose in her hand went on running over the same few inches of steel.

I let her. I didn't ask about her weekend. I didn't make the joke that would have made it easier. She was watching exactly the right thing, in exactly the right proportion, and she was afraid of precisely what there was to be afraid of. Full marks.

My coffee was on the office desk where I had put it at eight. It had a skin on it. I didn't drink it, and I wasn't going to go to lunch, and my belt was on the hole I had made with the trussing needle and was loose on that.

The Chief had done the Senator himself, on the Tuesday, with two federal observers in the room and Mara assisting. He wanted, I assume, a face that had never been near politics; Mara's had now been over all three of its bodies. I was not asked, and I did not ask for the report. My hands had written most of it.

At the sinks we scrubbed side by side, a basin apart. She told me the way I had taught her to report a finding: flat, in order, nothing in front of the fact.

"The FBI rang at nine," she said. "Special Agent Reyes. The Chief's on the Senator's file, and I've got the rota this month, so it came to me."

"What did she want?"

"Your shift pattern this week. I said days." She turned off her tap with her elbow. "She said you were meant to see her last Wednesday. Before."

The water ran over my wrists at the temperature I had set it, which was no colder than my flat.

"Did you know?" Mara said.

"No."

It was true. It was one of very few true things I said that day.

I had a face for the voice she was reporting. Flat shoes. A trouser suit cut a little long on the right. Short unpainted nails, and a biro mark on the middle finger of the right hand, and a corridor half a second long. E. Reyes. I didn't tell Mara that.

I didn't ask what else the agent had said. Mara told me anyway, drying her hands on a paper towel one finger at a time, because it was in the order and she hadn't reached the end of it.

"She was explaining the lost week," she said. "She's going down a list. In order, she said. Eight of them done by the end of that week. Then on Friday one came in through a lawyer, and she was the last name on it. I don't know what the list is for. She didn't say. So that's nine of the eleven. There are two left. A woman who's left the country." She put the towel in the bin. "And Dr. Vale. Postponed by Election Eve. Rescheduled for Thursday."

* * *

I had bought a chicken on the walk home. I remember the paper it came in, and the weight of it in the crook of my arm, about a kilo and a half, and the man at the counter saying something about the cold, and that I answered him. It was on the kitchen counter in its paper.

The heating was still off. I was standing at the writing desk with my coat on when the phone lit up on the wood and said HANNAH. Under her name the notification was still there where it had been for two weeks: voicemail, 1:14.

I answered.

Her kitchen came down the line before she did, warm and ordinary and a little compressed, the way everything is through a phone: the dishwasher in its long churn, a tap running and stopping, and somewhere farther off June singing something from school, the same four words over and over with total conviction. My flat had nothing to set against it. The fridge. My own breath. The phone was warm against my ear from the battery, and it was the only warm thing in the room; the hand I had put down flat on the desk had gone cold through the palm, and I left it there.

"There you are," Hannah said. Not unkindly.

"I've been busy."
